December 5, 2025 iii

 ABSTRACT
 This report describes the FY25 status of international collaboration on geologic disposal
 research and development (R&D) in the Office of Spent Fuel and High-Level Waste Disposition
 (SFHLWD) in the U.S. Department of Energy’s (DOE) Office of Nuclear Energy (NE). The
 mission of this office is to provide confidence in the safe long-term management of the nation’s
 spent nuclear fuel and high-level radioactive waste by reducing uncertainty and advancing
 technology for extended storage, transportation, and geologic disposal. R&D in geologic
 disposal is administered in SFHLWD’s Disposal R&D Campaign.
 High-Level Objective of International Collaboration in Geologic Disposal R&D
 Since 2012, Lawrence Berkeley National Laboratory (LBNL) has coordinated the Disposal R&D
 Campaign’s efforts to advance active collaboration with several international geologic disposal
 programs across the world. The joint research activities with international programs, initiatives,
 and projects are beneficial to DOE’s disposal research program because they: (1) provide first-
 hand access to the decades of experience of our international partners gained in investigations
 of various disposal options in geologic environments; (2) give DOE scientists a large library of
 experimental data from many past and ongoing in-situ tests conducted in several international
 underground research laboratories (URLs) in different host rocks; (3) establish a framework for
 active peer-to-peer research participation in international groups that conduct, analyze, and
 model performance-relevant processes; and (4) open the door to jointly conducting in-situ
 experiments in international URLs. Last not least, international collaboration allows the Disposal
 R&D Campaign to benefit from substantial international investments and achieve cost savings
 via joint funding of expensive field experiments.
 Status of International Collaboration
 Over the past nearly 15 years, the Disposal R&D Campaign has established formal
 collaboration agreements with multiple international initiatives and various international partners.
 National lab scientists associated with the campaign have conducted numerous collaborative
 R&D activities that align well with its R&D priorities. Formal partnerships in collaborative
 initiatives, such as the Mont Terri Project (since 2012), the DECOVALEX Initiative (since 2012),
 the Colloid Formation and Migration Project (CFM) (2012 – 2015), the FEBEX Dismantling
 Project (FEBEX-DP) (2015 – 2018), the SKB Task Forces (2014 – 2025), and the HotBENT
 Project (since 2018), have allowed access to collaborative research with a focus on field
 experiments in international URLs, including their analysis, predictive and interpretative
 modeling, and assessment of safety impacts. Other multinational initiatives, such as several
 Nuclear Energy Agency (NEA)-coordinated activities, have provided valuable opportunities for
 information exchange and data assessment. Additional opportunities for multinational
 collaboration exist: For example, the Disposal R&D Campaign recently instituted a technical
 partnership with the EURAD-2 European Commission collaborative research program, which
 initiated a new round of repository R&D work for a 5-year period from 2024 through 2029. The
 campaign has also explored direct bilateral opportunities for active research collaboration, with
 institutions from Germany, Republic of Korea, Sweden, Israel, France, Japan, Belgium, Finland,
 Czech Republic, and China. Some of these opportunities (e.g., with Germany, Republic of
 Korea, Sweden, Israel, and France) have resulted in close bilateral research activities between
 DOE scientists and their international counterparts; the others provide opportunities for active
 research collaboration in the future. Today, international collaboration activities form a
 considerable portion of the Disposal R&D Campaign’s research program, specifically in key
 disposal research areas such as the Engineered Barrier System (EBS), Crystalline, Argillite,
 Salt and Geologic Disposal Safety Assessment (GDSA) Work Packages.

December 5, 2025 iv

 FY25 R&D Accomplishments
 In a balanced portfolio, international collaboration activities have addressed important research
 areas and enabled significant advances. Selected achievements are summarized below:
 • Temperature-Induced Impacts on Engineered and Natural Barriers: Collaborations with the
 international HotBENT and FE Heater Test projects involve large-scale experimental and
 modeling studies to understand temperature-dependent perturbations and alterations in
 engineered and natural barriers. Complemented by an international benchmarking exercise,
 these activities have provided valuable insights into how these perturbations and alterations
 may affect repository performance.
 • Experimental and Modeling Studies of Engineered Barrier and Host Rock Interactions:
 International collaboration studies made significant progress in evaluating interfacial
 reactions between engineered-barrier-system components and host rock materials.
 Materials alterations near these interfaces can affect repository performance in multiple
 ways, for example, by changing the flow and transport properties for radionuclides to
 migrate from the engineered into the natural barrier systems.
 • Repository-Induced Perturbations in Argillite Rock: Analysis of in-situ experiments at the
 Mont Terri URL allowed for considerable scientific advances in understanding how
 repository-induced perturbations may affect the long-term performance of low-permeability
 argillite host rocks. One potential concern addressed is that gas generation in a repository
 could lead to pressure increases in the low-permeability barriers that may induce rock
 deformation and possibly damage. The second issue addressed is the potential for thermally
 induced fault reactivation within the host rock, which may in turn cause the formation of
 permeable transport pathways.
 • Hydromechanics, Fluid Flow, and Transport in Fractured Crystalline Rock: Because
 crystalline formations are often fractured, repository performance assessment studies
 require an understanding of (1) the characteristics of fracture networks and how they can be
 represented with fracture network models, (2) the impact of stress changes and other
 perturbations on fracture flow and transport properties, and (3) the ability to simulate large-
 scale transport patterns of radionuclides migrating in fracture networks. Four international
 collaboration activities associated with the DECOVALEX Initiative and the SKB Task Forces
 have made significant progress in addressing these challenges.
 • Sealing Systems for Repository Tunnels and Shafts: Effective sealing of tunnels and shafts
 is important to block potential pathways of radionuclides between a nuclear waste repository
 and the biosphere. Under the umbrella of DECOVALEX-2027, DOE scientists contributed to
 the successful modeling of the Sandwich Experiment at Mont Terri, which tests a newly
 developed shaft sealing concept comprising alternating layers of bentonite and equipotential
 layers.
 • Performance Assessment and Radionuclide Transport Modeling: Excellent progress was
 made in developing and building confidence in the models, methods, and software used for
 post-closure performance/safety assessments and radionuclide transport predictions.
 Important international activities were the (1) collaborative modeling of crystalline and salt
 reference cases under the umbrella of the DECOVALEX-2027 initiative, (2) the development
 of thermochemical databases in collaboration with international partners, (3) the joint
 modeling of iodine transport at the Cigar Lake natural analog.
 • Next Generation Workforce Development: Investing in the next generation of nuclear waste
 scientists and leaders is vital to fulfilling DOE NE’s mission of developing solutions for the
 long-term geologic disposal of radioactive waste. A few years ago, the Disposal R&D

December 5, 2025                                                                                            v

 Campaign established a Next Generation Workforce Development Pilot Program to attract,
 train and advance a talented and diverse future workforce in disposal research. International
 collaboration has been important in this context. To help recruit and train a broad talent pool,
 the workforce development program provided interesting research challenges and attractive
 work-abroad opportunities in conjunction with multiple international collaboration partners.
 Benefits of International Collaboration
 International collaboration has enabled deep engagement of DOE researchers with the
 international waste management R&D community, emphasizing topics like new scientific
 advances, state-of-the-art simulation tools, site characterization and monitoring approaches,
 and best practices. DOE researchers have significantly improved their understanding of the
 current technical basis through the joint R&D with international scientists, the worldwide sharing
 of knowledge and experience, and the access to field and laboratory experiments from a variety
 of URLs and host rocks. Comparison with experimental data has contributed to the testing and
 validation of predictive computational models for evaluating disposal system performance in
 various generic disposal system concepts. Comparison of model results with other international
 modeling groups, using their respective simulation tools and conceptual understanding, have
 enhanced confidence in the robustness of predictive models used for performance assessment.
 The possibility of linking model differences to conceptual model setup provides valuable
 guidance into “best” modeling choices and understanding the effect of conceptual model
 selection on prediction uncertainty. Advances in making long-term predictions of complex
 coupled thermal-hydrological-mechanical-chemical (THMC) and flow and transport processes
 contribute directly to improving post-closure performance assessment (PA) and safety
 assessment (SA) models. Access to international field testing also gives an opportunity to
 develop, test, and demonstrate new methods and workflows for site characterization and
 monitoring of deep geological disposal. International collaboration finally provides opportunities
 for training/educating junior staff well suited to move the U.S. disposal research program
 forward into the next decades.
 Recent Developments and Future Direction
 In fiscal year 2025 (FY25), the Disposal R&D Campaign in DOE’s Office of Nuclear Energy
 underwent a significant realignment of R&D priorities and scope. Consequently, the international
 disposal R&D portfolio changed substantially, placing increased emphasis on argillite and
 crystalline as potential host rocks and highlighting R&D collaborations and activities that support
 two main objectives: (1) Understanding the effects of high-temperature disposal on repository
 readiness and safety assessment (i.e., up to 200°C at the canister surface), and (2) Testing and
 validating the accuracy of performance and safety assessment predictions for evaluation and
 comparison of disposal solutions. This report covers the portfolio transition in FY25 as follows:
 all collaborations and activities undertaken during the fiscal year are documented, even if some
 of these concluded before or at the end of the year.
 The changes in FY25 have led to a more concentrated, targeted, and cohesive international
 disposal R&D portfolio going forward into FY26. Yet, international collaboration remains a
 central element in DOE’s Disposal R&D Campaign. Since its inception in 2012, the international
 portfolio has continuously evolved in response to changing priorities and emerging collaboration
 opportunities, and it will continue to do so in the future. The campaign routinely evaluates the
 technical merit of all international activities and their alignment with DOE priorities; reviews,
 assesses, and develops new opportunities in close integration with international partners; and
 revises the portfolio of international R&D activities as appropriate.

December 5, 2025 vi

 EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
 Background and Main Objectives
 This report describes the FY25 status of international collaboration in geologic disposal
 research. Since 2012, in an effort coordinated by LBNL, the Disposal R&D Campaign has
 advanced active collaboration with several international geologic disposal programs worldwide.
 Such collaboration has enabled the campaign to benefit from a deep knowledge base regarding
 alternative repository designs and host rock environments. It has provided a framework for
 active peer-to-peer research participation with international groups that conduct, analyze, and
 model performance-relevant processes. Through international collaboration, the Disposal R&D
 Campaign also benefits from substantial international investments in research facilities (such as
 URL testing and modeling) and achieves cost savings via joint funding of R&D studies (including
 those involving expensive field experiments). To date, the Disposal R&D Campaign has
 established formal collaboration agreements with multiple international initiatives and various
 international partners, and national lab scientists associated with the campaign have conducted
 numerous collaborative R&D activities. Guiding principles for the selection of collaboration
 options and activities have been as follows:
 • Focus on international activities that align with the strategic direction of the Disposal R&D
 Campaign and ideally complement other disposal R&D activities in the campaign (e.g., use
 international experiments to test new modeling tools developed in the campaign).
 • Select collaborative R&D activities based on technical merit, relevance to safety case, and
 cost/benefit, and strive for a balanced portfolio in terms of relevant repository designs and
 host rock environments.
 • Emphasize collaborations that provide access to and/or allows for participation in field
 experiments conducted in operating underground research laboratories.
 • Focus on opportunities for active R&D participation of DOE researchers in international R&D
 projects.
 Key Issues Tackled in Current and Planned Portfolios
 Over the past nearly 15 years, the work conducted within the international disposal R&D
 portfolio has centered on the following high-level key research questions:
 • Engineered Barrier Integrity: What is backfills and seals’ long-term stability and retention
 capability? Can bentonite be eroded when in contact with water from flowing rock fractures?
 How relevant are interactions between engineered and natural barrier materials, such as
 metal-bentonite-cement interactions? Is an increase in gas pressure gas migration a
 concern for barrier integrity?
 • Near-Field Perturbation: How important are thermal, mechanical, and other perturbations to
 a host rock (such as argillite/clay/shale, crystalline rock, and salt), and how effective is
 healing or sealing of the damage zone in the long term? How reliable are predictive models
 for the strongly coupled THMC behavior of clay and salt formations?
 • Flow and Radionuclide Transport: How does high temperature impact the diffusion and
 sorption properties of clay materials, considering the heat load from direct disposal of large
 dual-purpose canisters (DPCs)? What is the potential for increased transport via colloids?
 Can transport in diffusion-dominated systems like clay, bentonite, and salt as well as in
 advection-dominated formations such as fractured granite be reliably predicted?

December 5, 2025 vii

 • Integrated System Behavior, Performance Assessment, and Site Characterization: Can the
 early-time behavior of an entire repository system—including all engineered and natural
 barriers and their interaction— be measured and demonstrated? Can this integrated
 behavior be reliably predicted? Are the planned construction and emplacement methods
 feasible? Which characterization and monitoring techniques are suitable for site selection
 and performance confirmation? How reliable are performance and safety assessment
 models?
 A FY25 realignment of DOE’s disposal R&D program resulted in a narrower focus of planned
 international activities in FY26. In addition to a stronger emphasis on argillite and crystalline
 host rocks, future international collaboration will pursue two main goals: (1) Understand the
 effects of high-temperature disposal on repository readiness and safety assessment, and (2)
 Test and demonstrate the validity of performance and safety assessment predictions for
 evaluating and comparing disposal options.
 Collaboration Opportunities Provided by International Initiatives (Section 3 of this report)
 Since 2012, the Disposal R&D Campaign has participated in several multinational collaboration
 initiatives as a formal partner and has built a balanced portfolio of selected R&D projects
 collaborating with international peers. Below, we offer brief summaries of all collaboration
 agreements (Mont Terri Project, DECOVALEX Initiative, HotBENT Project, and SKB Task
 Forces) that were active in FY25. For past partnerships and collaborations (e.g., CFM Project
 and FEBEX-DP Project), we refer to previous reports (e.g., Birkholzer et al., 2024b).
 Mont Terri Project (since 2012)
 The Mont Terri Project is an international research partnership (currently 22 partners) for the
 characterization and performance assessment of a clay/shale formation. The partnership
 provides open access to an existing URL in Switzerland, the Mont Terri URL. Partner
 organizations can conduct experiments in the URL, participate in experiments conducted by
 others, and have access to all project results from past and ongoing efforts. In the current
 phase, the Mont Terri Project comprises about 40 separate experiments relevant to all
 phases in the lifetime of a repository. The annual budget for the in-situ work amounts to
 several million U.S. dollars, complemented by the partners' interpretation, analyses, and
 modeling work. The U.S. DOE joined the Mont Terri Project in July 2012. Since then, DOE
 researchers have been involved in projects ranging from large-scale heater tests to damage
 zone characterization, diffusion tests, experiments examining the interaction between
 engineered and natural barrier materials, and fault slip experiments. A significant extension
 of the Mont Terri URL has been undertaken in 2019-2020, providing significantly more
 space for new experiments. This extension ensures the long-term future of the Mont Terri
 Project.
 DECOVALEX Initiative (since 2012)
 The DECOVALEX Initiative (DEvelopment of COupled Models and their VALidation Against
 EXperiments) is an international research collaboration and model comparison activity for
 coupled process simulations in geologic repository systems (currently 18 partners). The
 project has been active and running for more than 30 years and centers on modeling test
 cases often based on experimental data sets from international URLs. Typically, these
 experimental test cases are proposed by one of the project’s partners and collectively
 studied and modeled by all DECOVALEX participants. Over the years, DOE scientists have
 been deeply engaged in several phases of the DECOVALEX Initiative, often participating in
 multiple tasks and occasionally serving as task leads. The currently active DECOVALEX
 Initiative phase, referred to as DECOVALEX-2027, commenced in the Spring of 2024 and

December 5, 2025 viii

 will continue through the end of 2027, with strong participation from DOE researchers in
 collaborative modeling activities related to natural analog studies, radionuclide transport
 simulations, and fractured rock characterizations.
 Grimsel Test Site Projects (since 2012)
 The Grimsel Test Site (GTS) is a URL situated in sparsely fractured crystalline host rock in
 the Swiss Alps. GTS was established in 1984 as a center for underground R&D supporting a
 wide range of collaborative disposal research experiments related to crystalline rock and
 EBS. In the past, the Disposal R&D Campaign was a partner in two such experiments
 (Birkholzer et al., 2024a), the Colloid Formation and Migration (CFM) Project (2012 – 2015)
 and the FEBEX Dismantling Project (FEBEX-DP) (2015 – 2018). Currently, the campaign is
 involved in the HotBENT Project, a full-scale high-temperature heater experiment conducted
 by several international organizations to investigate the performance of bentonite buffers
 and near-field rock at maximum temperatures ranging from 175°C to 200°C. DOE scientists
 have been active from the beginning of the HotBENT project and have made significant
 contributions: (1) Scoping calculations were conducted with simplified models to facilitate
 the design of the full-scale heater test; (2) High-temperature laboratory column tests and
 hydrothermal batch experiments have been carried out to provide information that cannot be
 measured in the field test; (3) Interpretive and predictive models have been developed and
 applied as part of the HotBENT Modeling Platform to deepen the understanding and
 improve modeling capabilities. This emphasis on HotBENT activities aligns with DOE’s
 emphasis on high-temperature disposal.
 SKB (Swedish Nuclear Fuel and Waste Management) Task Forces (2014 - 2025)
 The SKB Task Forces serve as a platform for international collaboration related to
 conceptual and numerical modeling of performance-relevant processes in natural and
 engineered systems (currently 12 partners). One task force focuses on flow and
 radionuclide migration processes in naturally fractured crystalline rock, referred to as
 Groundwater Flow and Transport of Solutes (GWFTS) Task Force), The other task force
 tackles the coupled behavior of the engineered barrier system, referred to as Engineered
 Barrier System (EBS) Task Force. While historically both task forces have centered on
 experimental work conducted at the Äspö Hard Rock Laboratory (HRL) situated in crystalline
 rock, there have been other tasks in recent years that are generic or bring in experiments
 and data from other URLs. DOE joined both Task Forces in January 2014 and has been
 actively engaged since. In the GWFTS Task Force, DOE researchers recently studied
 modeling approaches to predict flow channelized flow and reactive transport in fractured
 crystalline rock (Task 10). In the EBS Task Force, DOE researchers developed complex
 reactive transport models to simulate the interaction of cement and bentonite (Task 12).
 Following its recent priority realignment, DOE decided to discontinue its SKB Task Force
 activities in FY26.
 The above collaborative initiatives foster active research projects, provide access to field and
 laboratory data, and/or may allow participation in field experiments in URLs. Complementing
 these activities, DOE or its national laboratories have engaged in collaboration opportunities
 provided by the Nuclear Energy Agency (NEA). Many of these NEA opportunities are less
 focused on active research collaboration rather than on the exchange of information or shared
 approaches, such as NEA’s Integration Group for the Safety Case (IGSC), the Clay, Salt, and
 Crystalline Clubs, the Thermochemical Database Project, and the Information, Data and
 Knowledge Management (IDKM) Project. In past years including FY25, the Disposal R&D
 Campaign has participated in many of these NEA collaborations.

December 5, 2025 ix

 In FY24, the Disposal R&D Campaign also instituted a technical partnership with the EURAD-2
 European Commission collaborative research program in repository R&D which will run for five
 years between 2024 and 2029. The goal is to enable collaboration of DOE scientists with
 projects funded under EURAD-2, thereby achieving cross-benefit between these two large
 research programs. Some of DOE’s national laboratories were incorporated as Associated
 Partners into selected work package proposals for EURAD-2.
 Bilateral Collaboration Opportunities (Section 4 of this report)
 In addition to its extensive cooperation with multiple international organizations under the
 aforementioned initiatives, the Disposal R&D Campaign has also explored (and in some cases
 executed) direct bilateral opportunities for active research collaboration with international
 partners. The status of selected and potential opportunities is described below.
 • Bilateral Collaborations with Germany: DOE and several German partners have a long
 history of collaborating on model benchmarking and data exchange for salt repositories in
 bedded and domal salt. The U.S.-German collaboration on salt has encompassed a range
 of topics over the past several years, including the performance of geotechnical barriers in
 salt formations and the coupled behavior of crushed rock salt as a drift backfill material.
 These topics are of particular importance for the design, operation, and evaluation of the
 long-term safety of underground repositories for the disposal of high-level radioactive waste
 in salt. In its FY25 disposal program realignment, DOE decided to place less emphasis on
 salt host rock R&D, which in turn ended many of the U.S.-German salt collaborations.
 However, there are many other opportunities for joint research between the two countries,
 as Germany, like the U.S., is now considering a broad range of host rocks and repository
 designs and has recently implemented a comprehensive repository siting program.
 • Bilateral Collaborations with France: DOE and the National Radioactive Waste Management
 Agency of France (Andra) have established a Memorandum of Understanding (MoU)
 regarding collaborative work in clay/shale disposal research at the LSMHM Underground
 Laboratory near Bure (also referred to as Bure URL), which is co-located with the French
 disposal site Cigeo in Meuse/Haute-Marne in the east of France. (LSMHM stands for
 Laboratoire de recherche Souterrain de Meuse/Haute-Marne, which translates to an
 underground laboratory in France’s Meuse/Haute-Marne region.) Andra and DOE
 researchers have been closely cooperating under the umbrella of the DECOVALEX
 Initiative. Andra is currently leading a modeling task in the DECOVALEX-2027 Project,
 which focuses on gas migration in sand-bentonite mixtures using data from a field
 experiment at the Bure URL.
 • Bilateral Collaborations on Site Characterization: Collaboration with a scientific deep drilling
 project in Sweden called COSC (Collisional Orogeny in the Scandinavian Caledonides) has
 provided a valuable opportunity for field testing of site characterization techniques in
 crystalline host rocks. From FY15 to FY24, DOE scientists have conducted two major field
 campaigns using the deep borehole to develop and apply a multi-step monitoring workflow
 for the in-situ characterization of fractures, including their hydromechanical properties and
 stress states. This workflow was independently demonstrated in FY24 and FY25 to
 characterize a fault zone at 1 km depth in the Bedretto URL in Switzerland. International
 collaboration on best practices and technologies for site selection and characterization is a
 long-term goal in the SFHLWD Disposal Research R&D 5-Year Plan (Sassani et al., 2023).
 • Potential Bilateral Collaboration Opportunities: Several other disposal programs may provide
 promising avenues for cooperation, such as the Republic of Korea, Japan, Belgium, Finland,
 the Czech Republic, and China. All these countries are open to collaboration with the
 Disposal R&D Campaign.

December 5, 2025                                                                                             x

 – In Korea, a generic underground research laboratory KURT, situated in a shallow tunnel
 in granite host rock, has been the subject of collaboration activities with the Disposal
 R&D Campaign in the past and would be open to more in the future. In addition, Korea
 recently launched a call for proposals to host a deeper generic URL for research into
 high-level radioactive waste disposal.
 – In Japan, the Horonobe (sedimentary) and Mizunami (crystalline) URLs in Japan may be
 accessible for Disposal R&D Campaign activities under the JNEAP (Joint U.S.–Japan
 Nuclear Energy Action Plan) agreement. A new opportunity for collaboration with the
 Japan Atomic Energy Agency (JAEA) has recently emerged with the launch of the
 Horonobe International Project (HIP), a partnership of currently eight international
 organizations, collaborating under the umbrella of the NEA, to use the Horonobe URL for
 the demonstration of advanced technologies in the design, operation and closure of a
 repository in a sedimentary (mudstone) formation.
 – Belgium has an R&D program in geologic disposal and a long history of experimental
 work at its High Activity Disposal Experimental Site (HADES) underground research
 laboratory.
 – Finland has already selected a site, submitted a license to operate, has almost finalized
 the construction of its disposal facility Onkalo near Olkiluoto, and is about to embark on
 a final step of getting ready for waste emplacement, the so-called Trial Run of Final
 Disposal. Construction of the repository has provided a wealth of relevant new data from
 the pilot hole and deposition tunnel investigations, such as fracture frequencies, fracture
 trace maps, and groundwater inflows.
 – The Czech Republic has an internationally oriented nuclear waste disposal program, and
 since 2017, has conducted in-situ testing in its Bukov Underground Research Facility in
 an existing mine in crystalline rock. The Czech radioactive waste management agency,
 SÚRAO, is expanding its experimental area for new experiments with the buildout of a
 second stage, referred to as Bukov URF II.
 – China has launched an ambitious long-term URL R&D plan and started construction of
 its Beishan URL, which is also situated in crystalline host rock. As of May 2024, the shaft
 has been completed, the ramp has been advanced to 4,000 m using a tunnel boring
 machine, and excavation of the first working level has started.
 • Bilateral collaboration opportunities may also be pursued to gain knowledge about
 international siting approaches and site characterization practices for geologic disposal
 through engagement with countries currently undertaking such efforts. Countries that come
 to mind are, for example, Germany, which is currently in the early stages of site selection
 considering the entire country and a broad range of host rocks, and Canada, which just
 selected the Ignace site from a long list of volunteer communities, or Switzerland which
 characterized three siting regions in detail and just picked one of them for license application
          (Kuhlman et al., 2025a).
 Research Activities Associated with International Collaboration (Section 6 of this report)
 Starting in 2012, the Disposal R&D Campaign has established a balanced portfolio of
 international R&D activities in disposal science, addressing relevant R&D challenges in fields
 like engineered barrier integrity, near-field perturbation, radionuclide transport, integrated
 system behavior, performance and safety assessment, site characterization, and monitoring
 approaches. These form a considerable portion of DOE’s disposal research portfolio, and
 significant advances have been made over the past few years across different host rock types
 and engineered barrier research challenges.

December 5, 2025 xi

 Figure ES-1 provides a visual overview of the significant international field experiments in which
 DOE researchers have participated since 2012, either as active members of the experimental
 teams or as scientists involved in the interpretative evaluation and model interpretation of the
 experimental data (see the list of experiments in Table ES-1). The figure tries to graphically
 illustrate the balance and focus of DOE’s international disposal research program over the past
 nearly 15 years. Notable observations are as follows:
 • From the center outward, one can see that the Disposal R&D Campaign’s internationa
 portfolio is well-balanced between an EBS focus, a near-field focus, and a far-field
 emphasis, utilizing field experiments conducted in multiple URLs in several countries. Not
 shown in Figure ES-1 are additional R&D activities that translate findings from the analysis,
 interpretation and simulation of field experiments into performance and safety assessment
 predictions of repository systems.
 • Several experiments address EBS behavior and near-field processes at the same time,
 which is no surprise because in-situ experiments in URL tunnels by design have near-field
 host rock impacts even if the main emphasis may be on the engineered barrier behavior.
 • Many activities are related to field experiments in argillite and crystalline host rock, whereas
 less international work has been conducted in salt host rock. This can be explained by the
 U.S. program having significant experience with the disposal of transuranic wastes in the
 bedded salts at the Waste Isolation Pilot Plant (WIPP) in New Mexico, so there is less need
 for international research outside the U.S.
 • The topic of “Integrated System Behavior, Performance Assessment, and Site
 Characterization” features two full-scale demonstration experiments with DOE participation,
 the FE Heater Test at the Mont Terri URL in Switzerland (ongoing) and the EBS Experiment
 at the Horonobe URL in Japan (ended in 2023). As mentioned above, the Disposal R&D
 Campaign has also participated in the field-testing of new site characterization methods and
 workflows, such as at the COSC deep drilling site in Sweden, the FEAR testing at the
 Bedretto URL in Switzerland, and the analysis of fracturing data from the Onkalo URL and
 repository site in Finland.
 Overall, International collaboration has enabled deep engagement of DOE researchers with the
 international waste management R&D community, emphasizing topics like new scientific
 advances, state-of-the-art simulation tools, site characterization and monitoring approaches,
 and best practices. DOE researchers have significantly improved their understanding of the
 current technical basis through the joint R&D with international researchers, the worldwide
 sharing of knowledge and experience, and the access to field and laboratory experiments from
 a variety of URLs and host rocks. Comparison with experimental data has contributed to the
 testing and validation of predictive computational models for evaluating disposal system
 performance in various generic disposal system concepts. Comparison of model results with
 other international modeling groups, using their respective simulation tools and conceptual
 understanding, have enhanced our confidence in the robustness of predictive models used for
 performance assessment. The possibility of linking model differences to conceptual model setup
 provides valuable guidance into “best” modeling choices and understanding the effect of
 conceptual model selection on prediction uncertainty. Advances in making long-term predictions
 of complex coupled THMC and flow and transport processes contribute directly to improving
 post-closure PA and SA models. Access to international field testing also gives an opportunity to
 develop, test, and demonstrate new methods and workflows for site characterization and
 monitoring of deep geological disposal. International collaboration finally provides opportunities
 for training/educating junior staff well suited to move the U.S. disposal research program
 forward into the next decades.

December 5, 2025 xii

 Selected highlights of research advances related to international collaboration activities in FY25
 are given below:
 Temperature-Induced Impacts on Engineered and Natural Barriers
 Collaborations with the international HotBENT and FE Heater Test projects involve large-scale
 experimental and modeling studies to understand temperature-dependent perturbations and
 alterations in engineered and natural barriers. Complemented by an international benchmarking
 exercise, these activities have provided valuable insights into how these perturbations and
 alterations may affect repository performance.
 • Three-Dimensional (3D) thermo-hydro-mechanical (THM) modeling of the HotBENT field
 experiment shows that the simulation results match the observed evolution of temperature
 and relative humidity. Overall, simulations confirm the combined effects of heating and
 hydration on the spatial variability of porosity, which influences the density distribution within
 the buffer. The 3D THM model, successfully tested against HotBENT field test data, can
 now be used to predict the long-term and large-scale temperature behavior of a high-
 temperature repository.
 • A series of benchtop-scale high-temperature column experiments was conducted to support
 the HotBENT field test. Building on lessons learned from previous HotBENT Lab Tests #1
 and #2, a new column experiment, HotBENT Lab #3, began in FY25, with the goal of
 helping to calibrate time-domain reflectometry (TDR) data obtained in the field test to
 evaluate hydration status.
 • The full-scale FE heater test at Mont Terri, now over ten years into its heating phase with
 temperatures up to about 130°C near the heater, continues to provide valuable THM data at
 relevant spatial and temporal scales. Under the umbrella of Task C of the DECOVALEX-
 2023 project, DOE researchers and several international partners finalized their
 interpretative and comparative THM simulations for model validation. Based on a
 comprehensive sensitivity study, the teams achieved an excellent overall match with the
 measured pore pressure changes in the host rock, demonstrating that reliable models exist
 for the long-term and large-scale temperature behavior of a moderate-temperature
 repository in argillite host rock.
 Experimental and Modeling Studies of Engineered Barrier and Host Rock Interactions
 Several international collaboration studies made significant progress in evaluating interfacial
 reactions between EBS components and host rock materials. Materials alterations near these
 interfaces can affect repository performance in multiple ways, for example, by changing the flow
 and transport properties for radionuclides to migrate from the engineered into the natural barrier
 systems. Interfacial reactions are most prominent early in a repository's lifetime when EBS
 materials such as bentonite, metal containers, and cementitious materials have just been
 emplaced and when TH perturbations remain strong.
 • Reactive transport simulations were conducted to interpret interfacial reactions and their
 impact across aged bentonite, concrete/cement, and clay rock interfaces. These activities
 were aligned with (1) the international Cement Clay Interaction (CI-D) experiment, which
 probes diffusive transport across materials that were emplaced more than 15 years ago in a
 borehole at the Mont Terri URL, and (2) the reactive-transport modeling of long-term
 cement-bentonite interaction as part of the SKB EBS Task Force Task 12 benchmarking
 activities. These experimental and modeling studies provide valuable insights interfacial
 processes and alterations and how they might impact repository performance in the long
 term.

December 5, 2025 xiii

 • High-resolution 3D simulations of the CI-D diffusion test at Mont Terri considered both the
 complex 3D geometry and the anisotropy of the geological formation. The high resolution of
 the model enabled the inclusion of “skin effects” developed at the interface between two
 highly reactive materials, the high-pH cement and the circumneutral but electrostatic
 Opalinus Clay. With these model improvements made, the simulation results were in good
 agreement with distinct geochemical and physical properties of the cement and the Opalinus
 Clay in the field experiment. Such analyses are crucial for establishing for long-term
 predictions of barrier performance.
 • The reactive-transport benchmarking exercise for interfacial reactions, conducted under the
 umbrella of SKB’s EBS Task Force, demonstrated good qualitative consistency in mineral
 dissolution and precipitation behavior among four international models, despite differences
 in some aspects of the model setup. Of particular importance are results for a modeling
 benchmark where advective and diffusive processes control interfacial processes between
 cement and bentonite. Modeling results suggest that a strong reduction in cement porosity
 near the interface effectively prevents any further mass exchange between the two
 engineered barrier materials. If confirmed, this “precipitation” barrier in the bentonite would
 improve the long-term safety of a repository as radionuclides cannot easily escape from the
 bentonite buffer.
 Repository-Induced Perturbations in Argillite Rock
 Through analysis of in-situ experiments at the Mont Terri URL, DOE scientists made significant
 scientific advances in understanding how repository-induced perturbations may affect the long-
 term performance of low-permeability argillite host rocks. One potential concern addressed is
 that gas generation in a repository could lead to pressure increases in the low-permeability
 barriers that may induce rock deformation and possibly damage. The second issue addressed is
 the potential for thermally induced fault reactivation within the host rock, which may in turn
 cause the formation of permeable transport pathways through the otherwise low-permeability
 argillite.
 • In the context of the DECOVALEX-2027 HyMAR task, LBNL scientists developed a new
 constitutive mechanical model based on a smoothed Mohr-Coulomb-Rankine (MCR)
 approach that can effectively describe the hydromechanics involved in gas migration
 through clay materials that may undergo plastic deformation. The new model was
 successfully applied to mechanical laboratory experiments combined with microscopic
 analyses of damage and dilatancy. Pending further testing against gas migration
 experiments, the new MCR model provides a solid framework for simulating the anisotropic
 hydro-mechanical (HM) behavior of bedded argillaceous rocks.
 • DOE scientists advanced the data and modeling analysis of the Mont Terri fault reactivation
 experiments to explore the ability of reactivated faults to self-seal over time. The integration
 of in-situ experiments and modeling revealed several key mechanisms contributing to the
 observed self-sealing phenomena following the activation of a shale fault. The fault rupture
 occurred mainly without seismic activity, and several months after activation, the fault nearly
 sealed itself to almost zero permeability, but not entirely. Time estimates for the full return of
 a fault to its initial small transmissivity project a minimum of 50 years.
 • Design planning was initiated for a new thermal fault activation experiment to address the
 risk of losing natural barrier integrity due to thermal stresses, a topic becoming increasingly
 important with higher emplacement temperatures. As currently envisioned, the Fault Heating
 Experiment will be conducted in late FY26 in the same fault testbed used for isothermal
 testing, benefiting from the already installed multi-modal monitoring network.

December 5, 2025 xiv

 Hydromechanics, Fluid Flow, and Transport in Fractured Crystalline Rock
 Because crystalline formations are often fractured, repository performance assessment studies
 require an understanding of (1) the characteristics of fracture networks and how they can be
 represented with fracture network models, (2) the impact of stress changes and other
 perturbations on fracture flow and transport properties and (3) the ability to simulate large-scale
 transport patterns of radionuclides migrating in fracture networks from the repository to the
 biosphere. Four international collaboration activities have made significant progress in
 addressing these challenges.
 • The SAFENET-2 task of the DECOVALEX-2027 initiative involves collaborative modeling of
 fracture nucleation and evolution processes from the laboratory scale to the field scale. In
 the first step of this modeling task, international scientists tested their respective modeling
 solutions for hydraulic fracturing processes against known benchmark tests. DOE scientists
 demonstrated the suitability of a new phase-field framework for hydraulic fracture generation
 and propagation.
 • Participating teams in the DECOVALEX-2027 FRESCIP task used statistical analyses to
 verify predictions of fracture orientation, spacing, intensity, and transmissivity against known
 synthetic benchmarks of complex fracture networks. Emphasis is placed on quantifying
 model accuracy and supporting decision-making processes related to repository design,
 construction, and long-term performance assessment. Los Alamos National Laboratory
 (LANL) researchers support the FRESCIP task with the use of their powerful discrete
 fracture network (DFN) software tool dfnWorks. The next step of FRESCIP will assess
 predictive capabilities for rock suitability and groundwater against actual data from the
 Demonstration Area at Posiva's ONKALO facility in Olkiluoto, Finland.
 • Task 10 of the SKB GWFTS Task Force focuses on validation approaches for groundwater
 flow and transport modeling involving discrete fracture features. The participating groups,
 dealing with flow, geometry, and transport at the small fracture network scale, have
 developed and applying a standardized workflow for pragmatic validation, to be further
 tested against extensive datasets form the TRUE Block Scale experiments at SKB's Äspö
 Hard Rock Laboratory.
 • The DOwnhole Robotic Stress/Strain Analyzer (DORSA) probe for long-term
 hydromechanical monitoring of faults and fractures, recently developed by DOE scientists,
 was tested in two crystalline rock testbeds made available by international collaboration with
 Japanese and Swiss programs. Enhanced with fiber optics monitoring, DORSA provided
 measurements of high relevance for repository safety assessments, as large fault
 displacements could potentially lead to shear stresses on waste canisters and fracture
 permeability increases could generate pathways for radionuclide transport.
 Coupled Processes, Geomechanics, and Brine Migration in Salt Rock
 A broad portfolio of international collaboration resulted in significant advances in understanding
 complex mechanical, hydrological, thermal, and chemical processes in salt host rock. In the
 Spring of 2025, DOE’s disposal research portfolio experienced a significant realignment of R&D
 priorities and scope, with resulted in the termination all salt-related disposal research work in
 May 2025. Key accomplishments made in FY25 until the work was terminated are listed in
 below.
 • The MEASURES project is a collaboration of German, Dutch, American, and British
 researchers seeking to improve THM models for crushed salt to be used as backfill for
 emplacement tunnels. DOE researchers worked on a new constitutive model for crushed
 salt featuring the following improvements: (1) a generalized grain geometry allowing for a

December 5, 2025 xv

 consistent transition from porous to intact salt, (2) an anisotropic formulation capable of
 capturing deformation history, and (3) a new physicochemical model as an alternative to the
 phenomenological approach.
 • International collaboration within the BATS and BATS2 tasks of the international
 DECOVALEX Initiative allowed scientists involved in the Disposal R&D Campaign to benefit
 from the engagement of international partners from Germany, the Netherlands, and the
 United Kingdom in the analysis and modeling of brine migration in heated salt. THM
 modeling conducted by the joint team of LBNL, Sandia National Laboratories (SNL), and
 LANL researchers captured the main characteristics observed in the heater experiments at
 WIPP, including the dynamics of brine migration and damage in the rock salt during heating
 and cooling. This work helped improve the understanding of the complex processes
 expected to occur in the Excavation Damage/Disturbed Zone (EDZ) of a salt repository for
 the disposal of heat-generating radioactive waste and supports building the long-term
 repository safety case for radioactive waste disposal in salt.
 • Under the umbrella of the NEA Salt Club, the DOE scientists and their international
 colleagues continued to develop a comprehensive Features, Events, and Processes (FEP)s
 database for salt host rock. They extended these efforts towards a generalized approach to
 scenario development for a high-level waste repository at a generic salt site.
 Sealing Systems for Repository Tunnels and Shafts
 Effective sealing of tunnels and shafts is important to block potential pathways of radionuclides
 between a nuclear waste repository and the biosphere. Under the umbrella of DECOVALEX-
 2027, DOE scientists joined the collaborative modeling of the Sandwich Experiment at Mont
 Terri, which tests a newly developed shaft sealing concept comprising alternating layers of
 bentonite and equipotential layers.
 • Hydro-Mechanical (HM) simulations conducted for the DECOVALEX-2027 Sandwich task
 demonstrate the effectiveness of this innovative shaft sealing system, which consists of
 alternating layers of bentonite and equipotential layers. Early modeling results agree
 reasonably well with data from laboratory-scale experiments.
 Performance Assessment and Radionuclide Transport Modeling
 Recognizing the importance of building confidence in the models, methods, and software used
 for post-closure performance/safety assessments and radionuclide transport predictions, the
 Disposal R&D Campaign has increased its international collaboration in this area. Important
 activities were the (1) collaborative modeling of crystalline and salt reference cases under the
 umbrella of the DECOVALEX-2027 initiative, (2) the development of thermodynamic and
 thermochemical databases in collaboration with international partners, (3) the joint modeling of
 iodine transport at the Cigar Lake natural analog.
 • In the Crystalline PA Task of the DECOVALEX-2027 initiative, confidence in performance
 assessment models and methods was enhanced by comparing PA modeling results from
 ten international disposal programs applied to the same reference case involving a
 repository in fractured crystalline host rock. To identify the sources and relative effects of
 uncertainties in models, participating teams developed and tested a workflow to conduct
 uncertainty quantification and sensitivity analyses (UQ/SA) on radionuclide transport in
 complex fracture networks.
 • Initial modeling results from the DECOVALEX-2027 Crystalline PA task for deterministic and
 stochastic fracture network scenarios indicate that stress-driven aperture adjustments can
 cause up to an order of magnitude variation in transport times, especially in networks with

December 5, 2025 xvi

 dominant fractures oriented perpendicular to principal stress directions. The impact of stress
 is less prominent in modeling scenarios with deterministic large fractures compared to purely
 stochastic networks. Developing reliable models for performance and safety assessments is
 a primary goal of DECOVALEX-2027.
 • Continued international collaboration of Lawrence Livermore National Laboratory (LLNL)
 researchers with the international geochemical modeling community led to significant
 improvement in thermodynamic and thermochemical databases and models for radionuclide
 transport simulations. Interoperable open-source databases have been developed that
 leverage modern data science workflows and algorithms. This collaborative achievement
 provides a strong foundation for future work in geochemical modeling, with the potential to
 support complex scenarios in nuclear waste isolation, rock-water interactions, and other
 related applications.
 • Natural analogs are a valuable tool for bridging the scales (spatial and temporal) between
 laboratory/field tests and a deep geologic repository. In the context of DECOVALEX-2027,
 modeling teams from LBNL and SNL have been collaborating with several international
 teams in the comparative modeling of the Cigar Lake Analog. Although the mechanisms of
 129
 I migration at the Cigar Lake uranium deposit remain somewhat unclear due to data
 uncertainties, the preliminary models developed to date illustrate the complex interactions
 between rate-controlling reactions and transport processes.
 Next Generation Workforce Development
 Investing in the next generation of nuclear waste scientists and leaders is vital to fulfilling DOE
 NE’s mission of developing solutions for the long-term geologic disposal of radioactive waste. A
 few years ago, the Disposal R&D Campaign established a Next Generation Workforce
 Development Pilot Program to attract, train and advance a talented and diverse future workforce
 in disposal research. LBNL and SNL initiated the program and additional national laboratories
 were incorporated in later years. International collaboration has been important in this context.
 To help recruit and train a broad talent pool, the workforce development program provided
 interesting research challenges and attractive work-abroad opportunities in conjunction with
 international collaboration partners. Unfortunately, the funding for the program ended in early
 2025 when DOE’s disposal research priorities were going through a significant realignment.
 Accomplishments from the three-year program are summarized below.
 • Stimulating Student Interest in Nuclear Waste Research: Between FY22 and FY24, the Next
 Generation Workforce Development Pilot Program inspired hundreds of students via
 organized lectures and seminars to become interested in nuclear waste disposal science.
 • Creating the Pipeline: The summer internship program trained a large diverse student group
 of about 30 interns at LBNL and SNL. This clearly created a pipeline: Many of the summer
 interns returned to their hosting national lab (or other opportunities) after their initial summer
 program ended, either for another summer program or in some cases as year-round interns.
 At LBNL, three summer interns were later hired as postdoctoral researchers.
 • Advancing the Pipeline: LBNL and SNL each hired a dedicated “next generation” postdoc for
 a three-year research project with international collaboration, which enabled them to make
 significant research contributions. For example, LBNL’s dedicated post-doc worked on THM
 modeling for the international HotBENT Project and, to better connect with the international
 community, participated in a 2023 summer training program at the Grimsel Test Site. SNL’s
 dedicated postdoc worked in GDSA Repository Safety Assessment (RSA), incorporating
 geology into UQ/SA. Unfortunately, the postdocs could not transition into career scientist

December 5, 2025 xvii

 positions at the two national laboratories, due to recent funding reductions in the Disposal
 R&D Campaign.
 Future Direction of International Collaboration (Sections 5 and 7)
 As mentioned before, DOE’s disposal research portfolio in FY25 experienced a realignment of
 R&D priorities and scope, placing a stronger focus on argillite and crystalline host rocks and
 emphasizing R&D collaborations and activities that advance two major campaign priorities: (1)
 To demonstrate technical readiness and provide options for decision makers with robust safety
 assessments for disposal reference cases, and (2) To reduce program liabilities by
 demonstrating the feasibility of more cost-effective high-temperature disposal. The following
 international activities will be conducted in FY26, aligned with the major campaign priorities as
 follows:
 • Demonstrate Technical Readiness and Provide Options for Decision Makers:
 International activities in this area support the validity of and confidence in performance and
 safety assessment predictions for evaluation and comparison of disposal solutions. DOE
 has a strong interest in reducing unwarranted conservatism in performance and safety
 assessment modeling for future licensing purposes. The FY26 portfolio includes three model
 comparison tasks in the DECOVALEX-2027 initiative (Crystalline PA, ANALOG, and
 FRESCIP), interpretative analysis and modeling of the CI-D cement-clay interaction test
 conducted at the Mont Terri URL, and cooperation with three relevant EURAD-2 work
 packages referred to as RAMPEC, ANCHORS, and DITUSC.
 • Reduce liabilities:
 International activities in this area are geared towards understanding the potential
 performance and safety impacts of high-temperature disposal. DOE is considering the direct
 geological disposal of DPCs currently in dry storage (Hardin et al., 2014), which would
 benefit from much higher emplacement temperatures of up to 200°C at the canister surface.
 The FY26 portfolio includes participation in three thermal experiments at the Mont Terri URL
 (FE Heater Test, HE-E 300 Heater Test, and Thermal Fault Activation experiment) and
 continued work on the HotBENT experiment at the Grimsel Test Site.
 With these changes, the FY26 international disposal portfolio will be smaller and more focused
 than it has been in previous years. Yet, international collaboration will continue to be a key
 element within the Disposal R&D Campaign. In the past, the international portfolio has often
 adapted to priority changes or new emerging collaboration opportunities, and it will continue to
 do so in the future. The campaign routinely evaluates the technical merit of all international
 activities and their alignment with DOE priorities; reviews, assesses, and develops new
 opportunities in close integration with international partners; and revises the portfolio of
 international R&D activities as appropriate.

 1 INTRODUCTION
 Since 2012, in an effort coordinated by LBNL, the Disposal R&D Campaign in the Office of
 Spent Fuel and High-Level Waste Disposition Technology (SFHLWD) within the U.S.
 Department of Energy (DOE) has promoted active collaboration in disposal research with
 several international geologic disposal programs across Europe, North America, and Asia. This
 cooperation has enabled the campaign to leverage a comprehensive knowledge base of
 alternative repository environments developed over decades and has established a framework
 for active peer-to-peer research within international groups that conduct, analyze, and model
 performance-relevant processes. Through international collaboration, the Disposal R&D
 Campaign also benefits from substantial global investments in research facilities, such as
 underground research laboratories for testing and modeling, and achieves cost savings through
 joint funding of expensive field experiments. DOE has formalized collaboration agreements with
 multiple international disposal initiatives and partners. Scientists from national labs involved in
 the Disposal R&D Campaign participate in a balanced portfolio of international R&D activities,
 focusing on key challenges such as near-field perturbation, engineered barrier integrity,
 radionuclide transport, integrated system behavior, performance assessment, and repository
 siting. These efforts have constituted a considerable portion of DOE’s disposal research and
 have enabled significant advances over the past years.
 Overall, the emphasis on international collaboration has allowed deep engagement of DOE
 researchers with the international disposal R&D community in terms of new scientific advances,
 state-of-the-art simulation tools, site characterization and monitoring approaches, lessons
 learned and best practices. U.S. scientists have significantly improved their understanding of the
 current technical basis through the joint R&D with international researchers, the worldwide
 sharing of knowledge and experience, and the access to field and laboratory experiments from
 a variety of underground research laboratories (URLs) and host rocks. Comparison with
 experimental data has contributed to testing and validating predictive computational models for
 the evaluation of disposal system performance in a variety of generic disposal system concepts.
 Comparison of model results with other international modeling groups, using their own
 simulation tools and conceptual understanding, has enhanced our confidence in the robustness
 of predictive models used for performance assessment. The possibility of linking model
 differences to choices in conceptual model setup provides guidance into “best” modeling
 choices and understanding the effect of model uncertainty. Advances in making long-term
 predictions of complex coupled thermal-hydrological-mechanical-chemical (THMC) and flow and
 transport processes contribute directly to improving post-closure performance assessment (PA)
 and safety assessment (SA) models. Access to international field testing also gives an
 opportunity to develop, test, and demonstrate new methods and workflows for site
 characterization and monitoring of deep geological disposal. International collaboration finally
 provides opportunities for training/educating junior staff suited to move the U.S. disposal
 research program forward into the next decades.
 This annual report describes the fiscal year 2025 (FY25) status of DOE’s participation in
 international collaborative research activities on nuclear waste disposal in geologic formations.
 The focus of the report is on the description of opportunities that provide access to field data
 (and respective interpretation and modeling), and/or allow for participation in ongoing and
 planned field experiments. The report is an update to earlier annual reports summarizing
 international activities in disposal research (Birkholzer, 2012; 2014; 2015; 2016; Birkholzer et
       al., 2017; 2018; 2019a; 2020; 2021; 2022; 2023; 2024b).

December 5, 2025                                                                                             1

 2 INTERNATIONAL COLLABORATION STRATEGY AND REPORT
 ROADMAP
 Recognizing the benefits of international collaboration toward the common goal of safely and
 efficiently managing the back end of the nuclear fuel cycle, DOE’s Office of Nuclear Energy
 (NE) and its Office of Spent Fuel and High-Level Waste Disposition Technology (formerly Used
 Fuel Disposition, UFD) in 2012 developed a strategic plan to advance cooperation with
 international partners (UFD, 2012). The strategic plan lays out two interdependent areas of
 international collaboration (UFD, 2012). The first area is cooperation with the international
 nuclear community through participation in international organizations, working groups,
 committees, and expert panels. Such participation typically involves conference and workshop
 visits, information exchanges, reviews, and training and education. Examples with DOE
 participation include multinational activities under the International Atomic Energy Agency
 (IAEA) (e.g., review activities, conference participation, and education) and the Nuclear Energy
 Agency (NEA, see Section 3.5) (e.g., participation in annual meetings, NEA’s Clay, Salt and
 Crystalline Club, NEA’s IDKM Group). DOE also supports bilateral agreements for international
 information exchanges and other collaboration activities.
 The second area of international collaboration in the strategic plan involves active R&D
 participation of U.S. researchers within international projects or programs (UFD, 2012). Active
 collaboration means in this report that U.S. researchers work closely with international scientists
 on specific R&D projects relevant to both sides. Concerning the geologic disposal of radioactive
 waste, such active collaboration provides direct access to information, data, and expertise on
 various disposal options and geologic environments collected internationally over the past
 decades. Many international programs have been operating URLs in clay/shale, granite, and
 salt environments, in which relevant field experiments have been and are being conducted.
 Depending on the type of collaboration, U.S. researchers can participate in planning,
 performing, and interpreting experiments in these URLs, thereby getting early access to field
 studies without having in-situ underground research facilities in the U.S. The Disposal R&D
 Campaign considered this second area to be particularly beneficial to the program and decided
 in 2012 to focus on advancing and utilizing such active international collaboration in disposal
 research. Coordinated by LBNL, a focused effort was made to collect information on
 international opportunities that align with DOE’s priorities and complement ongoing disposal
 R&D within the campaign, help identify those activities that provide the most significant potential
 for substantive technical advances, interact with international organizations and programs to
 help advance specific collaborations, and initiate specific R&D activities in cooperation with
 international partners.
 Active collaboration can be achieved under different working models. One option is informal
 peer-to-peer interaction with international R&D organizations. Many U.S. scientists involved in
 DOE’s disposal research activities have close relationships with their international counterparts,
 resulting from workshops and symposia meetings or active R&D collaboration beyond disposal
 research. Continued support from the Disposal R&D Campaign for the participation of U.S.
 researchers in relevant international workshops, meetings, and conferences, and symposia will
 help foster discussion and expand such relationships.
 Another working model for active international collaboration requires that DOE becomes a
 formal member in multinational initiatives. Since the launch of the international disposal
 research collaboration program in 2012, DOE has participated in six international cooperation
 initiatives as a formal partner: the Mont Terri Project (since 2012), the DECOVALEX Initiative
       (since 2012), the CFM Project (2012 – 2015), the FEBEX-DP (2015 – 2018), the SKB Task
 Forces (2014 – 2025), and the HotBENT Project, a high-temperature EBS experiment at the

December 5, 2025                                                                                             2

 Grimsel Test Site in Switzerland (since 2018). These provide access to field data from URLs
 and/or allow participation in ongoing and planned URL field experiments. Section 3 of this report
 gives a comprehensive overview of these initiatives and describes the various opportunities
 arising from DOE’s membership, emphasizing those cooperations that were active in FY25.
 Outside of the above initiatives, DOE scientists also collaborated with individual international
 disposal programs. Section 4 presents an overview of international disposal programs that are
 collaborating with U.S. researchers or are open to such bilateral collaboration. Both sections
 describe relevant collaboration opportunities that the Disposal R&D Campaign has already
 engaged with as well as others that are promising for potential engagement.
 The benefit of international collaboration needs to be evaluated and periodically reevaluated in
 the context of the open R&D issues that can be addressed through collaborative scientific
 activities. A first planning exercise for the international portfolio in FY11 and FY12 led to the
 initial selection of a set of R&D activities that aligned with DOE’s goals and priorities. A thorough
 “deep dive” reassessment was conducted in FY18 and FY19 as part of a broad Disposal R&D
 Campaign roadmap update, described in Sevougian et al. (2019). The purpose of this roadmap
 update was to summarize the progress of ongoing generic disposal R&D activities since 2012,
 to re-assess R&D priorities, and to identify new activities of high priority, such as R&D on direct
 disposal of dual-purpose canisters (DPCs) which contain a significant fraction of the nation’s
 commercial spent fuel activity. Formal 5-year plans for disposal research activities in the
 Disposal R&D Campaign were developed in FY20 (Sassani et al., 2020) and updated in in 2021
 and 2023 (Sassani et al., 2021; 2023). Each of these 5-year plans included an updated section
 on the future direction of international collaboration activities. In FY25, DOE’s Disposal R&D
 Campaign experienced a major realignment of R&D priorities and scope. As a result, the
 international disposal R&D portfolio underwent considerable changes, leading to a more
 concentrated, targeted, and cohesive international disposal R&D portfolio going forward into
 FY26. Section 5 describes the process for planning and reevaluation of international activities
 and briefly discusses the recent alignment of the international portfolio with new program
 priorities.
 Section 6 summarizes key international collaboration research activities conducted by DOE
 researchers in FY25. The descriptions of these research activities are kept very brief, as more
 detailed information is provided in various referenced feeder reports. The section is organized
 by scientific challenge area whereas Sections 3 and 4 are organized by collaboration
 opportunity. Each research activity in Section 6 includes a reference back to the specific
 international initiative or bilateral cooperation described in Section 3 and 4 that has facilitated
 the collaboration. Note that Section 6 addresses the portfolio transition in FY25 and into FY26
 as follows: all collaborations and activities conducted during the fiscal year are reported on,
 even if some concluded during or at the end of the year, while making sure to point to any

 3.1 MONT TERRI PROJECT
 3.1.1 INTRODUCTION TO THE MONT TERRI PROJECT
 The Mont Terri Project is an international research project for the hydrogeological, geochemical,
 and geotechnical characterization of a clay/shale formation suitable for geologic disposal of
 radioactive waste (Zuidema, 2007; Bossart and Thury, 2007). The project was officially initiated
 in 1996 and has been conducted in a clay-rock underground rock laboratory, which lies north of
 St-Ursanne in northwestern Switzerland. The URL is ~300 m below the surface in argillaceous
 claystone (Opalinus Clay). The rock laboratory resides in and beside the security gallery (initially
 the reconnaissance gallery) of the Mont Terri motorway tunnel, which was opened to traffic at
 the end of 1998. The rock laboratory consists of the security gallery with small niches excavated
 in 1996, Gallery 98, with five lateral niches excavated in 1997-1998; a gallery for the EZ-A
 experiment, excavated in 2003; Gallery 04 with 4 lateral niches excavated in 2004, and Gallery
 08 with side galleries for the Mine-by Test and Full-scale Emplacement Experiment excavated
 in 2008 (Figure 3.1-1). An additional tunnel excavation was conducted between 2018 and 2020,
 significantly expanding the URL and providing ample space for additional experiments. More
 information can be found at https://www.mont-terri.ch.
 The Mont Terri Project operates as a collaborative program providing open access to an
 existing URL and the results of investigations. The research program consists of individual
 experiments divided into annual project phases. The Swiss Federal Office of Topography,
 swisstopo, helps conduct the operation and maintenance of the rock laboratory and provides
 operational management and experimental support. Organized as a consortium, the partner
 organizations (currently 23) fund the experiments and the related technical work. Partner
 organizations can select to conduct experiments, participate in experiments conducted by
 others, and have access to all project results from past and ongoing efforts, which are available
 in reports and a project-owned web-based database. Planning, steering, and financing is the
 responsibility of all partners participating in each experiment.
 Over the years, the organizations involved in the Mont Terri Project have provided substantial
 financial investments. Additional support has been given by the European Community and by
 the Swiss Federal Office for Science and Education. The Mont Terri Project has been very
 successful, and a wide range of experimental studies on clay/shale behavior (including
 backfill/buffer behavior) have been conducted. In recognition of the Mont Terri Project’s 20th
 anniversary in 2016, a Special Issue of the Swiss Journal of Geosciences provided a range of
 publications describing key experiments and achievements of the Mont Terri Project (Bossart et
       al., 2017).
 In 2011, the DOE leadership recognized that membership in the Mont Terri Project could be
 highly beneficial to DOE’s disposal research mission, and in early 2012, decided to formally
 apply for membership. DOE’s partnership started officially with Phase 18 of the project, which
 ran from July 1, 2012, through June 30, 2013. Today, DOE is one of 22 partners (plus swisstopo
 as a managing organization) from nine countries of the Mont Terri Project, namely from
 Switzerland (ENSI, Nagra, ETH), Belgium (SCK/CEN, FANC), Canada (NWMO), France
 (Andra, IRSN, Total), Germany (BASE, BGE, BGR, GRS, Helmholtz Association), Japan
 (OBAYASHI, JAEA, CRIEPI), Spain (ENRESA), United Kingdom (Shell, NWS), and the U.S.
 (Chevron, DOE). Participation in the project provides unlimited access to operating URLs in a
 claystone environment, with several past and ongoing experiments that are highly relevant to
 DOE’s R&D objectives. Membership has given DOE’s disposal researchers access to relevant
 field data and projects resulting from all past Mont Terri phases. More importantly, DOE
 researchers have been able to work collaboratively with international scientists on selected
 experimental studies, including the design, characterization, modeling, and interpretation

December 5, 2025                                                                                              5

 aspects of field experiments. DOE also has an opportunity to propose and eventually conduct its
 experiments at the Mont Terri URL, which could be an option for project future phases.

 Figure 3.1-1. 3D schematic of the Mont Terri URL with side galleries and drifts. This
 schematic does not show the recently finalized expansion of the URL.
 Abbreviations point to some of the key experiments conducted at Mont Terri.

 Figures 3.1-2 and 3.1-3 show an overview of key experiments conducted at the Mont Terri URL
 as described in the 2016 Special Issue of the Swiss Journal of Geosciences (see Bossart et al.,
 2017 and other publications in the Special Issue). The timeline in Figure 3.1-2 places these
 experiments in the context of relevance to different phases in the lifetime of a repository: (1)
 experiments related to initial conditions and repository construction, (2) experiments related to
 early-time perturbations, (3) experiments related to transient- to late-time post-closure phase of
 a repository. For comparison, the figure also shows two features that are important for the
 repository evolution: (1) the temperature variations in the buffer (red curve) indicating the
 thermal loading of the disposal system canister emplacement, and (2) the transient changes in
 buffer saturation (blue curve) indicating the slow process the bentonite buffer reaching fully
 saturated conditions. Figure 3.1-3 shows the same key experiments in a plan.
 Many experiments shown in Figures 3.1-2 and 3.1-3 are long-running tests that have carried on
 into the current project phase of the Mont Terri Project and in some cases may continue into
 future project phases. While a few experiments at Mont Terri are relevant to other subsurface
 applications, such as geologic carbon sequestration, most activities are related to the geologic
 disposal of radioactive waste or have cross-cutting relevance, including waste disposal. Since
 2012, DOE has engaged in several such experiments, such as Engineered Barrier (EB)
 Experiment, the Mine-by Test, the Gas Path through Host Rock and Seals Experiment (HG-A),
 and the DR-A Diffusion, Retention, and Perturbation Experiment.
 In FY25, DOE scientists have been participating in, or utilizing data from, three major tests at
 Mont Terri: the FE Heater Test, the FS and FS-B Fault Slip Experiments, and the CI-D
 Experiment, and for FY26 are considering participation in another experiment referred to as
 HE-E 300 Heater Test. General information about these experiments is given in Sections 3.1.2
 through 3.1.5, and summaries of DOE research activities related to these experiments are
 provided in Section 6. Section 3.1.6 discusses a few other selected experiments that may at
 some point be interesting to the Disposal R&D Campaign. Almost all field experiments at Mont
 Terri are further supported by laboratory and modeling tasks.
