 scientists, participate in the reference case modeling as one of the modeling teams. A summary
 of their activities is given in Section 6.7.2 below while more detail is provided in Section 3.1.2 of
 Mariner et al. (2025b) and Section 2.2 of Hyman et al. (2025).
 In contrast, Tara LaForce and Richard Jayne served as DECOVALEX-2027 task leads and
 modeling team for the Salt PA case at the beginning of FY25, but with the mid-year changes in
 DOE priorities needed to transfer task leadership to colleagues from Germany (Section 6.7.3).

December 5, 2025                                                                                                                                                    62

 3.2.4 DECOVALEX SUMMARY
 Benefits of Participation:
 • Access to multiple sets of experimental data from different URLs and different host rock
 environments,
 • Opportunities for modeling and analysis of existing data in collaboration with other modeling
 groups (typically less direct interaction with the project teams that run or interpret the
 experiments), and
 • Opportunity to suggest and/or lead modeling test cases of interest to DOE
 Status of Participation:
 DOE formally joined the DECOVALEX Initiative in 2012, with the start of the DECOVALEX-2015
 phase. During this reporting year, DOE scientists finalized several modeling tasks associated
 with the DECOVALEX phase, referred to as DECOVALEX-2023, which was initiated in early
 2020 and ended in December 2023. These tasks included studies on heat and thermal
 fracturing of low-permeability rocks, bentonite gas migration studies, two full-scale heater tests
 in argillite and a mudstone, respectively, a brine migration field test in salt, a task focusing on
 performance assessment methods, and finally, a fundamental task on shear activation in brittle
 host rocks. A new four-year DECOVALEX phase started in Spring 2024, called DECOVALEX-
 2027, with 18 partner institutions, more than 50 research teams, and eight research tasks. Jens
 Birkholzer of LBNL continues to serve as Chair of the DECOVALEX Initiative, which helps
 ensure that DECOVALEX tasks remain important to DOE’s R&D goals.
 Early during DECOVALEX-2027, two tasks were led by SNL scientists (Kris Kuhlman for BATS,
 Paul Mariner, Rosie Leone, Tara LaForce, and Richard Jayne for the Repository Modeling and
 Performance Assessment Methodologies task) but because all salt-related research of the
 Disposal R&D Campaign ended in FY25, the leadership of BATS2 and of the Salt PA task
 needed to be transferred to German researchers. And while seven of the eight tasks initially had
 DOE participation, DOE researchers are now involved in only three tasks, namely FRESCIP,
 ANALOG, and the Crystalline PA task.
 Outlook:
 As discussed in Sections 3.2.2 and 3.2.3, many of the tasks in the most recent DECOVALEX-
 2023 and in the current DECOVALEX-2027 phase are of high relevance for the Disposal R&D
 Campaign, and given the relatively small annual membership fee, the benefit of participating in
 DECOVALEX remains very high.

 3.3 GRIMSEL TEST SITE PROJECT
 The Grimsel Test Site (GTS) is a URL situated in sparsely fractured crystalline host rock in the
 Swiss Alps. The URL was established in 1984 as a center for underground R&D supporting a
 wide range of research projects on the geologic disposal of radioactive waste (Figure 3.3-1).
 GTS provides an environment analog to a repository site, thus allowing the development and
 testing of equipment, methodology, and models under fully realistic conditions
 (www.grimsel.com). GTS is a research facility and not a potential repository site, though
 investigations may utilize a wide range of radioactive tracers. As the site operator, Nagra has
 organized most experimental activities in the URL as multinational collaborative projects, which
 typically include several partners from Europe, Asia, and North America.
 Over the years, DOE has been quite involved with GTS activities. For example, DOE was a
 partner in the CFM Project at GTS from 2012 through 2015. DOE also participated in the
 FEBEX-DP at GTS from 2015 until its official close in 2018. Between 2018 and 2024, DOE
 researchers informally continued selected FEBEX-DP activities, for example, using FEBEX-DP
 data to improve modeling capabilities and using FEBEX-DP samples for further analysis. Since
 2018, DOE is one of several international partner organizations engaged in the high-
 temperature heater test (HotBENT), which started operating its heaters at the target
 temperature in May 2022 after years of planning and installation (Section 3.3.1). HotBENT is
 closely aligned with a main priority of DOE’s current disposal research programs: to
 demonstrate the feasibility of high-temperature disposal options.

 Figure 3.3-1. 3D view of layout of the GTS in Switzerland (Nagra, 2010).

December 5, 2025                                                                                          64

 GTS also offers collaboration opportunities that DOE has not currently tapped into. Examples
 are the Long-Term In-situ Test (LIT) and the In-situ Bentonite Erosion Test (i-BET) as part of the
 CFM Project, the Gas Permeable Seal Test (GAST), and the Materials Corrosion Test
 (MaCoTe), all of which have been ongoing for a while and are moving into interesting project
 phases. These ongoing tests and two recently initiated experiments (Advanced Corrosion Test,
 ACT; Muon Tomography Proof-of-Principle Project, MUT) of potential interest are further
 described in Section 3.3.2.

 3.3.1 HOTBENT – A FULL-SCALE HIGH-TEMPERATURE HEATER TEST

       3.3.1.1   OVERVIEW
 Several international disposal programs have been conducting research activities to understand
 if clay-based barriers can withstand temperatures higher than the 100°C, a threshold
 temperature that is usually assumed in a repository designs. For example, the Disposal R&D
 Campaign has been investigating the feasibility of direct geological disposal of DPCs currently
 in dry storage (Hardin et al., 2014), which would benefit from much higher emplacement
 temperatures. The performance of bentonite barriers in the <100°C temperature range is
 underpinned by a broad knowledge base built on laboratory and large-scale in-situ experiments.
 Bentonite parameter characterization above 100°C is sparser (especially for pelletized
 materials). At temperatures above this threshold, a potentially detrimental temperature-driven
 physicochemical response of materials (cementation, illitization) may occur, the characteristics
 of which are highly dependent on, and coupled with, the complex moisture transport processes
 induced by strong thermal gradients. The impact of such complex processes on the
 performance of a repository cannot be realistically reproduced and properly (non-conservatively)
 assessed at a smaller laboratory scale. Such an assessment needs to be conducted by large in-
 situ URL experiments, where the most relevant features of future emplacement conditions can
 be adequately reproduced.
 Starting in 2015, options for a targeted high-temperature experiment (150°C to 200°C) in a
 crystalline environment were actively pursued under the leadership of Nagra with several
 international partners, including DOE (Vomvoris et al., 2015). In FY16, Nagra and partners
 proposed a collaboration effort called the HotBENT experiment, a full-scale high-temperature
 heater test using the well-characterized FEBEX drift at GTS, which had been empty since the
 FEBEX dismantling in 2015. The benefit of such a large-scale test, accompanied by a
 systematic laboratory program and modeling effort, is that the temperature effects can be
 evaluated under realistic conditions of strong thermal, hydraulic gradients, which cannot be
 reproduced in the laboratory. This leads to improved mechanistic models for predicting
 temperature-induced processes, including chemical alteration and mechanical changes, which
 can then be used for PA analysis of high-temperature scenarios. The key question is whether
 higher repository temperatures would trigger mechanisms compromising the various barrier
 functions assigned to the engineered components and host rock. If the barrier function is
 (partially) compromised, PA analysis can evaluate whether the reduced performance of a sub-
 barrier (or parts thereof) would still give adequate performance. DOE’s participation in this
 collaborative effort is extremely beneficial; substantial cost savings would be achieved in the
 design of a repository if the maximum temperature of bentonite backfill can be raised with
 acceptable performance implications.
 From FY16 through FY19, Nagra held several planning meetings to discuss the potential
 partners' interests in the project and to develop a plan/design for the HotBENT project. Potential
 partners agreed on a common set of objectives as follows: (1) increase database (monitoring,
 sampling, lab-analysis) and understanding of buffer behavior at high thermal (T) conditions (up

December 5, 2025                                                                                            65

 to 200°C conditions at the canister surface), (2) assessment of buffer performance at realistic
 scales and gradients compared to small scale laboratory tests, (3) evaluate effects and impacts
 of microbial activities/corrosion processes/gas evolution, and (4) integrate modeling (e.g.
 THMC) and lab activities (e.g. mock-up experiments). The partners decided on a modular
 design with several modules that differ mainly in the type of bentonite, temperature range,
 design with/without concrete liner, etc. LBNL actively participated in the project planning and
 design from the very beginning and used THMC modeling work to help the final design of the
 experiment. A comprehensive report was completed by LBNL scientists to determine the extent
 of coupled geochemical alterations expected in HotBENT and whether these would lead to
 measurable changes in bentonite properties (Zheng et al., 2018). The official HotBENT Project
 started in 2018 once all agreements with the partner institutions had been in place. In addition to
 DOE and Nagra, the joint project currently has four other full partner institutions (SÚRAO, NWS,
 NUMO, and BGE) and five associated institutions (NWMO, BGR, ENRESA, Obayashi, and
 KORAD).

 3.3.1.2 HOTBENT FIELD TEST STATUS
 The following description and figures provide an overview of the design and status of the
 HotBENT experiment. The test was constructed at the end of the FEBEX gallery (drift) that
 starts at the northern entrance of the Grimsel Test Site (Figure 3.3-4). The gallery is
 geologically, hydraulically, and geophysically well characterized (Huertas et al., 2005) and has
 proven realistic conditions for saturation (Villar et al., 2017a,b). The experimental concept builds
 on previous experiments and large-scale heater tests in granitic and claystone host rock, such
 as FEBEX at the Grimsel Test Site, EB, HE-E, and FE at the Mont Terri URL. The experiment is
 designed modularly, whereby a module represents a heater rested on a bentonite block
 pedestal and encapsulated by a granulated bentonite backfill (Figure 3.3-5). Modules differ in
 design temperature, bentonite type, experimental duration, and whether a liner is used. The two
 modules deepest in the drift are separated from the others by an insulation plug to enable
 earlier excavation (i.e., after at least five years, Phase 1) of part of the experiment with minimal
 perturbations to the remaining modules (which may run as long as 20 years, Phase 2).

 Figure 3.3-4. Location of the FEBEX Drift at GTS (Nagra, 2017).

December 5, 2025                                                                                              66

 Figure 3.3-5. Final HotBENT design with individual modules (Kober, 2020).

 HotBENT features a dense and broad portfolio of monitoring and instrumentation equipment.
 The THMC evolution of the engineered barrier and the surrounding host rock will be tracked
 with ~4,100 instruments arranged in 28 monitoring segments along the tunnel axis, measuring
 properties such as temperature, mechanical pressure, hydraulic pressure, water content,
 saturation, and humidity, displacements, gas concentration, and gas pressure. HotBENT also
 includes corrosion studies with metal coupons distributed near the heaters and plans for
 microbiological characterization. The photo in Figure 3.3-6 shows the complex arrangement of
 the heater and the sensor equipment before the backfilling of the tunnel with granular bentonite.
 Note that four boreholes have been drilled in the host rock alongside the heated tunnel which
 are equipped with multiple packers for segmented forced hydration of the experimental testbed.

 Figure 3.3-6. Photo showing the heater and sensor equipment in the HotBENT tunnel
                 (Kober, 2021).

December 5, 2025                                                                                           67

 A detailed timeline for the experiment is given in Figure 3.3-7. After on-site and off-site
 preparatory work since 2018, the construction of the field test started in February 2020. The
 construction was completed in August 2021, and on September 9, 2021, all heaters were
 switched on to an initial power of 300 W per heater. The plan was to increase the temperature
 stepwise to reach the target temperature in 4.5 months. However, due to the poor accessibility
 of the test site in winter, the heating was conducted at a slower pace with close monitoring at
 the site. Eventually, all heaters reached the target temperature in May 2022. Sensors started
 producing data while the heating was ramping up to the targeted temperature. The variables
 monitored at the sites include temperature, pore pressure, relative humidity, water content, total
 pressure, thermal conductivity, heater displacement, plug displacement, and oxygen. Monitoring
 data have been periodically released by Nagra.
 Phase 1 in Figure 3.3-7 refers to the first heating period where all modular heating sections are
 in operation. As mentioned above, the plan was for Phase 1 to end with the dismantling of
 Heater 3 and 4, while Heaters 1 and 2 deepest in the drift continue to operate during Phase 2.
 Because the target temperature was reached later than initially planned, the HotBENT partners
 decided at the 9th partner meeting in November 2022 to extend Phase I to 2027 (Figure 3.3-7).
 More recently at the 14th partner meeting in May 2025, a discussion was initiated on possibly
 further extending Phase 1 by 1-3 years, to ensure that all monitoring objectives of this first
 project phase had been achieved.

 Figure 3.3-7. The planned timeline for HotBENT experiment as planned in 2022 (Kober et
                 al., 2022).

 3.3.1.3 HOTBENT MODELING
 In parallel with the construction of the experiment, Nagra and partners launched a HotBENT
 Modeling Platform, which includes modeling groups associated with individual partner
 organizations working on interpretative modeling of the test data. The multi-institutional group is
 structured to: (a) increase the support to the HotBENT experiment and its interpretative
 evaluation, (b) broaden questions being addressed to advance fundamental understanding, and
 (c) address modeling issues, specifically the quantification of conceptual uncertainties. In
 addition, specific expertise and skills of the modeling groups can be used to examine aspects
 raised by the data collected as part of the HotBENT experiment or to make specific predictions.
 DOE scientists from LBNL are participating in the Modeling Platform. LBNL started with a simple
 2D TH model and gradually increased the complexity and dimensionality of the model. In FY25,
 LBNL revised a 2D cross-sectional THM model to better interpret the data collected around
 Heater #1 and developed a 3D THM model. The thermal model considers the heat conduction

December 5, 2025                                                                                             68

 and convection, the hydrological model is represented by a two-phase flow model, and the
 mechanical model is an elastic model using a state-surface approach. The key TH parameters
 are the saturation-dependent thermal conductivity, water retention curve, relative permeability
 and vapor diffusion coefficient. After adjusting the thermal conductivity function and water
 retention curve measured specifically for one of the bentonites used in HotBENT and
 recalibrating the vapor diffusion coefficient, both the 2D and 3D THM models were able match
 the data reasonably, see Figure 3.3-8 and 3.3-9 for the temperature and relative humidity at
 experimental Section 4 as an example. More information on the modeling work is given in
 Section 8 of Zheng et al. (2025a). A summary is provided in Section 6.1.2 of this report. In
 FY26, LBNL will continue work on 2D/3D coupled models for HotBENT, pushing towards
 THC/THMC models by adding chemical reactions and mineralogy changes to the simulations.

 Figure 3.3-8. Time series results of temperature in Section 4 (center of heater 1) (Zheng et
                 al., 2025a).

December 5, 2025                                                                                         69

 Figure 3.3-9. Time series results of relative humidity in Section 4 (center of heater 1)
                 (Zheng et al., 2025a).

 3.3.1.4 HOTBENT LAB TESTS
 A two column tests associated with HotBENT Lab#1 (one heated and one unheated) started in
 June 2019 and were dismantled in December 2020 after running for 564 days. Extensive
 characterization of heated versus unheated samples has since been conducted. Note that when
 HotBENT Lab #1 started, the conditions in the field test were not finalized yet. Therefore,
 HotBENT Lab #1 had some conditions that differed from the field test design. For example, the
 density of bentonite and its hydration pressure were lower than in the field test.
 To better support the field test, HotBENT Lab #2 was launched in 2021 with test conditions that
 were very close to those deployed in the field test: (1) the bentonite was the same as that used
 in the field test, (2) the dry density of bentonite after installation was similar to that in the field
 test, and (3) the hydration pressure was 2 MPa, which was the expected hydration pressure in
 the field test after artificially pressurizing the surrounding rocks around the test tunnel with four
 boreholes. HotBENT Lab #2 used similar monitoring methods, including embedded sensors for
 temperature measurements and electrical resistivity tomography (ERT) and time-lapse
 computed tomography (CT) scans to monitor bentonite evolution. HotBENT Lab #2 was
 dismantled after running for 15 months. In FY25, analyses of the clay samples were conducted
 to measure the water content, dry density of clays and the corrosion of metal coupons.
 In FY25, LBNL conducted a detailed analysis of the ERT data collected from HotBENT Lab
 Tests #1 and #2. Through a series of controlled experiments at both sample and bench scales,
 it was demonstrated the capability of ERT to capture dynamic changes in resistivity resulting
 from variations in water content, temperature, and mechanical alterations. LBNL also developed
 a comprehensive THM model to interpret the results from HotBENT Lab #2, utilizing a dual-
 porosity framework to represent the complex pore structure of bentonite mixtures and deploying
 the BExM model to capture the evolution of macro-porosity and swelling behavior. See Section
 6.1.2 in this report for a summary of the FY25 work and Sections 5 and 6 in Zheng et al. (2025a)
 for more detail.

December 5, 2025                                                                                                 70

 Another HotBENT column experiment, HotBENT Lab #3 (see Figure 3.3-10), was constructed in
 FY25 to further support the field test and related modeling work. It differs from HotBENT Lab #2
 in the following aspects: (1) The laboratory column test uses the same Czech bentonite (BCV)
 that was selected to pack the section around Heater 4 in the HotBENT field test (see Figure 3.3-
 5). (2) The design hydration pressure is 0.8 MPa. In the field test, because the hydration
 indicated by RH data has been quite fast, the hydraulic pressure in the surrounding rocks was
 only pressurized to 0.8 MPa, which was lower than the initially planned 2 MPa. (3) Instead of
 using a CT scanner, geophysical monitoring is done with ERT. This is in addition to relative
 humidity sensors and TDR probes to monitor the hydration status of the bentonite. Because of
 the size of TDR probes (26 cm long), the configuration of HotBENT Lab #3 is different from that
 of HotBENT Lab #2: now the heating is implemented by a plate on the top and hydration is
 conducted through a ceramic porous plate at the bottom. The non-heated test column started in
 FY25, and early monitoring results are described in Zheng et al. (2025a, Section 5). The heated
 column will start in November 2025. See Section 6.1.2 in this report for a summary.

 Figure 3.3-10. Schematic diagram of the column design for HotBENT-Lab #3 experiment.
 Left: non-heated column; Right: heated column (Zheng et al., 2025a).

 3.3.1.5 HOTBENT SUMMARY
 Benefits of Participation:
 • Access to the results of field and laboratory experimental investigations from a long-term
 full-scale heater experiment focusing on high-temperature (up to 200°C) disposal of
 radioactive waste. This temperature range is important if DOE is to pursue direct disposal of
 DPCs.
 • Opportunity to participate directly in international research groups that analyze monitoring
 data and conduct modeling work on coupled THM and THC behavior in EBS and natural
 barrier system (NBS) materials.
 • Opportunity for designing the field experiment to DOE-specific needs as well as conducting
 own laboratory experiments.

December 5, 2025                                                                                          71

 Status of Participation:
 After years of planning, design, construction, and implementation, the HotBENT field experiment
 commenced its heating phase on September 9, 2021. The field test is designed modularly, with
 individual segments insulated from each other so that part of the experiment may be ended and
 excavated after 5 years (Phase 1) while the remainder of the test keeps running for up to 20
 years (Phase 2). HotBENT will provide valuable data on heat-induced perturbations and
 alterations in the EBS and NBS of a high-temperature repository for years to come. DOE
 scientists have participated in the HotBENT planning from the very beginning and DOE has
 been one of the founding partners of the HotBENT consortium. DOE scientists are also
 participating in the collaborative HotBENT Modeling Platform, and LBNL has been conducting a
 series of bench-scale laboratory experiments to mimic closely heating and hydration conditions
 of the field-scale experiment (the so-called HotBENT Lab test) to support parameter calibration.
 Currently the partners of HotBENT are discussing the possibility of extending Phase 1 by 1-3
 years and conducting a focused drilling campaign before the dismantling to ensure the condition
 of bentonite permits a successfully sampling.
 Outlook:
 The HotBENT field experiment and supporting laboratory and modeling activities are expected
 to remain a central element of the Disposal R&D Campaign for years to come. HotBENT is a
 key demonstration experiment for high-temperature disposal in bentonite-backfilled waste
 emplacement tunnels.

 3.3.2 OTHER EXPERIMENTS AT GTS
 Besides the HotBENT project, other collaboratively conducted experiments at the GTS may also
 be of interest the Disposal R&D Campaign. Below we briefly introduce some opportunities,
 including new projects that have recently been initiated (such as ACS and the MUT Project) as
 well as selected projects that have been running for years but are still available for DOE to join
 (such as LIT and i-BET as part of the CFM Project, GAST, and MaCoTe). Updates on these
 projects were given in the annual Grimsel Test Site International Steering Committee (ISCO) in
 2025 and some follow-up meetings, which are summarized as follows.

 3.3.2.1 ADVANCED CORROSION STUDY (ACS)
 The ACS is a recently initiated field experiments at GTS. The project is motivated by the
 concern that sealing (i.e., welding) of the canisters may lead to localized vulnerability. However,
 the corrosion of sealing materials affected by the transient aerobic period has not been studied
 in detail. The project, therefore, is focused on investigating the aerobic corrosion of sealing
 materials under the influence of the bentonite buffer. A series of field and laboratory
 experiments with various combinations of sealing materials, temperature, and geochemical
 conditions are planned in three phases. Phase 1 features small-scale modules (MaCoTe) at

December 5, 2025                                                                                             72

 ambient temperature under oxic/anoxic conditions for base metal and weld (2025 to 2029).
 Phase 2 will test the medium-scale modules at heated, oxic/anoxic conditions with radioactive
 source (2030 to 2034). Phase 3 will conduct a large-scale corrosion test (after 2023). The
 design for small-scale modules experiment was finalized in 2025. The Japanese waste
 management organization NUMO is leading this effort.

 3.3.2.2 A MUON TOMOGRAPHY PROOF-OF-PRINCIPLE PROJECT (MUT PROJECT)
 Muon is an omnipresent particle created in the upper atmosphere, penetrating deeply into the
 Earth’s subsurface. Working like medical x-rays, the application of muon tomography could
 provide a non-invasive way to detect key geological features (caverns, faults, and fractures)
 surrounding the nuclear waste emplacement tunnels. A proof-of-principle project has been
 proposed at GTS to study (1) if muon tomography is a feasible technique for safeguards and
 safety considerations in a repository, (2) what can be monitored by muon tomography (e.g.,
 spent fuel emplacement chambers, voids, tunnel systems, etc.), and (3) the role of muon
 tomography in unattended monitoring of a repository infrastructure during its construction and
 the operational phase. The experimental work will focus on testing the operation of different
 instruments and detecting known voids and fractures. The project was kicked off in April 2024
 and is scheduled to be finished in 5 years in two phases. Phase 1 has two work packages: Work
 Package 1 seeks to establish an “Open Sky” instrument deployment on the surface that
 continually monitors the muon flux and thus provides calibration data to any underground
 instruments. Work Package 2 plans to image the overhead tunnel system from a series of
 suitable vantage points, which offers the opportunity to compare borehole and planar detectors.
 Phase 1 will last from 2026 to 2028.

 3.3.2.3 EXPERIMENTS ASSOCIATED WITH THE CFM PROJECT
 The Colloid Formation and Migration (CFM) Project is a long-term international research project
 for investigating colloid formation due to bentonite erosion, colloid migration, and colloid-
 associated radionuclide transport. The CFM project was initiated in 2004 and had operated in
 four major phases. Using this novel setup, the CFM team conducted 30 colloid tracer tests
 between 2006 and 2013 in the controlled flow field in and near the shear zone. These
 experiments evaluated the transport of bentonite colloids with radionuclides from the source to
 the extraction point at the tunnel wall. In subsequent phases, CFM focused on bentonite
 erosion, colloid generation, and radionuclide transport, with two prominent experiments (LIT and
 i-BET) that are briefly discussed below. A new CFM phase started in 2025 (Phase 5) which
 comprises a new migration experiment for RN and colloids in the shear zone and a new
 bentonite erosion test (i-BET2), supported by laboratory and modeling tasks.
 DOE joined the CFM Project in August 2012 but ended its official membership in 2015. After
 that, DOE researchers collaborated informally with CFM researchers for a few years by
 supplementing the field interpretation with targeted laboratory experiments and modeling
 studies (e.g. Viswanathan et al., 2024). Currently there are no DOE activities directly related to
 the CFM project.
 Long-Term In-Situ Test (LIT)
 The CFM project started the LIT experiment in 2014, with the emplacement of a radionuclide-
 doped bentonite plug into an injection interval CFM 06.002 intersecting the flowing shear zone
 at the GTS. Three smaller diameter boreholes, CFM 11.001, 11.002, and 11.003, were drilled
 through the shear zone in roughly a triangular pattern around CFM 06.002 to serve as near-field
 monitoring boreholes during the LIT. Starting in 2014, the radionuclide-doped bentonite plug
 was placed in the flowing shear zone for about five years (CFM Phase 3), until 2019 when the

December 5, 2025                                                                                            73

 ensemble of four boreholes was overcored. Samples obtained from the overcoring were used in
 a comprehensive post-mortem evaluation of the LIT to determine the disposition of both
 bentonite and radionuclides in the shear zone at the end of the experiment. The post-mortem
 analyses showed that 237Np and 233U were detected by (SF)ICP-MS, which agrees with data
 from the outflow GGW/bentonite pore water; No 241Am and 137Cs were detected in the bentonite
 ring but 137Cs and 237Np were detected in the gel. Future post-mortem analyses will focus on the
 detection of low amounts of 242Pu and 241Am. In the field experiment, the presence of copper
 and vanadium in the bentonite at the interface bentonite/steel was observed. Enrichment of iron
 at the interface between bentonite and carbon steel was also observed, which might impact the
 radionuclides behavior in the bentonite. Diffusion of radionuclides in the altered LIT bentonite
 (two profiles) are being studied, and results will be compared with the ones from the laboratory
 mock-up experiment.
 In-Situ Bentonite Erosion Test (i-BET)
 The i-BET experiment aims to study bentonite swelling, colloid generation, and radionuclide
 migration in fractured crystalline rock. It addresses a scenario where a waste package breach
 allows radionuclides to sorb onto bentonite backfill, which may erode into flowing fractures and
 carry radionuclides away on colloids. The experimental setup (Figure 3.3-11) involves bentonite
 rings emplaced into a borehole around a steel mandrel and sealed at both ends with packers.
 During the installation of i-BET in late 2018, all bentonite rings were equipped with multiple
 sensors to monitor the swelling pressure and the water content. Additional boreholes were
 drilled to monitor the groundwater composition around the source and detect potential bentonite
 erosion. Near-field sampling was conducted from boreholes about 14 cm from bentonite and far-
 field monitoring from an extraction borehole 2 m away. For over 1,800 days of monitoring,
 hydraulic pressure remained low and stable. Colloid concentrations significantly increased over
 the background. Participation in i-BET provides a great opportunity to understand and improve
 the modeling capability for bentonite erosion.

 Figure 3.3-11. Layout and instrumentation of i-BET (Schneeberger et al., 2025).

 Past DOE Participation in CFM
 The CFM experiments are unique in that they evaluate colloid-facilitated radionuclide transport
 in a field setting that mimics a high-level nuclear waste repository scenario. Realizing the benefit
 of becoming a formal partner, DOE in early 2012 formally applied for a partnership in the CFM

December 5, 2025                                                                                              74

 Project and was accepted as a new partner in August 2012. The partnership gave DOE
 researchers exclusive access to all experimental data generated by CFM. More importantly, it
 allowed DOE researchers to work collaboratively with international scientists in ongoing
 experimental and modeling studies, and it involved them in the planning of new experimental
 studies. Like the Mont Terri Project, this type of international collaboration goes beyond the
 modeling focus of DECOVALEX.
 In contrast to both the DECOVALEX Initiative and the Mont Terri project, which comprise a
 range of experiments covering a wide spectrum of relevant R&D issues, the CFM has a
 relatively narrow focus, i.e., colloid-facilitated radionuclide migration. In part because of this
 narrow focus and the comparably high membership fee relative to other international initiatives,
 DOE decided in 2015 to cancel its participation in the CFM Project. In FY16, a comprehensive
 synthesis of the current state of knowledge of colloid-facilitated radionuclide transport from a
 nuclear waste repository risk-assessment perspective was assembled in an overview report by
 Reimus et al. (2016). The report relied heavily on findings from the extensive and carefully
 controlled colloid-facilitated solute transport experiments conducted at GTS. DOE researchers
 have been and will be closely following the progress of CFM to evaluate whether ongoing or
 future experiments would offer important new scientific direction, in which case renewing the
 previous partnership makes sense.

 3.3.2.4 GAS PERMEABLE SEAL TEST (GAST)
 GAST investigates the performance of bentonite-sand mixtures for increased gas transport
 capacity (to mitigate pressure buildup from long-term gas generation because of canister
 corrosion) within the backfilled underground structures without compromising the radionuclide
 retention capacity of the engineered barrier system (Figure 3.3-12). The objectives of the test
 are as follows:
 • Demonstrate the effective functioning of gas-permeable seals,
 • Validate and improve current conceptual flow models for sand/bentonite, and
 • Determine up-scaled water and gas permeabilities of sand/bentonite.
 Designed and constructed in 2011, the full-scale in-situ experiment had been in a slow
 saturation phase for almost 10 years and finally reached full saturation and good
 homogenization a few years ago. After a lengthy preparation phase, the long-duration Gas Flow
 Test (GFT) was conducted between May 2, 2022 and the second quarter of 2023. Before the
 start of GFT, the pressure gradient across the sand/bentonite was maintained by a small water
 injection rate of 0.1 ml/min, with a pressure of 1.8 MPa at one end and a pressure of 1.4 MPa at
 the other end. The GFT underwent eight phases. In Phase 2-5, a stable gas flow path was
 established for a gas mixture with 98% N2 and 2% He. In Phases 6 and 7, the system was
 flushed with pure N2 to prepare for a gas tracer test #2 in Phase 8. The preliminary
 interpretation of the GFT data (Spillmann et al., 2025, NAB23-40, GAST, gas flow test
 preliminary interpretation) led to the following conclusions:
 • The saturation state of the sand/bentonite was high prior to the GFT.
 • A gas path formed quickly with no significant entry pressure, located in the sand/bentonite
 portion of the test between cross-sections L4 and L6.
 • The gas path was of low volume but highly permeable compared to the permeability to
 water.
 Since 2024, the GAST underwent re-saturation and a sinus injection to characterize hydraulic
 diffusivity/permeability (Figure 3.3-13). A dismantling project to be conducted from 2026 to 2027

December 5, 2025                                                                                            75

 is under consideration now. Nagra also started a GAST Modeling Group with simulations using
 TOUGH2 to match the history of GAST and forecast future test plans. A THM model using
 CODE_BRIGHT was furthermore applied to simulate the flow path development due to
 mechanical effects.
 Overall, GAST provides an excellent opportunity to understand gas flow in bentonite-sand
 mixtures. However, the design of GAST and its objectives are quite similar to the NSC Test
 conducted at the Bure URL in France, which is the focus of the BaSiSS Task (Bentonite and
 Sand in Sealing Systems) in the current DECOVALEX-2027 initiative (Section 3.2.3.5).
 Currently, DOE scientists are neither participating in GAST or in the BaSiSS Task. If the
 campaign decides that collaborative field studies of gas-permeable sealing materials are
 relevant to its mission, both GAST and BaSiSS offer suitable options.

 Figure 3.3-12. Schematic illustration of Gas Permeable Seal Experiment at GTS (Reinicke
                 et al., 2020).

 Figure 3.3-13. Pressure evolution at S02 and S15 when water was injected at S02 to
 saturate the system (Spillmann et al., 2025).

December 5, 2025                                                                                     76

 3.3.2.5 MATERIALS CORROSION TEST (MACOTE)
 MaCoTe consists of non-heated and heated experiments to study the in-situ corrosion of
 candidate canister materials embedded in bentonite. Broad aims are as follows (Martin, 2022):
 • Obtain in-situ corrosion rates under anoxic conditions,
 • Study corrosion products and material interactions (metal – bentonite),
 • Study the influence of microbial activity through use of two bentonite densities, and
 • Investigate the influence of heat.
 The ongoing in-situ experiment comprises a series of specially designed modules (0.3 m long)
 inserted into a 10 m long vertical borehole and sealed with a double packer system. Each
 module contains 12 specimens embedded in bentonite with dry densities of either 1.25 or 1.5
 Mg/m3 (Figure 3.3-14). Different bentonite types and densities are used, mostly MX-80
 bentonite from Wyoming and the Czech BaM lime-magnesium bentonite. Starting in 2014, metal
 samples have been (and will continue to be) periodically removed for destructive and non-
 destructive testing (after 1, 4.5, 7, 10 and 15 years). For instance, B2 will be removed in
 December 2025; KI2, N3, N5 will be removed in April 2026. Currently, the analysis of previously
 removed samples is ongoing.
 At this point, the experiment offers DOE researchers a rich and growing dataset for evaluating
 corrosion processes in an anaerobic bentonite environment, at ambient and under heated
 conditions. The current experimental partners (seven) are open to additional organizations
 joining. The experiment may offer the option of adding material specimens of particular interest
 to new partners.

 Figure 3.3-14. Schematic illustration of MaCoTe Experiment at GTS (Martin, 2022).

December 5, 2025                                                                                          77

 3.4 SKB TASK FORCES
 3.4.1 INTRODUCTION TO THE SKB TASK FORCES
 SKB, the Swedish Nuclear Fuel and Waste Management Company, has been organizing so-
 called Task Forces as a forum for international organizations to interact in conceptual and
 numerical modeling of performance-relevant processes in natural and engineered systems.
 There are two task forces: the GWFTS Task Force, initiated in 1992, and the EBS Task Force,
 initiated in 2004. The EBS Task Force has two parts, one for THM processes and the other for
 THC processes. Different modeling tasks are being addressed collaboratively, often involving
 experiments at SKB’s Äspö HRL situated in the crystalline rock near Oskarshamn in Sweden.
 The Äspö HRL consists of a main tunnel that descends in two spiral turns to a depth of 460 m,
 where various tests have been performed in several side galleries and niches (Figure 3.4-1). As
 SKB collaborates closely with the Finnish repository program, the SKB Task Force occasionally
 includes modeling tasks related to experiments conducted at the Onkalo URL in Finland
 (Section 4.9). In recent years, the task forces have moved to include relevant modeling tasks
 unrelated to the Äspö and Onkalo URLs.

 Figure 3.4-1. The layout of Äspö HRL and location of main experiments (Birkholzer,
 2012).

 Like the other collaborative initiatives introduced earlier in this report, participation in SKB in the
 Task Forces requires a formal membership agreement. Each participating organization is
 represented by a delegate; the modeling work is performed by modeling groups associated with
 these organizations (not unlike the DECOVALEX framework). The task forces meet regularly
 about once to twice a year. In tasks involving experimental work, task force members interact
 closely with the principal investigators responsible for conducting experiments. Much emphasis
 is put on building confidence in the approaches and methods in use for modeling of

December 5, 2025                                                                                                 78

 groundwater flow and migration, as well as coupled THM and THC process, to demonstrate
 their use for performance and safety assessments.
 DOE joined both the GWFTS and EBS Task Forces task forces in January 2014. Other
 participating organizations in the GWFTS and/or EBS Task Forces are SKB, POSIVA OY,
 KAERI, CRIEPI, JAEA, Nagra, BMWi/KIT, NWS, NWMO, and SÚRAO. Regarding the GWFTS
 task force, DOE researchers initially conducted simulation work supporting interpreting the
 Bentonite Rock Interaction Experiment (BRIE) at Äspö HRL, Sweden experiment (FY14 and
 FY15). In FY17, their focus shifted to another GWFTS modeling task, which involved diffusion
 and sorption experiments conducted at the Äspö HRL, Sweden, and the Onkalo URL, Finland.
 This effort ended in FY20 and instead, DOE researchers have participated in a new GWFTS
 Task 10, which addresses modeling challenges related to multi-scale flow channelization in
 fractured crystalline rock and validation of fracture network models (Section 3.4.2).
 Regarding the EBS Task Force (Section 3.4.3), DOE researchers initially focused on simulation
 studies related to a full-scale demonstration experiment at Äspö HRL, the Prototype Repository.
 In FY20, the Disposal R&D Campaign moved to participate in the collaborative THM analysis
 and modeling of the FEBEX-DP experiment, a continuation/expansion of the THMC modeling
 conducted for the FEBEX-DP Project under the umbrella of the EBS task Force. In FY24, DOE
 proposed to the EBS Task Force leadership to include the HotBENT Lab Tests as a new
 modeling task (Section 3.3.2.3). This proposal was accepted as a new EBS Task 11, led by
 LBNL researchers with seven participating modeling teams. DOE researchers also started
 participating in a new Task 12 of the SKB EBS Task Force which involves long-term reactive
 transport modeling of cement-bentonite interactions for a stylized benchmark problem,
 considering mineralogical, chemical, and textural changes near the interface.
 During FY25, both the DOE’s and SKB’s priorities changed. The realignment of DOE’s R&D
 priorities and scope resulted in DOE ending its participation in the SKB Task Forces by the end
 of FY25. Meanwhile, SKB decided to terminate its EBS Task Force by the end of 2025 and its
 GWFTS Task Force by end of 2026 as the Swedish program is actively constructing a deep
 geological repository at Forsmark. Informal collaborations are encouraged to finish some
 ongoing modeling tasks after that.

 3.4.2 GWFTS TASK FORCE
 The main objective of the GWFTS Task Force has been to develop and apply appropriate
 methods for investigating flow and transport in fractured crystalline rock to better understand the
 retention of radionuclides in crystalline rock and to improve the credibility of simulation models.
 The task force has also provided a platform for interaction in conceptual and numerical
 modeling of groundwater flow and solute transport in fractured rock.
 For the first few years of DOE’s participation, the main modeling task conducted in the GWFTS
 task force was Task 8: Modeling of the BRIE at Äspö HRL. The BRIE experiment was a joint
 task shared between the GWFTS and the EBS Task Forces. The objective of the BRIE
 experiment was to enhance the understanding of the hydraulic interaction between the fractured
 crystalline rock at Äspö HRL and the unsaturated bentonite used as backfill. The experiment
 was subdivided into two parts: the first part involved the selection and characterization of a test
 site and two central boreholes, and the second part handled the installation, monitoring, and
 later overcoring of the bentonite-rock interface. Modeling groups, including a group of DOE
 scientists, were able (a) to gain a better understanding of water exchange at the bentonite-rock
 interface and (b) to obtain better predictions of bentonite wetting in a fractured rock mass. Task
 8 (BRIE) ended in FY17, and Task 9 was initiated, which involved modeling of two
 diffusion/sorption experiments: the Long-Term Diffusion Experiment at Äspö HRL and the

December 5, 2025                                                                                             79

 REPRO (Rock Matrix Retention Properties) Experiment at Onkalo URL in Finland. Task 9 was
 concluded in 2021 with extensive discussion on the most important lessons learned.
 A new Task 10 was introduced in 2020 and gradually became the only task the GWFTS worked
 on. The task title is “Validation Approaches for Groundwater Flow and Transport”. The focus is
 on (1) improving our understanding of multi-scale flow channelization in fractured crystalline
 rock, and (2) validating fracture network models and other models that can account for such
 channelization. The spatial scales range from the small (within a single fracture) to the large
 (small fracture network) scale.
 While previous GWFTS tasks typically involved experiments, the main interest in Task 10 is to
 evaluate modeling methodologies and approaches, emphasizing validation and conceptual
 model uncertainty. Experiments are still needed, but in Task 10, they are used to assess
 validation strategies rather than being the central element of the task. Figure 3.4-2 shows the
 structure of Task 10, starting in Task 10.1 with the development of a White Paper on pragmatic
 model validation methods, followed by testing of such methods at three different scales from
 single fracture to small network to block scale. HM laboratory experiments conducted on natural
 rock joints were included in Task 10.2 to examine validation approaches for flow within a single
 fracture with HM coupling.

 Figure 3.4-2. Structure of GWFTS Task 10 (Lanyon, 2021).

 In FY24, LANL researchers worked with other GWFTS task force team members to finalize the
 White Paper on DFN model validation as a framework document for model validation and as a
 reference planning document for the Task 10 validation activities. In parallel, LANL scientists
 developed capabilities to simulate channelized flow and transport in individual fractures and
 fracture networks. In past years, this work comprised initial simulations of transport in fractured
 rock, considering surface topography and textures with different connectivity structures. In
 FY25, modeling activities have concentrated primarily on Task 10.2, dedicated to modeling
 geometry, flow, and transport within single fractures (see Section 6.4.4 below and for more
 details refer to Section 2.3 of Hyman et al., 2025). LANL contributed to SKB Task Force

December 5, 2025                                                                                             80

 GWFTS Task 10.2.1 by conducting geostatistical analyses to characterize fracture surface
 roughness, primarily using measurements from laser-scanned fracture samples. LANL’s
 approach involved preprocessing the provided fracture data onto regular grids to facilitate
 consistent statistical analysis. Additionally, LANL made progress on Task 10.3, which focuses
 on the pragmatic validation of DFN models at the small fracture network scale. As an example,
 Figure 3.4-3 shows selected model results for Task 10.3.2 (Hyman et al., 2025).

 Figure 3.4-3. Results of Task 10.3.2, dealing with flow, geometry, transport, and
 potentially reactive transport at the small fracture network scale, highlighting
 particular interest in modeling fracture intersection zones and network
 compartmentalization.

 3.4.3 EBS TASK FORCE
 As mentioned above, the EBS Task Force has historically involved two distinct focus areas, one
 on THM processes referred to as EBS-THM, and the other on chemical processes referred to as
 EBS-C. The two EBS sections on THM and C traditionally worked on different modeling tasks,
 and EBS Task Force meetings were jointly held but in separate sessions for THM and C. In
 recent years, activities of the EBS-THM and EBC-C focus areas have been more integrated.
 The main goal of the EBS-THM focus area has been to develop and apply general and effective
 tools for the advanced coupled THM analysis of buffer and backfill materials, and their
 interactions with a saturated fractured host rock environment. Specific goals have been as
 follows: (1) to verify the capability to model THM processes in unsaturated as well as saturated
 bentonite buffer and backfill materials, (2) to validate and further develop material models and
 computer codes by numerical THM modeling of laboratory and field tests and compare
 modeling results with measured data, and (3) to evaluate the influence of parameter variations,
 parameter uncertainties, and model deficiency. Tasks in the EBS-THM Task Force did not have
 a fixed period but typically were finished in 4-5 years. New tasks were added when old tasks
 were finished and usually three to four tasks were running simultaneously. Tasks that were
 closed recently were Task 7 (Gas Transport in Bentonite), Task 9 (FEBEX In-situ Test), Task 10
 (Water Transport in Pellet Filled Slots), and Task 13 (Unsaturated Homogenization). The
 currently active tasks are Task 11 (Berkeley High-Temperature Column Test, or HotBENT Lab
 #1 and #2) and Task 14 (Slow Hydration of Bentonite). DOE scientists have actively participated
 in the EBS-THM focus area since FY19 and have primarily engaged in Tasks 9 and 11. Below,

December 5, 2025                                                                                          81

 we briefly review the currently ongoing tasks in the EBS-THM Task Force, namely Tasks 11 and
 14.
 The EBS-C focus area has aimed at advancing the fundamental understanding of
 physicochemical processes in clay or bentonite materials relevant to various aspects of safety
 assessment. In contrast to the EBS-THM section, which usually has a close connection
 between models and experiments, the “chemical” task force had been mainly working on
 conceptual model development and benchmark/modeling studies because of the complexity of
 conducting reactive transport modeling. After conducting a range of benchmark simulations and
 simplified modeling studies for several years, the EBS-C focus area decided in FY21 to (1)
 better integrate with the EBS-THM focus area, and (2) add lab and field monitoring data to its
 task portfolio. LBNL then proposed that the EBS-C focus area develop coupled THMC models
 for the FEBEX in-situ test, as part of Task 9 in collaboration with the EBS-THM focus area. The
 proposal gained support initially as the teams participating in Task 9 planned to expand their
 existing THM models to handle full THMC coupling. However, about two years after the task
 force accepted the proposal, LBNL was the only team conducting THMC simulations, while
 other teams did not make much progress. Ultimately, Task 9 ended in FY21. Task 12 was then
 initiated in FY22 with a focus on modeling physicochemical processes at the cement-bentonite
 interface. This task, led by LianGe Zheng from LBNL, became part of the integrated EBS Task
 Force portfolio. Both LBNL and SNL participated in the modeling work. Below, Task 12 is briefly
 described along with the other EBS-THM tasks.

 3.4.3.1 BERKELEY HIGH-TEMPERATURE COLUMN TEST (TASK 11)
 Task 11 involved the HotBENT Column Test (or HotBENT Lab #1) conducted at LBNL, a bench-
 scale experiment specifically designed to provide time-lapse CT and ERT images of THMC
 perturbation of a hydrating bentonite sample heated to a maximum temperature of 200°C
 (Section 3.3.2.3). The task description was provided by LBNL and made available on the SKB
 task force website. Currently, three teams (LBNL, SKB, and Clay Technology) are modeling. In
 FY24, the HotBENT Lab #2 test was added to Task 11. HotBENT Lab #1 and Lab #2 share the
 same configuration but have different bentonite properties and hydration pressure, which is
 ideal for sensitivity analyses. LBNL developed a THM model to interpret the data from the
 HotBENT Lab #1 test (Zheng et al., 2021; 2022). The THM model reasonably matched the CT
 density data and qualitatively matches the displacement data (Yoon et al., 2024). SKB finished
 a 1D model using the Comsol Multiphysics software, starting with a TH model in 2023, which
 then evolved into a THM model in 2024. SKB also modeled the HotBENT Lab #2. Clay
 Technology developed a 2D THM model for HotBENT Lab #1 in 2024 and a TH model for
 HotBENT Lab #2 in 2025. Overall, the TH and THM models provided a decent match for the
 data from HotBENT Lab #1 but discrepancies between data and model for HotBENT Lab #2
 remain. LBNL’s FY25 modeling work for HotBENT Lab #2 is described in Section 6 of Zheng et
       al. (2025a).

 3.4.3.2 CEMENT-BENTONITE INTERACTION (TASK 12)
 In Task 12, modeling teams collaboratively simulated long-term reactions occurring at cement-
 bentonite interfacial regions and resulting in time-dependent mineralogical, chemical, or textural
 changes. Details on how these reactions occur in space and time define whether an aged
 cement/bentonite interface may impede radionuclide transport (a beneficial effect) or cause
 delays in bentonite saturation (an unwanted effect). International modeling teams worked on
 with three modeling benchmark cases for different types of bentonite and cement as well as
 different temperature (non-isothermal versus isothermal) and saturation conditions (unsaturated
 in bentonite versus saturated). Subtask A investigated the interaction between MX-80 and OPC;

December 5, 2025                                                                                            82

 Subtask B focused on the interaction between MX-80 and low pH OPC; and Subtask C was like
 Subtask A but had a different initial saturation state. Because of different ways of establishing
 initial porewater concentrations, Subtasks A and B were later expanded to Subtasks A1, A2,
 and A3, and Subtasks B1, B2, and B3.
 Considering the limitations of continuum scale reactive transport model to describe the change
 in pore structure and the resulting permeability change, LBNL scientists in FY21 and FY22
 attempted to use a pore-to mesoscale model based on the Lattice Boltzmann Model (LBM)
 approach to simulate the cement-bentonite interface area. LBM may be suitable to describe the
 change in pore structure and the resulting permeability change with a feasible computation load
 for a scale of 10-20 cm. After completing the preliminary model studies, it became clear,
 however, that the LBM model was too computationally expensive to simulate a spatial scale of
 20 cm for 100,000 years. To align with other international teams in Task 12 (SNL, SKB, and
 SÚRAO), the LBNL team later re-focused on the benchmark problem using a continuum-scale
 model.
 Benchmarking a reactive transport modeling for concrete/bentonite interaction is very
 challenging because many aspects of the model development are still uncertain, including
 important model choices such as thermodynamic database, kinetic reaction rate, aqueous
 complexes, and secondary minerals. The modeling teams met regularly online to determine
 modeling details. Up to the last EBS Task Force meeting in September 2025, the LBNL team
 finished 1D reactive transport simulations for Subtasks A, B and C (see Section 6.2.3 of this
 report, and for more detailed information see Section 7 of Zheng et al., 2025a). The SNL team
 also developed and applied a 1D reactive transport model for Subtasks A, B, and C (see
 Section 6.2.3 in this report, and for more detailed information see Part 1, Section 4 of Jové-
 Colón et al., 2025). The SKB team finished Subtasks A, B, and C and the SÚRAO team finished
 Subtasks A and B.
 A systematic comparison between Task 12 model results of LBNL, SNL, SKB, and SÚRAO was
 presented by Task Lead LianGe Zheng from LBNL in the final EBS Task Force Meeting #37 in
 September 2025 in Pori, Finland. Despite differences in some aspects of the model setup, such
 as reaction rate and diffusion coefficient, all models are qualitatively consistent in mineral
 dissolution and precipitation except the SNL model which predicts a broader impacted area
 around the interface and little mineral dissolution/precipitation, possibly because of higher
 diffusivities or dispersivities used in the model. In contrast, the SKB, SÚRAO and LBNL models
 suggest that the area affected by dissolution and precipitation is constrained to a few
 centimeters around the interface.

 3.4.3.3 SLOW HYDRATION OF BENTONITE (TASK 14)
 The hydration of the bentonite buffer in argillite or granite host rock depends on the ability of
 bentonite to draw water from the host rock (based on the capillary pressure gradient between
 the bentonite and rock) and the ability of the host rock to supply water. For a host rock with low
 permeability and less mobile water, the water supply can limit how fast the bentonite buffer
 becomes saturated. This task, therefore, focuses on simulating a scenario with a limited water
 supply from the host rock. It involves modeling a laboratory column test conducted at
 Germany’s BGR (Task 14A) followed by modeling the HE-E heater test conducted at Mont Terri
 (Task 14B). In 2025, teams from SKB, BGR, CRIEPI, Clay Technology and Obayashi made
 some progress on modeling the laboratory experiment. DOE scientists did not participate.

December 5, 2025                                                                                            83

 3.4.4 SKB TASK FORCES SUMMARY
 Benefits of Participation:
 • Access to several sets of experimental data from URLs in crystalline rock.
 • Opportunity to perform modeling and analysis of existing field and laboratory data in
 collaboration with other modeling groups (typically less direct interaction with the project
 teams that run or interpret the experiments).
 • Performing simulations for benchmark problems to improve conceptual understanding and
 test modeling capabilities.
 Status of Participation:
 DOE joined both SKB Task Forces in January 2014. In the GWFTS Task Force umbrella, DOE
 researchers finalized their involvement in GWFTS Task 9 (diffusion and sorption experiments).
 Subsequently, they worked on Task 10, which focused on flow channelization in fractured
 crystalline rocks and validation of DFN models (Section 3.4.2.1). Regarding the EBS Task
 Force, the HotBENT Column Test series conducted at LBNL was brought into the Task Force
 as Task 11: DOE researchers not only provided the test details and all the data needed for the
 model but also contributed with advanced THM models (Section 3.4.3.1). In addition, LianGe
 Zheng from LBNL chaired a recently added Task 12 (Cement-Bentonite Interaction) and both
 LBNL and SNL have completed the related modeling tasks (Section 3.4.3.2).
 Outlook:
 FY25 activities marked the end of DOE’s collaboration with international partners in the
 framework of SKB’s Task Force. As DOE decided to end its participation in both task forces at
 the end of FY25 due to its program realignment, SKB also changed its priorities as the program
 launched repository construction at the Forsmark site. As a result, SKB decided to terminate its
 EBS Task Force by the end of 2025 and its GWFTS Task Force by the end of 2026.
