 4.9 COLLABORATION OPPORTUNITIES AT BEISHAN URL IN CHINA
 As a major nuclear nation with a long-standing geological disposal program, China has
 embarked on an ambitious plan to develop area-specific underground research laboratories in
 representative rock formations (Wang, 2014). China is moving swiftly with URL site selection
 and characterization plans; the current idea is that the country would focus on a granite site first,
 followed by a clay formation. The granite formations in the Beishan area, located in
 northwestern China’s Gansu Province, have been selected as the most suitable area for a high-
 level waste repository in China. This is based on an abundance of favorable host rock
 geologies, with eight large granite intrusions identified as suitable subareas. The Beishan area
 has other advantages as well: the region has favorable socio-economic and natural conditions,
 characterized by a highly sparse population, no useful farmland, lack of mineral resources, and
 generally poor economic potential. At the same time, Beishan can be easily reached with
 convenient transportation options. In terms of potential clay sites, China focuses on two regions
 of possible interest. These are the Tamusu and the Suhongtu regions within the Bayingebi
 Basin, located in the Inner Mongolia region. The Beijing Research Institute of Uranium Geology
 (BRIUG) manages China's geological disposal program, which is open to international
 cooperation. Under the umbrella of IAEA, BRIUG over the past decade has closely worked with
 several countries, including the U.S., for technical guidance and practical advice related to their
 URL program, for example, related early siting criteria, site selection approaches, and specific
 site characterization techniques.
 In 2021, China started construction of a regional underground research laboratory in the
 Beishan area. According to the current design, the URL will have a sizeable expandable
 research space at two main working levels (280 m and 540 m below the surface), accessed by
 a ramp and a personnel shaft (Figure 4.10-1). Construction has progressed as planned and an
 important milestone was reached on October 10, 2025: The ramp excavated by a custom-built
 tunnel boring machine was successfully completed, reaching the target depth of -560 m and the
 length of 6,991 m. Meanwhile, the shaft has also been completed, and excavation of the
 working levels has started (Wang and Liu, 2024). Research to be conducted includes site
 characterization activities (some of which have already begun as the excavation occurs, such as
 geological and hydrogeological mapping), testing of engineering technologies and engineered
 barriers, and safety assessment activities such as monitoring of radionuclide release and
 migration behavior. It is expected that underground in-situ testing will take place in three phases
 between 2028 and 2040. The final repository development stage – the construction of the
 disposal facility – is planned to take place from 2041 to 2050, assuming the in-situ testing
 confirms the area’s suitability (Carter and Nieder-Westermann, 2021).

December 5, 2025                                                                                               113

 Figure 4.10-1. Design of the URL in Beishan, China (Carter and Nieder-Westermann,
 2021).

 4.10 COLLABORATION OPPORTUNITIES TO DEVELOP BEST PRACTICES FOR
 SITE SELECTION AND CHARACTERIZATION
 As the Disposal R&D Campaign continues to evolve and may at some point transition from
 generic R&D to disposal implementation, an important goal for international collaboration will be
 to gain knowledge about international siting approaches and site characterization practices, via
 engagement with countries that currently go through such efforts. For example, Switzerland
 recently submitted its license application for one of three remaining siting regions in the
 Northern part of the country. Germany is currently in the early stages of site selection
 considering a broad range of suitable host rocks in the entire area of Germany. In 2024, Canada
 down-selected from initially 22 volunteer areas to one potential host site. The German and
 Swiss programs are two slightly different examples of one end of the spectrum of potential siting
 approaches: Starting with the entire country, a technically based down-selection is performed to
 select the repository site. The Canadian program is an example of the other end of the
 spectrum: Here, the program encourages volunteer sites to come forward which are then
 evaluated and compared to select the repository site. In both cases, technical and social siting
 criteria may be used in the process.
 In collaboration with these international partners, DOE scientists may review the spectrum of
 participatory siting approaches considering the two end member cases (e.g., begin with
 volunteer communities vs. begin with the entire country and down-select based on geologic
 considerations), and thereby gain a better understanding of best practices and lessons learned.
 In addition, the campaign looked at different site characterization strategies, methods, and plans
 from advanced programs, especially Canada and Switzerland because they recently conducted
 comprehensive site characterization across multiple possible host sites. Furthermore, DOE

December 5, 2025                                                                                            114

 scientists may be actively collaborating with or be embedded in ongoing international site
 characterization efforts, for example, via joint development and in-situ application of surface-
 and borehole-based characterization tools.
 Below is a summary of the current program status in Germany, Canada, and Switzerland:
 • Switzerland: Site selection of deep geologic repositories in Switzerland is governed by the
 Sectoral Plan, which defines three siting stages. Stage 1 started in 2008 with a down-
 selection from the entire country of Switzerland per pre-defined exclusion criteria (i.e.,
 similar to the current status in Germany). At the end of Stage 1, the Swiss implementer
 Nagra defined six regions for further consideration in Stage 2, which by the end of that
 second stage in 2018 had been reduced to three regions. These three regions, named Jura
 Ost, Nördlich Lägern and Zürich Nordost, are in the northern part of the country /near each
 other. All regions feature the same host rock, the Opalinus Clay, with only moderate
 variations in characteristics and properties. Based on data obtained from a comprehensive
 characterization and drilling program, Nagra was able to demonstrate in 2024 that the post-
 closure safety of deep geological repositories can be ensured in all three siting regions.
 Furthermore, Nagra concluded that Nördlich Lägern is the safest siting region of the three,
 as it offers the best geological barrier, a higher stability against erosion, and a high degree
 of flexibility for the layout of the repository (Müller-Germanà, 2024). In late 2024, Nagra
 submitted its license application for the Nördlich Lägern site, which is currently reviewed by
 the Swiss regulator ENSI.
 • Germany: The German implementer BGE started with the search for a repository site in
 2017, by identifying an initial set of areas across the entire country that seemed in principle
 suitable for hosting a repository. In 2020, these “suitable areas” still covered more than half
 of Germany’s surface area, meaning a significant down-selection still needed to be done
 before a few siting regions could be selected for more detailed investigation. In August
 2022, BGE announced that the reduction from a so-called “white map” of Germany to a few
 siting regions would be delayed. In addition, the goal of naming a final host site in 2031 as
 stipulated by the law is expected to be missed by a wide margin. The current timeline
 envisions a target corridor for site selection between 2046 and 2068. This speaks to the
 complexity of selecting a repository site starting from an entire country and comparing a
 broad range of host rock options, a challenge that the U.S. program may also be facing.
 • Canada: The process to select a repository site for Canada’s spent nuclear fuel started in
 2010, when the country began asking for volunteer sites to come forward. The Canadian
 implementer, NWMO, made it clear from the beginning that the program would only move
 forward at a site with informed and willing hosts. By 2012, twenty-two municipalities and
 indigenous communities expressed interest in learning more about the project and exploring
 the potential to host it. Between 2012 and 2024, the number of communities was narrowed
 down to two, through technical assessments and community well-being studies, both in
 Ontario: the Wabigoon Lake Ojibway Nation (WLON)-Ignace area with a granitic host rock
 and the Saugeen Ojibway (SON)-South Bruce area with a low-permeability sedimentary
 host rock. In late 2024, NWMO selected Township of Ignace as the host for their deep
 geologic repository.
 In FY25, SNL and LBNL conducted a preliminary assessment of international experiences from
 site selection for deep geological repositories, based on the experiences from Switzerland,
 Canada and Germany (see Kuhlman et al., 2025a. These activities will continue in FY26, with a
 focus on (1) developing guidelines for qualitative/quantitative criteria for preliminary screening of
 potential sites to assist with down-selection, and (2) reviewing site characterization frameworks
 for host-rock specific site evaluations.

December 5, 2025                                                                                               115

 5 SELECTION AND RE-EVALUATION OF INTERNATIONAL
 COLLABORATION TASKS

 5.1 COLLABORATION PLANNING AND RE-EVALUATION
 Starting in 2012 with an emphasis on international collaboration, DOE became a formal partner
 in several multinational initiatives that promote active joint research, often with specific focus on
 URL field experiments and related data: the Mont Terri Project (since 2012), the DECOVALEX
       Initiative (since 2012), the CFM Project (2012 - 2015), the FEBEX-DP Project (2015 - 2018), the
 SKB Task Forces (2014 - 2025) and the HotBENT Project (since 2018) (Sections 3.1 - 3.4). As
 a result, DOE researchers have been in a position for almost 15 years now that allows for
 participation in planning, conducting, and interpreting the many past and ongoing field
 experiments associated with these initiatives, and they do so in close collaborative partnerships
 with international scientists. DOE also reached out to—and explored options of collaboration
 with—individual international disposal and other research programs, such as from the Germany,
 Republic of Korea, Sweden, Switzerland, Israel, France, Japan, Belgium, Czech Republic,
 Finland and China (Section 4), which have led to several bilateral joint R&D activities.
 In FY12, when the Disposal R&D Campaign decided to focus on active international
 collaboration as a strategic emphasis for disposal research, the campaign first developed a set
 of guiding principles for the selection of collaboration options and activities, as follows:
 • Focus on international activities that align with the strategic direction of the Disposal R&D
 Campaign and ideally complement other disposal R&D activities in the campaign (e.g., use
 international experiments to test new modeling tools developed in the campaign).
 • Select collaborative R&D activities based on technical merit, relevance to safety case, and
 cost/benefit, and strive for a balanced portfolio in terms of relevant repository designs and
 host rock environments.
 • Emphasize collaborations that provide access to and/or allows for participation in field
 experiments conducted in operating underground research laboratories.
 • Focus on opportunities for active R&D participation of DOE researchers in international R&D
 projects.
 Starting from these principles, the campaign initiated a planning exercise to identify the most
 relevant and promising international opportunities and to select and develop a set of activities
 that align with its goals, priorities, and funding plans. In a general sense, the benefits of
 international collaboration are obvious: The Disposal R&D Campaign can gain substantial value
 from the knowledge, data, and capabilities that international partners have developed over
 decades of research. In terms of specific activities, however, the benefit of international
 collaboration needs to be evaluated in the context of the open R&D issues that can be
 addressed through collaborative scientific activities. The Disposal R&D Campaign had identified
 such open R&D issues in several planning exercises, as summarized for example in Wang
 (2010) and Jové-Colón et al. (2010), and in a roadmap exercise prioritized them by importance
 to the safety case (Nutt, 2011, Tables 7 and 8).
 As part of the initial planning exercise, DOE management and national laboratory scientists
 developed a comprehensive table of international opportunities to provide a basis for the
 selection of international activities. The table listed the most relevant ongoing or planned field
 experiments conducted in international URLs, provided information on how DOE participation
 can be achieved, which research areas would be the main benefactor, the key FEPs addressed
 (including a link to roadmap and FEPs importance ranking; using the Used Fuel Disposition

December 5, 2025                                                                                               116

 Campaign Disposal Research and Development Roadmap, FCRD-USED-2011-000065 Rev 0,
 March 2011 [Nutt, 2011]), and finally information on the experimental schedules. Detailed
 planning of an initial set of international activities was then conducted in three workshops held in
 FY11 and FY12. The objectives of these workshops were: (1) to inform DOE leadership and
 campaign scientists about existing or future international opportunities, and (2) to align ongoing
 work package activities with opportunities for international collaboration. The first workshop was
 a session held in conjunction with the Working Group Meeting in Las Vegas in July 2011, at this
 point mostly for informative purposes. The second workshop, held in Las Vegas in April 2012,
 was a full-day meeting to review the current and planned work scope within Disposal R&D
 Campaign work packages for possible leveraging with the international programs, and to
 develop an initial set of R&D activities that align with goals, priorities, and funded plans of the
 campaign. A third workshop was held in conjunction with the Working Group Meeting in Las
 Vegas in May 2012, to inform the campaign about the outcome of the full-day planning
 workshop, to present them with the collaboration options, and to initiate active R&D
 participation.
 As research priorities changed over the years and new opportunities for collaboration have
 emerged, DOE’s international disposal research portfolio evolved, and it will continue to evolve
 in the future. The campaign has conducted a regular effort to re-evaluate its international
 collaboration activities, in a process similar to the initial planning phase in 2012. Planning
 sessions were held in conjunction with the annual Working Group Meetings to review existing
 and emerging opportunities for international collaboration, evaluate their technical merit and
 cost/benefit ratio, to align these opportunities with the current and planned work scope within
 work packages for possible leveraging, and to develop a revised portfolio of international R&D
 activities that align with goals, priorities, and funded plans of the Disposal R&D Campaign.
 In FY18 and FY19, the Disposal R&D Campaign initiated a detailed re-planning effort to ensure
 that all disposal research activities were aligned with and of high relevance to the safety case.
 Starting at the Working Group Meetings in Las Vegas in May, 2018 and continued/finalized at a
 dedicated R&D Roadmap Update Workshop in Las Vegas in January 2019, all ongoing and
 planned research activities were discussed in the context of their priority score, which in turn
 was evaluated by answering two important questions: (1) how important is the knowledge
 gained from a given research activity in terms of the three safety case elements (pre-closure
 safety analysis, post-closure safety assessment, confidence enhancement) and its technical
 basis, and (2) what is the state of the art level that the research activity would support. Highest
 priorities assigned to research activities that were both important to the safety case and still had
 significant knowledge gaps. Detailed results from this roadmap update are given in the report by
 Sevougian et al. (2019), which summarizes the progress of ongoing disposal R&D activities
 since 2012, re-evaluates R&D priorities for all existing activities, and identifies new activities of
 high priority.
 International collaboration activities, and their integration with other disposal research activities,
 were included in this 2018 priority assessment. In addition to reviewing the benefits of ongoing
 activities, the participating experts evaluated several new international collaboration
 opportunities, e.g., the new tasks proposed for the upcoming DECOVALEX-2023 phase, and
 some planned Mont Terri, GTS, or SKB experiments. Priority scoring considered emerging R&D
 needs recognized by the international community (e.g., the issue of gas pressure buildup in
 response to gas generation from corrosion) or emerging priorities in the Disposal R&D
 Campaign, such as R&D on disposal of DPCs which now contain a significant fraction of the
 Nation’s commercial spent fuel activity.
 In FY20, the DOE requested development of a formal plan for activities in the SFHLWD disposal
 research portfolio over 5 years. The campaign leadership then worked across several control

December 5, 2025                                                                                                117

 accounts to discuss the 5-year direction of the campaign. It developed a report that serves as a
 strategic guide to the work within the disposal research R&D technical areas (i.e., the control
 accounts), focusing on the highest priority technical thrusts (Sassani et al., 2020). The 5-year
 plan, as well as its FY21 and FY23 updates (Sassani et al., 2021; 2023), distinguishes the near-
 term (i.e., the more certain 1- to 2-year timeframe) and the longer term (less specific 3- to 5-
 year) timeframe. The near-term emphasis can represent the present R&D portfolio with modest
 modifications that reflect emerging priorities and funding levels. In contrast, the 3- to 5-year
 period represents a longer-term vision of where the disposal research portfolio is heading
 provided there is no major external change to the program direction. According to this plan,
 international collaboration will remain an important cross-cutting campaign activity over the next
 five years, with regards to opportunities involving in-situ experiments in underground research
 laboratories across a range of geologic systems.
 In FY25, the Disposal R&D Campaign underwent a significant realignment of disposal R&D
 priorities and scope. The international disposal R&D portfolio went through another replanning
 effort, placing increased emphasis on argillite and crystalline as host rocks and highlighting R&D
 collaborations and activities that support two main objectives: (1) Understanding the effects of
 high-temperature disposal on repository readiness and safety assessment (up to 200°C at the
 canister surface), and (2) Testing and validating the accuracy of performance and safety
 assessment predictions for evaluating and comparing disposal options. As a result, the
 international disposal R&D activities changed considerably, leading to a more concentrated,
 targeted, and cohesive international disposal R&D portfolio going forward into FY26 (see
       Section 5.2.2).

 5.2 OVERVIEW OF THE INTERNATIONAL COLLABORATION PORTFOLIO
 5.2.1 INTERNATIONAL PORTFOLIO 2012 – PRESENT
 Today, nearly 15 years after its inception, the Disposal R&D Campaign has built a well-balanced
 portfolio of international collaboration activities in disposal science, tackling relevant R&D
 challenges and open research questions as follows:
 • Engineered Barrier Integrity: What is backfills and seals’ long-term stability and retention
 capability? Can bentonite be eroded when in contact with water from flowing rock fractures?
 How relevant are interactions between engineered and natural barrier materials, such as
 metal-bentonite-cement interactions? Is an increase in gas pressure gas migration a
 concern for barrier integrity?
 • Near-Field Perturbation: How important are thermal, mechanical, and other perturbations to
 a host rock (such as argillite/clay/shale, crystalline rock, and salt), and how effective is
 healing or sealing of the damage zone in the long term? How reliable are predictive models
 for the strongly coupled THMC behavior of clay and salt formations?
 • Flow and Radionuclide Transport: How does high temperature impact the diffusion and
 sorption properties of clay materials, considering the heat load from direct disposal of
 DPCs? What is the potential for increased transport via colloids? Can transport in diffusion-
 dominated systems like clay, bentonite, and salt as well as in advection-dominated
 formations such as fractured granite be reliably predicted?
 • Integrated System Behavior, Performance Assessment, and Site Characterization: Can the
 early-time behavior of an entire repository system—including all engineered and natural
 barriers and their interaction— be measured and demonstrated? Can this integrated
 behavior be reliably predicted? Are the planned construction and emplacement methods

December 5, 2025                                                                                            118

 feasible? Which characterization and monitoring techniques are suitable for site selection
 and performance confirmation? How reliable are performance and safety assessment
 models?
 Figure 5.2-1 provides a visual overview of the major international experiments conducted in
 various countries that DOE researchers have participated in since 2012, either as active
 members of the experimental teams or as scientists involved in the interpretative evaluation and
 model interpretation of the experimental data (see the list of experiments in Table 5.2-1).
 Experiments highlighted in bold denote collaborations that were active in FY25. The figure tries
 to graphically illustrate the balance and focus of DOE’s international disposal research program
 over the past years. There are a few notable observations:
 • Going from the center outward, one can see that DOE’s international disposal research
 portfolio is well balanced between EBS focus, near-field focus and far-field emphasis.
 • Several experiments address at the same time EBS behavior and near-field processes,
 which comes as no surprise because in-situ experiments in URL tunnels by design have
 near-field host rock impact even if the main emphasis may be on the engineered barrier
 behavior.
 • There are many activities related to field experiments argillite and crystalline host rock,
 whereas less international work has been conducted for salt. This can be explained by the
 U.S. program having significant experience with the disposal of transuranic wastes in the
 bedded salts at the Waste Isolation Pilot Plant (WIPP) in New Mexico, so there is less need
 for international research outside the U.S.
 • The topic of “Integrated System Behavior, Performance Assessment, and Site
 Characterization” features two full-scale demonstration experiments with DOE participation,
 the FE Heater Test at the Mont Terri URL in Switzerland and the EBS Experiment at the
 Horonobe URL in Japan. The Disposal Research Campaign also participated in the field-
 testing of new site characterization methods and workflows, such as at the COSC deep
 drilling site in Sweden, the FEAR testing at the Bedretto URL in Switzerland, and the
 analysis of fracturing data from the Onkalo URL and repository site in Finland.

 5.2.2 FUTURE PRIORITIES AND ACTIVITIES
 As mentioned earlier, DOE’s disposal research portfolio in FY25 underwent a realignment of
 R&D priorities and scope, placing a stronger focus on argillite and crystalline host rocks and
 emphasizing R&D collaborations and activities that support two main campaign priorities: (1) To
 demonstrate technical readiness and provide options for decision makers with robust safety
 assessments for disposal reference cases, and (2) To reduce program liabilities by
 demonstrating the feasibility of more cost-effective high-temperature disposal. The following
 international activities will be conducted in FY26, aligned with the major campaign priorities as
 follows:
 • Demonstrate Technical Readiness and Provide Options for Decision Makers:
 International activities in this area support the validity of and confidence in performance and
 safety assessment predictions for the evaluation and comparison of disposal solutions. The
 Disposal R&D Campaign has a strong interest in reducing unwarranted conservatism in
 performance and safety assessment modeling for future licensing purposes. The FY26
 portfolio includes three model comparison tasks in the DECOVALEX-2027 initiative
 (Crystalline PA, ANALOG, and FRESCIP), interpretative analysis and modeling of the CI-D
 cement-clay interaction test conducted at the Mont Terri URL, and cooperation with three
 relevant EURAD-2 work packages referred to as RAMPEC, ANCHORS, and DITUSC.

December 5, 2025                                                                                            119

 • Reduce Liabilities:
 International activities in this area are focused on understanding the potential performance
 and safety implications of high-temperature disposal. DOE is considering the direct
 geological disposal of DPCs currently in dry storage (Hardin et al., 2014), which would
 benefit from much higher emplacement temperatures of up to 200°C at the canister surface.
 The FY26 portfolio includes participation in three thermal experiments at the Mont Terri URL
 (FE Heater Test, HE-E 300 Heater Test, and Thermal Fault Activation experiment) and
 continued work on the HotBENT experiment at the Grimsel Test Site.
 With these changes, the FY26 international disposal portfolio has become smaller and more
 focused than it has been in previous years. Yet, international collaboration will continue to be a
 key element within the Disposal R&D Campaign. In the past, the international portfolio has often
 adapted to priority changes or new emerging collaboration opportunities, and it will continue to
 do so in the future. The campaign routinely evaluates the technical merit of all international
 activities and their alignment with DOE priorities; reviews, assesses, and develops new
 opportunities in close integration with international partner; and revises the portfolio of
 international R&D activities as appropriate.

 5.2.3 INTERNATIONAL LEADERSHIP
 DOE’s disposal researchers have been highly active internationally, taking on leadership roles
 in global initiatives and shaping the future of international disposal science. For instance, with
 Jens Birkholzer of LBNL serving as Chair of the DECOVALEX Initiative, DOE scientists have
 played a significant role in project task selection and have led related modeling efforts in recent
 years. Additionally, DOE researchers co-developed the planning and design of the HotBENT
 Project with international partners. Moreover, DOE scientists have held leadership positions in
 NEA initiatives such as the Salt Club, the Crystalline Club, and the Thermodynamic Database
 Project. They frequently present technical highlights from international collaborations at
 conferences and symposia to engage with the broader radioactive waste management
 community and other geoscience fields. They also take a leading role in organizing international
 conferences and sessions focused on geologic disposal research: for example, the First
 International DECOVALEX Coupled Processes Symposium, co-organized by DOE researchers,
 was held in Brugg, Switzerland, in 2019 (http://decovalex-coupled-processes-symposium.org/),
 followed by the Second International DECOVALEX Symposium in Troyes, France, in 2023
 (https://2023-decovalex-coupled-processes-symposium.lbl.gov/home). A session on
 international collaboration in disposal research was organized during the “International
 Conference on Coupled Processes in Fractured Geological Media – CouFRAC” (held in
 Berkeley, November 14-16, 2022), focusing on recent advances in the nuclear waste disposal
 field and other applications. A similar session was held at the TOUGH Symposium 2023 in
 Berkeley from September 18-20, 2023.

 6 GEOLOGIC DISPOSAL RESEARCH ACTIVITIES ASSOCIATED
 WITH INTERNATIONAL COLLABORATIONS
 Section 6 summarizes the ongoing international research and development (R&D) activities
 carried out by DOE scientists in collaboration with international initiatives and programs. These
 activities include U.S. researchers participating in experiments conducted in URLs, analyzing
 laboratory experiments, conducting comparative modeling studies, developing thermodynamic
 databases, advancing site characterization and monitoring techniques, performing performance
 and safety assessments, interpreting natural analogs, and helping create a next-generation
 workforce in geologic disposal science. The summaries of R&D activities are prepared based on
 the FY25 milestone reports provided by DOE researchers from LANL, LBNL, LLNL, and SNL
 (see a list of FY25 milestone reports in Section 8 “FY25 Feeder Reports”). The structure of
 Section 6 is as follows:
 • Section 6.1 summarizes R&D activities related to the analysis and modeling of temperature-
 induced coupled processes in engineered and natural barriers and how strongly elevated
 temperatures may impact repository safety. Experimental and modeling investigations are
 associated with two full-scale in-situ heater experiments, complemented by an international
 model benchmarking exercise.
 • Section 6.2 details international activities aimed at enhancing understanding of physical and
 geochemical interactions between EBS and host rock materials. Material changes near
 these interfaces can significantly influence repository performance, affecting the flow and
 transport of radionuclides through engineered barriers into the surrounding natural rock.
 • Section 6.3 summarizes international activities aimed at predicting how repository-induced
 perturbations may affect the long-term performance of low-permeability argillite host rocks.
 One potential concern addressed is that gas production in a repository could lead to
 pressure increases in the low-permeability barriers that may induce rock deformation and
 possibly damage. The second issue tackled is the potential for thermally induced fault
 reactivation within the host rock, which in turn may cause the formation of permeable
 transport pathways through the otherwise low-permeability argillite.
 • Section 6.4 focuses on crystalline rock formations as host rocks for radioactive waste
 disposal. Because crystalline formations are often fractured, repository performance
 depends on (1) the characteristics of fracture networks and how they can be represented
 with fracture network models, (2) how stress changes and other disturbances impact these
 fracture networks through dilation, shearing, or fracturing processes, and (3) how
 radionuclides migrate through fracture networks from the repository to the biosphere. DOE
 scientists conducted several activities in FY25 to address these relevant topics, involving
 HM modeling studies to analyze fracturing processes, prediction of fracture geometries and
 groundwater inflows into deposition holes, validation of discrete fracture models, and
 application of a new high-precision and high-accuracy device for quantifying spatiotemporal
 stress perturbations in fractured crystalline rock.
 • Section 6.5 focuses on international collaboration activities related to the performance of salt
 as a host rock, including investigations of engineered barriers in salt repositories and
 predictions of brine migrations as measured in the BATS heater test at WIPP.
 • Section 6.6 summarizes activities to evaluate the performance of shaft seals used to block
 potential pathways of radionuclides between a nuclear waste repository and the biosphere.
 Under the umbrella of DECOVALEX-2027, DOE scientists joined the collaborative modeling

December 5, 2025                                                                                             123

 of the Sandwich Experiment at Mont Terri, which tests a newly developed shaft sealing
 concept comprising alternating layers of bentonite and equipotential layers.
 • Section 6.7 describes collaborative activities to improve post-closure performance/safety
 assessments and radionuclide transport predictions. DOE researchers were involved in
 (1) collaborative modeling of crystalline and salt reference cases under the umbrella of the
 DECOVALEX-2027 initiative, (2) development of thermodynamic and thermochemical
 databases in collaboration with international partners, (3) joint modeling studies to evaluate
 the transport of iodine at the Cigar Lake natural analog.
 • Section 6.8 provides a summary assessment of a pilot program at LBNL, SNL, and other
 national laboratories to develop a next-generation workforce in geologic disposal science.
 To help recruit and train a broad talent pool, the program provided interesting
 learning/research challenges and attractive work-abroad opportunities in conjunction with
 DOE’s multiple international collaboration partners.

 R&D Campaign milestone reports.

 6.1 TEMPERATURE-INDUCED IMPACTS ON ENGINEERED AND NATURAL
 BARRIERS

 6.1.1 INTRODUCTION
 This section summarizes international R&D activities related to the analysis and modeling of
 temperature-induced coupled processes in engineered and natural barriers and how strongly
 elevated temperatures may impact repository safety. Experimental and modeling investigations
 have been associated with two full-scale in-situ heater experiments, the HotBENT test at the
 Grimsel Test Site (Section 6.1.2) and the FE Heater Test at the Mont Terri URL (Section 6.1.3).
 Drawing from lessons learned in the heater test analyses, a third activity involves an
 international model benchmarking exercise for long-term THM processes in an argillite
 repository with heat-emanating waste inventory (Section 6.1.4).

 6.1.2 HOTBENT HIGH-TEMPERATURE EXPERIMENTS AND MODELING
 Background and International Coordination: The HotBENT field test is a full-scale high-
 temperature heater experiment conducted since 2020 at the Grimsel Test Site in Switzerland
 (see Section 3.3.1 for a description of the HotBENT Project). With a maximum temperature of
 200°C at the heater-bentonite interface, the experiment targets the temperature range expected
 when directly disposing of large-capacity DPCs in a geologic repository. To ensure the
 performance and safety of such repository designs, it is paramount to demonstrate that such
 strongly elevated temperatures do not impact the engineered barrier system and near-field
 environment.
 Objectives: To examine whether a bentonite barrier can withstand temperatures up to 200°C
 and to assess if significantly elevated temperatures cause changes that compromise the
 functions of both engineered and natural barriers.
 Associated Disposal R&D Campaign Milestone Report: Sections 5, 6 and 8 in Zheng et al.
 (2025a), Engineered Barrier System R&D, LBNL Report, SF-25LB010308032.
 Summary:
 DOE scientists continued participating in the HotBENT Project, with focus on interpreting and
 modeling the full-scale HotBENT field test and comparing simulation results with the
 experimental data. To support the operation of the field test and the interpretation and
 understanding of data from the field test, LBNL conducted and analyzed a series of laboratory-
 scale experiments, named HotBENT Lab.
 • Modeling the THMC Evolution of the HotBENT Field Test
 The HotBENT field test continued operating at the design temperature of 175° and 200°C.
 Data were collected through the extensive sensor network, and data from different sectors
 were shared with the partners (Section 3.3.1.2). LBNL has actively participated in the
 HotBENT Modeling Platform and in previous years built 2D and 3D TH models for the
 HotBENT Field Test. In FY25, the modeling focus was on developing a 3D THM model by
 applying the TOUGH-FLAC simulator, which integrates the TOUGH multiphase flow and
 heat transport simulator with the FLAC3D geomechanical code. The thermal processes
 considered are heat conduction and convection, the hydrological processes are represented
 by a two-phase flow model, and the mechanical processes are described by an elastic
 model using a state-surface approach.
 LBNL’s 3D THM model results align well with observed trends in thermal and hydrodynamic
 behavior in the EBS (Section 8 in Zheng et al., 2025a). The model initially underestimated

December 5, 2025                                                                                         126

 the measured temperature increases, especially in sectors surrounding the heaters with
 bentonite fill. However, after updating the model with new data on thermal conductivities,
 diffusion coefficients and water retention curves of the bentonite, the thermal and hydration
 behavior within the buffer was much better captured. Porosity was not measured in the field
 test, but the 3D THM model shows how it behaves: In both the granular bentonite and
 bentonite blocks, porosity remains stable during early stages near the heater. Once heating
 begins, a decrease in porosity occurs, more prominently in the bentonite blocks. As water
 infiltrates the buffer from the host rock, the porosity of the granular bentonite increases over
 time. Conversely, the porosity decreases less significantly near the bentonite-rock interface
 compared to near the heater, due to effect of infiltration from the rock and the lower
 temperatures. Continued hydration causes swelling in granular bentonite over time. The
 model suggests no major changes in porosity in end sections of the experiments aside from
 minor swelling near the host rock interface. This behavior aligns with the patterns of low
 temperature increases and relative humidity (RH) in these regions. Overall, the combined
 effects of heating and hydration create spatial heterogeneity in porosity and, consequently,
 in the density distribution within the buffer.
 • Heating and Hydration Column Test of Bentonite – HotBENT Lab Experiments
 In addition to being the primary modeling group supporting the HotBENT field test, LBNL
 continued conducting and interpreting a series of bench-scale column tests that mimic
 closely the heating and hydration conditions of the field-scale experiment (Section 3.3.1.4).
 Significant effort was made in FY25 to interpret the electrical resistivity tomography (ERT)
 data from HotBENT Lab Tests #1 and #2 which had been previously conducted and
 dismantled. An ERT-based framework was investigated for effectively monitoring THM
 processes in bentonite buffers. LBNL was able to demonstrate the capability of ERT (a non-
 destructive geophysical method) to capture dynamic changes in resistivity in bentonite
 buffers resulting from variations in water content, temperature, and mechanical alterations
 (Section 5 in Zheng et al., 2025a).
 LBNL also attempted to develop a THM model to interpret the complex bentonite evolution
 exhibited in HotBENT Lab #2, utilizing a dual-porosity framework to represent the complex
 pore structure of bentonite mixtures and deploying the BExM model to capture the evolution
 of macro-porosity and swelling behavior (Section 6 in Zheng et al., 2025a). Verification tests
 demonstrate that this method successfully reproduces interactions between pore domains
 and provides accurate predictions of swelling pressure development. The new model will be
 used in FY26 to test whether the dual-porosity THM framework combined with BExM can
 explain the rapid hydration and the flat density gradient observed in the lab tests.
 In FY25, a new column experiment, HotBENT Lab #3, was initiated (Section 5 in Zheng et
 al., 2025a). The goals of this experiment are to: (1) use Time-Domain Reflectometry (TDR)
 probes and RH sensors to achieve better measurements of water content, and (2)
 implement a different column design, with heating from the top and hydration from the
 bottom. The HotBENT Lab #3 experimental portfolio involves both a non-heated column and
 a heated column. So far, the non-heated column has been running for two months, and the
 installation of the heated column has been completed. Early test results from the non-heated
 column show a quick increase in travel time during water injection and pressurization,
 followed by stable readings. ERT measurements were compared with TDR and RH
 measurements. The log-scaled ERT shows strong linear agreement with column-level
 saturation and RH values, and the correlation to both RH sensors is very similar, increasing
 confidence in using ERT to scale these variables up or down. In FY26, the nonheated and
 heated column tests will continue to run for several months before being dismantled.

December 5, 2025                                                                                             127

 Bentonite and pore water samples will be carefully extracted in the dismantling process and
 will be provided for more detailed physical and chemical analyses.

 6.1.3 MODELING THE FE EXPERIMENT IN OPALINUS CLAY AT THE MONT
 TERRI URL
 Background and International Coordination: The full-scale emplacement experiment (FE
 experiment) at the Mont Terri URL replicates the emplacement tunnel of Nagra’s reference
 repository design at a 1:1 scale as an ultimate test for the performance assessment of geologic
 disposal in Opalinus Clay (OPA). Mimicking the Swiss repository design, the temperature target
 for the FE Heater Test is moderate, with a temperature of 125°C to 135°C at the inner part of
 the buffer (near the heater) and less than 100°C in the argillite host rock. Monitoring data from
 about 10 years of heating have been used in international studies to validate numerical models
 for long-term THM performance predictions in argillaceous rocks, such as in DECOVALEX-2023
 Task C task, which allowed for model-to-model and model-to-data comparison of multiple
 approaches to simulating the THM response of the EBS and the host rock. In FY25, LBNL
 resumed its THM simulations for the FE Heater Test as part of the Nagra modeling task force.
 See more details on the FE Heater Test in Section 3.1.2 and on DECOVALEX-2023 Task C in
 Section 3.2.2.3 of this report.
 Objective: To examine the evolution of EBS and host rock behavior at full emplacement scale
 affected by thermal effects, engineering factors (e.g., shotcrete, tunnel shape), and damage
 caused by tunnel construction, and to validate predictive THM models using more than 10 years
 of data from the FE Experiment.
 Associated Disposal R&D Campaign Milestone Reports: Section 3 in Rutqvist et al. (2025),
 Investigation of Coupled Processes in Argillite Rock: FY25, LBNL Report, M3SF-
       25LB010301031, Report No. LBNL-2001702.
 Summary:
 In FY25, the DECOVALEX-2023 model comparisons on the Mont Terri FE experiment were
 completed with the synthesis journal article that was published in March 2025 (Graupner et al.,
 2025). Researchers from LBNL and SNL participated in Task C together with eight other
 international modeling groups. A blind prediction exercise was conducted by eight modeling
 teams predicting the THM response of the FE test to a temporary thermal power increase
 implemented after about 9 years of heating (Section 3 in Rutqvist et al., 2025). The modeling of
 this test and the prediction of future response of 6,000 days (about 16 years) was conducted
 with the THM models that had been validated against field data from the first 5 years of the
 heater test within the DECOVALEX-2023 project. Overall, the teams were able to model the
 THM coupled processes relevant to the FE experiment and successfully reproduced the data.
 Those results for the FE experiment were only achieved due to the rather extensive model
 comparison beforehand. This underlines the value of well-constrained benchmarking studies
 when comparing complex process model implementations. Confidence in long-term predictions
 for disposal systems is built by using a group of modeling teams, codes, and approaches, as the
 predictions of an ensemble of models will capture model uncertainties better than relying on a
 single modeling team.
 Overall, modeling the FE experiment and comparison with the monitoring data confirms that the
 understanding of coupled THM processes in an argillite repository system is accurate. The
 results also indicate that damage caused in the rock by excavation can significantly impact the
 increase in pore pressure due to heating and its subsequent dissipation. The properties and
 extent of damage in the argillite host rock around tunnels will be different for high-temperature
 disposal designs, which will require more detailed future work.

December 5, 2025                                                                                           128

 6.1.4 REPOSITORY-SCALE THM MODEL TESTING IN THE INTERNATIONAL
 BENVASIM PROJECT
 Background and International Coordination: The international BenVaSim project is an
 initiative focused on benchmarking and validating numerical simulators for THM coupled
 processes relevant to deep geological repositories for heat-emanating radioactive waste. It
 involves international partners from the USA, Germany, and Switzerland. The project aims to
 increase confidence in the results of the THM simulations by comparing them across different
 models and a standardized set of benchmarks. In FY25, LBNL participated in an argillite
 repository benchmarking exercise until LBNL’s involvement was discontinued in March 2025
 due to changes in DOE priorities.
 Associated Disposal R&D Campaign Milestone Reports: Section 2 in Rutqvist et al. (2025),
 Investigation of Coupled Processes in Argillite Rock: FY25, LBNL Report, M3SF-
       24LB010301031, Report No. LBNL-2001702.
 Objective: Perform comparative 3D modeling of coupled THM models to test an advanced
 temperature-dependent soil water retention curve (SWRC) method that can consider both
 capillarity and adsorption mechanisms processes.
 Summary:
 2D and 3D numerical modeling was performed in FY25 to compare results from LBNL (using
 TOUGH4-FLAC) and TU Clausthal, TUC (TOUGH3-FLAC. The modeling benchmark considers
 a simplified argillite repository setting with bentonite backfilled repository tunnels. The simulation
 results show excellent agreement across all variables, confirming the ability of the TOUGH4–
 FLAC framework to reliably capture THM responses in the horizontal direction. The comparison
 was based on simulated liquid saturation, gas pressure, pore water pressure, and temperature.
 The new temperature-dependent SWRC model provides superior performance in simulating the
 dehydration behavior of bentonite under thermal loading. This improvement is attributed to the
 ability of the new model to simultaneously capture both capillarity and adsorption mechanisms.

 6.1.5 KEY ACCOMPLISHMENTS
 • Collaborations with the international HotBENT project include experimental and modeling
 studies to understand how temperature-dependent disturbances and modifications in
 bentonite affect its use as an engineered barrier material at temperatures approaching
 200°C. These activities have provided valuable insights into how repository performance
 might be impacted by these disturbances and changes.
 • 3D THM modeling of the HotBENT field experiment shows that the simulation results match
 the observed temperature increase and relative humidity in the field. Overall, simulations
 confirm the combined effects of heating and hydration on the spatial variability of porosity,
 affecting the density distribution within the buffer. The 3D THM model, successfully tested
 against HotBENT field test data, can now be used to predict the long-term and large-scale
 temperature behavior of a high-temperature repository.
 • A series of benchtop-scale high-temperature column experiments was conducted to support
 the HotBENT field test. Building on lessons from previous HotBENT Lab Tests #1 and #2, a
 new column experiment, HotBENT Lab #3, started in FY25, aiming to assist in the
 calibration of TDR data at the field test to assess hydration status.
 • The full-scale FE heater test at Mont Terri, now more than ten years into its heating phase
 with temperatures up to about 130°C near the heater, continues to provide valuable THM
 data at relevant spatial and temporal scales. Under the umbrella of Task C of the

December 5, 2025                                                                                                129

 DECOVALEX-2023 project, DOE researchers and several international partners finalized
 their interpretative and comparative THM simulations for model validation. Based on a
 comprehensive sensitivity study, the teams were able to achieve an excellent overall match
 with the measured pore pressure changes in the host rock, demonstrating that reliable
 models exist for the long-term and large-scale temperature behavior of a moderate-
 temperature repository in argillite host rock.

 6.2 EXPERIMENTAL AND MODELING STUDIES OF ENGINEERED BARRIER
 AND HOST ROCK INTERACTIONS

 6.2.1 INTRODUCTIONS
 This section summarizes DOE’s international research activities aimed at enhancing
 understanding of physical and geochemical interactions between EBS and host rock materials.
 Material changes near these interfaces can significantly influence repository performance,
 affecting the flow and transport of radionuclides through engineered barriers into the
 surrounding natural rock. Interfacial reactions are most significant early in a repository's life
 when EBS materials, like bentonite, metal containers, and cementitious materials, have just
 been placed, and thermal-hydrological-mechanical-chemical disturbances are still strong. The
 experimental and modeling studies described here seek to provide insights into how interfacial
 processes and alterations, such as those between different host rocks and EBS materials, might
 impact repository performance in the long term.
 Section 6.2.2 presents reactive transport modeling results to interpret the evolution of the in-situ
 CI-D diffusion experiment at the Mont Terri URL, which examines how diffusive transport across
 the interfaces can be impacted by the interaction between ordinary Portland cement (OPC),
 bentonite, and Opalinus Clay. Section 6.2.3 summarizes the reactive-transport benchmarking
 studies carried out as part of Task 12 of the SKB EBS Task Force. The objective is to compare
 reactive-transport models for predicting long-term interactions between bentonite and cement
 barrier materials and understanding their impact on barrier repository performance.

 6.2.2 MODELING AND THE CI-D EXPERIMENT AT THE MONT TERRI URL
 Background and International Coordination: The CI-D experiment at the Mont Terri URL
 examines the long-term (>15 years) interactions among cement, bentonite, and Opalinus Clay
 (see Section 3.1.4). LBNL’s model predicts the hydro-chemical processes during the Cl-D
 Experiment using the code CrunchClay, a recent development within the CrunchFlow family of
 simulators specifically designed for reactive transport in clay-based materials (Steefel et al.,
 2015).
 Objective: The aim of this work is to develop a predictive understanding of the coupled
 processes governing the evolution of the Cement-Clay Interaction (CI-D) Experiment at Mont
 Terri, focusing on understanding the reaction-induced porosity, permeability, and diffusivity
 changes evolving at interfaces between bentonite, cement and Opalinus Clay.
 Associated Disposal R&D Campaign Milestone Report: Section 9 in Rutqvist et al. (2025),
 Investigation of Coupled Processes in Argillite Rock: FY25, LBNL Report, M3SF-
       25LB010301031, Report No. LBNL-2001702.
 Summary:
 A diffusion test was conducted in 2019 to evaluate how tracers would migrate across CI-D
 interfaces. A water cocktail with radionuclide tracers was released in a slanted pilot borehole
 drilled across the vertical borehole in a cement/concrete section. The tracer cocktail has since

December 5, 2025                                                                                              130

 been allowed to migrate outwards mainly by diffusion across the interface and into the argillite
 host rock. Simulations of the CI-D diffusion test at Mont Terri have been performed using 1D
 radialsymmetric, 2D axisymmetric, and more recently 3D full dual-continuum numerical models
 using LBNL’s CrunchClay software, which considers electrostatic effects in the Opalinus Clay
 with complex electrical double layer models (Tournassat and Steefel, 2019; Tournassat et al.,
 2020; Steefel and Tournassat, 2021). The reactive transport simulations conducted in FY25
 account for both the complex 3D geometry and the anisotropy of the geological formation. The
 high resolution of the model enabled the inclusion of “skin effects” developed at the interface
 between highly reactive materials, specifically between the high-pH cement and the
 circumneutral but electrostatic Opalinus Clay. The model suggested that the tracer plume is
 primarily confined within the cement during the first 450 days, while after 450 days the
 anisotropy of the Opalinus Clay and the negative net charge of the clay matrix begins to impact
 the charged solute’s transport. The simulated evolution of the tracer concentrations in the
 borehole over 900 days was in good agreement with the experimental results. This successful
 history matching with the field experiment demonstrates that the distinct geochemical and
 physical properties of the cement and the Opalinus Clay in the field experiment can be
 represented accurately. Such analyses are crucial for establishing a solid safety case for
 underground radioactive waste storage.

 6.2.3 REACTIVE-TRANSPORT MODELING OF CEMENT-BENTONITE
 INTERACTIONS: SKB EBS TASK FORCE
 Background and International Coordination: Task 12 of the SKB EBS Task Force involves
 long-term reactive transport modeling of cement-bentonite interactions for a stylized benchmark
 problem, considering mineralogical, chemical, and textural changes near the interface.
 Collaborative modeling results are compared between four international modeling teams: SKB,
 SURAO, LBNL, and SNL. Section 3.4.3.2 of this report provides more details on Task 12 of the
 EBS Task Force.
 Objective: Evaluate and test reactive-transport models to represent long-term interactions of
 bentonite and cement barrier materials using a benchmark problem with different levels of
 complexity. Comparison between international teams allows understanding the relevance of
 conceptual modeling choices, such as selection of the thermodynamic database, kinetic
 reaction rate, selection of aqueous complexes, and choice of secondary minerals.
 Associated Disposal R&D Campaign Milestone Reports: (a) Part 1, Section 4 in Jové-Colón
 et al. (2025), Evaluation of Argillite Host Rock Properties & Processes (FY25), SNL Report,
 M2SF-26SN010203032, and (b) Section 7 in Zheng et al. (2025a), Engineered Barrier System
       R&D, LBNL Report, SF-25LB01030803.
 Summary:
 Task 12 was divided into three subtasks: Subtask A models the interaction between ordinary
 Portland cement (OPC) and MX-80 bentonite under fully saturated conditions. Subtask B
 examines the interaction between low pH concrete (LPC) and MX-80. Subtask C focuses on the
 interaction between OPC and MX-80 (like Subtask A) but assumes different initial saturation
 conditions: Here, the bentonite is initially unsaturated and is hydrated by the cement pore water
 through hydraulic and capillary pressure gradients. For some benchmark simulations, the
 subtasks were further divided into three variants, each with different initial pore water
 compositions in the cement (e.g., referred to as Subtasks A1, A2, and A3).
 Researchers from SNL and LBNL participated in this modeling task together with two other
 international modeling groups from SKB and SÚRAO, each using their own reactive transport
 simulators and making their own conceptual modeling choices. Both U.S. Teams finalized their

December 5, 2025                                                                                           131

 simulation work on Subtasks A, B and C in FY25. Despite differences in some aspects of the
 model setup, such as reaction rate and diffusion coefficient, all international models are
 qualitatively consistent in mineral dissolution and precipitation except the SNL model which
 predicts a broader impacted area around the interface and little mineral dissolution/precipitation,
 possibly because of higher diffusivities or dispersivities used in the model. In contrast, the SKB,
 SÚRAO and LBNL models suggest that the area affected by dissolution and precipitation is
 constrained to a few centimeters around the interface. The task will no longer continue under
 the SKB EBS Task Force and all activities have essentially concluded at the end of FY25.
 Below are more detailed summaries of SNL’s and LBNL’s findings:
 • SNL Reactive-Transport Modeling of Cement-Bentonite Interactions
 The FY25 simulations built on SNL’s initial modeling work reported in FY24 (Jové-Colón and
 Lopez, 2024). The modeling studies comprise a rectangular domain of fully saturated MX-80
 bentonite and cement, interacting for 100,000 years. The domains are isothermal at 25°C
 and held at constant atmospheric pressure. Modeling was conducted using PFLOTRAN
 (Lichtner et al., 2019), with the application of the THERMODDEM database (Blanc, 2017;
 Blanc et al., 2012) and the addition of specific cement phases from CEMDATA18
 (Lothenbach et al., 2019).
 Subtask A (OPC and MX-80): The PFLOTRAN 1-D reactive-transport model of cement-
 bentonite interactions reveals significant changes in spatial-temporal concentration profiles
 for Ca and Si at early times near the interface. The pore solution chemistry in the domain
 with the highest saturated porosity (bentonite) influences the diffusive fluxes between
 domains and the long-term evolution of pore solution chemical composition across all
 domains. Gypsum is the most active solid in the simulations, especially in the interface
 region, while ettringite becomes saturated in the OPC region. The presence of cation
 exchange in both the OPC and bentonite has a minimal impact on the resulting spatio-
 temporal solute profiles.
 Subtask B (LPC and MX-80): Like in Subtask A, significant variations in Ca and Si
 concentrations were observed for up to approximately 100 years. After this period, the
 concentrations of these solutes stabilize to levels consistent with the bentonite pore
 solutions. The pH evolution in the low-pH cement (LPC) domain differs markedly from that in
 the ordinary Portland cement (OPC) domain in subtask A; after a substantial increase in the
 early stages, the pH subsequently decreases to near-neutral values over time. As in
 Subtask A, the pore solution chemistry in the domain with the highest saturated porosity
 influences both diffusive fluxes and the long-term chemical behavior of the pore solution.
 Changes in porosity were relatively larger than those observed in Subtask A, although they
 were not considered significant.
 Subtask C (OPC and unsaturated MX-80): The initially unsaturated bentonite region
 achieves saturation in approximately 5 years due to a saturation front moving away from the
 OPC interface. In previous simulations with much lower initial saturations, the bentonite took
 longer to reach full saturation. The migrating saturation front in the bentonite region governs
 the changes in pore solution chemistry within this domain. Like Subtasks A and B, most
 changes in pore solution chemistry (including Ca, Si, and pH) occur near the interface,
 where the solute concentration gradient is the steepest. The pH in the bentonite region
 increases significantly as the domain saturates over time, closely following the changes
 brought about by the advancing liquid saturation front. Ultimately, the total concentrations of
 Ca and Si reach levels comparable to those in the OPC region.

December 5, 2025                                                                                             132

 • LBNL Reactive Transport Modeling of the Mineral Alterations at the Cement-Bentonite
 Interface
 LBNL set up a 1D TOUGHREACT model for the Task 12 modeling challenge and in FY24
 finalized simulations for Subtask A and B (Zheng et al., 2024). In FY25, the LBNL team used
 the same model setup to complete the simulations for Subtasks C1, C2, and C3 (OPC and
 unsaturated MX-80). Simulation results showed that, unlike in Subtask A where diffusive flux
 dominates, advective flux from OPC to MX-80 becomes the main transport process
 alongside diffusion in the early stages. This causes a larger area of mineral alteration
 (dissolution) in the MX-80 bentonite, resulting in a wider zone with slightly increased
 porosity, while mineral alterations (precipitation) in the OPC stays concentrated near the
 interface. On the OPC side, porosity drops to nearly zero within 3-5 cm from the interface,
 whereas in MX-80, porosity increases by a modest 20%. This pore clogging in OPC near the
 interface, where porosity nears zero, reduces both advective and diffusive fluxes and
 effectively prevents further mass exchange with MX-80. The modeling results for Subtask C
 showed no sensitivity to the initial porewater composition, since outcomes of Subtasks C1,
 C2, and C3 converged to similar results.
 In comparison to Subtask A, Subtask C demonstrates a distinct transport process. Instead
 of being dominated by diffusive flux as in Subtask A, advective flux from OPC to MX-80
 becomes the primary transport mechanism, alongside diffusive flux in the early stages. This
 difference results in a broader area of mineral alteration in the MX-80 bentonite, leading to a
 wider area with porosity increases that are modest in magnitude. However, the spatial
 extent of mineral alteration in OPC remains similar in both Subtasks A and C. Regarding
 implications for the safety of the repository, the models indicate that the reduction of porosity
 to nearly zero in OPC adjacent to the interface halts mass exchange between OPC and MX-
 80. In the event of radionuclide migration, this suggests that radionuclides cannot escape
 from the bentonite buffer, significantly enhancing the safety of the repository.

 6.2.4 KEY ACCOMPLISHMENTS
 • Reactive transport simulations were conducted to interpret interfacial reactions and their
 impact across aged bentonite, concrete/cement, and clay rock interfaces. These activities
 were aligned with (1) the international CI-D experiment, which probes diffusive transport
 across materials that were emplaced more than 15 years ago in a borehole at the Mont Terri
 URL, and (2) the reactive-transport modeling of long-term cement-bentonite interaction as
 part of the SKB EBS Task Force Task 12 benchmarking activities. These experimental and
 modeling studies provide valuable insights interfacial processes and alterations and how
 they might impact repository performance in the long term.
 • High-resolution 3-D simulations of the CI-D diffusion test at Mont Terri considered both the
 complex 3D geometry and the anisotropy of the geological formation. The high resolution of
 the model enabled the inclusion of “skin effects” developed at the interface between two
 highly reactive materials, the high-pH cement and the circumneutral but electrostatic
 Opalinus Clay. With these model improvements made, the simulations results were in good
 agreement with distinct geochemical and physical properties of the cement and the Opalinus
 Clay in the field experiment. Such analyses are crucial for establishing for long-term
 predictions of barrier performance.
 • The reactive-transport benchmarking exercise for interfacial reactions conducted under the
 umbrella of SKB’s EBS Task Force showed good qualitative consistency in mineral
 dissolution and precipitation behavior between four international models, despite differences
 in some aspects of the model setup. Of particular importance are results for a modeling

December 5, 2025                                                                                              133

 benchmark where advective and diffusive processes control interfacial processes between
 cement and bentonite. Modeling results suggest that a strong reduction in cement porosity
 near the interface effectively prevents any further mass exchange between the two
 engineered barrier materials. If confirmed, this “precipitation” barrier in the bentonite would
 improve the long-term safety of a repository as radionuclides cannot easily escape from the
 bentonite buffer.

 6.3 REPOSITORY-INDUCED PERTURBATIONS IN ARGILLITE HOST ROCK

 6.3.1 INTRODUCTION
 This section summarizes DOE’s international research activities aimed at enhancing
 understanding repository-induced perturbations that are important for the long-term
 performance of low-permeability argillite host rocks. This is motivated by the recognition that the
 argillites are a highly effective natural barrier to radionuclide migration in argillaceous rocks, as
 long as repository-induced perturbations do not affect the integrity of the barrier.
 One potential concern is that gas production in a repository could lead to pressure increases in
 the low-permeability barriers that in turn could induce rock deformation and possibly damage. In
 repositories hosted in argillaceous formations, the host rock plays a critical role in retarding
 radionuclide transport through its low permeability, low diffusivity, and sorption capacity.
 However, argillaceous rocks are mechanically weaker than crystalline rocks and are susceptible
 to damage. The DECOVALEX-2027 Task referred to as HyMAR (Hydro-Mechanics of
 Argillaceous Rocks) investigates the processes and mechanisms that govern the damage,
 deformation, and dilatancy behavior of argillaceous rocks and the influence of this behavior at
 relevant repository scales (Section 3.2.3.2). The task utilizes three complementary experimental
 datasets from micro- to field-scale, all using Opalinus Clay: (1) Different laboratory mechanical
 tests combined with microscopic analyses of damage and dilatancy, (2) Gas injection laboratory
 tests parallel and perpendicular to bedding, and (3) the GT field experiment at Mont Terri with
 gas injection and monitoring of pore pressure and deformation (Section 3.1.6.2). Section 6.3.2
 below summarizes FY25 activities conducted in the context of HyMAR modeling by DOE
 scientists.
 Repository-induced effects may also result in the reactivation of pre-existing faults within the
 host rock, which in turn may cause the formation of permeable transport pathways through the
 otherwise low-permeability argillite. Understanding such reactivation and its short- to long-term
 effects is critical in assessing the performance of radioactive waste repositories in clay
 formations. Section 6.3.3 summarizes international collaboration activities where DOE scientists
 monitor and simulate the hydromechanical behavior of faulted argillite host rock affected by pore
 pressure perturbations. These activities are related to a series of highly instrumented controlled-
 injection fault activation experiments at the Mont Terri URL in Switzerland (Section 3.1.3). FY25
 activities have focused on the mechanisms that lead to long-term sealing of the fault after
 activation.

 6.3.2 MODELING GAS MIGRATION IN ARGILLITE HOST ROCK: DECOVALEX-
 2027 HYMAR TASK
 Background and International Coordination: LBNL’s scientists participated in the
 DECOVALEX-2027 HyMAR Task on gas migration in argillite host rock, using laboratory
 experiments performed on Opalinus Clay and field data from the GT Experiment at the Mont
 Terri URL in Switzerland. The task aims to improve understanding of processes and
 mechanisms governing the advective movement of gas in argillaceous clays, improve physics-

December 5, 2025                                                                                               134

 based models of these systems, and support the development of performance and safety
 assessment models.
 Objective: The goal of HyMAR is to collaboratively develop new constitutive models of gas
 migration in argillaceous rocks and how it is affected by damage, deformation, and dilatancy
 behavior. The new models are then to be tested against laboratory- and field-scale gas injection
 tests in Opalinus Clay.
 Associated Disposal R&D Campaign Milestone Report: Section 4 in Rutqvist et al. (2025),
 Investigation of Coupled Processes in Argillite Rock: FY25 Progress, LBNL Report, M3SF-
       25LB010301031.
 Summary:
 Like other international participants in HyMAR, the LBNL team started in FY25 with the
 development of new constitutive mechanical models that can adequately describe the
 hydromechanics involved in gas migration through clay materials that may undergo plastic
 deformation. LBNL decided to test a new formulation based on a smoothed Mohr-Coulomb-
 Rankine (MCR) model for transversely isotropic Opalinus Clay. The smoothness of the model is
 respected in two aspects: (1) sharp corners of both yield curves in the deviatoric plane are
 removed by introducing a William- Warnke model; (2) the Mohr-Coulomb (MC) criterion is
 combined with a Rankine model with a smooth function. As a result, the developed model can
 simulate both shear and tensile failure, as well as hardening and softening effects governed by
 accumulated plastic strains.
 Comparisons with experimental uniaxial compression tests (UCS) results showed that the
 model can effectively reproduce the anisotropy of strength caused by bedding orientation. The
 simulated data closely matched the measured results, especially in representing the orientation-
 dependent shear strengths. Simulations of Brazilian tensile tests (BTS) indicated that the
 inclination and size of the failure zone are strongly influenced by the dip angle. Discrepancies
 observed in the simulation of consolidation undrained triaxial tests (UT) may result from
 uncertainties in estimating HM properties experimentally and from simplifications in the current
 model, such as how confining pressure effects are handled. The proposed smoothed MCR
 model offers a solid framework for simulating the anisotropic HM behavior of bedded
 geomaterials. LBNL’s modeling results demonstrated excellent agreement with the three
 complementary datasets from UCS, BTS, and UT.
 The next steps in HyMAR would be to apply the new constitutive model to simulate the
 laboratory gas migration tests followed by application to the GT field experiment. However, DOE
 decided in September 2025 to end its involvement in HyMAR, a result of the recent realignment
 of DOE’s disposal R&D priorities. LBNL thus needed to discontinue its HyMAR modeling.

 6.3.3 ACTIVATION AND SELF-SEALING OF FAULTS IN ARGILLITE HOST
 ROCK AT THE MONT TERRI URL
 Background and International Coordination: Since 2015, several fault activation experiments
 have been conducted to study the HM behavior of the Main Fault crossing the Mont Terri URL in
 Switzerland. These activities, including fieldwork and modeling, have involved several
 international partners in the Mont Terri Project. These partners provide both funding and
 expertise to support the experiments. For more information on the fault activation experiments,
 see Section 3.1.3 of this report.
 Objective: Use field observations to assess the long-term self-sealing processes of activated
 faults in argillite host rock at the Mont Terri URL by analyzing the Mont Terri fault zone geology
 and fault activation mechanisms, as well as applying new monitoring techniques.

December 5, 2025                                                                                            135

 Associated Disposal R&D Campaign Milestone Report: Section 8 in Rutqvist et al. (2025),
 Investigation of Coupled Processes in Argillite Rock: FY25, LBNL Report, M3SF-
       25LB010301031.
 Summary:
 Since 2015, LBNL scientists have conducted a series of fault activation experiments at the Mont
 Terri URL, which demonstrate the potential for significant permeability increases during fault
 activation and rupture along initially low-permeability faults in clay host rock (Guglielmi et al.,
 2020; Cappa et al., 2022). The experiments simulated fault reactivation under realistic
 geological conditions through pressure or flow-controlled fluid injections directly into the fault
 zone. All experiments showed a rapid rise in fault transmissivity by several orders of magnitude
 during activation. Follow-up observations after the injections revealed a gradual transmissivity
 decrease by about three orders of magnitude, with long-term fault creep competing with
 secondary processes, especially clay mineral swelling. A key aspect of this research is the use
 of advanced active and passive seismic imaging techniques combined with local borehole
 measurements to observe fault HM processes before, during, and after injections (Guglielmi et
       al., 2025; Shadoan et al., 2023).
 In FY25, LBNL scientists finalized their analysis and interpretative modeling of these
 experiments and summarized important findings, focusing specifically on hydromechanical fault
 activation mechanisms and self-sealing in the clay formation:
 • The fault activation observations revealed a high sensitivity to relatively small changes in
 pore pressure and stress. Not only did the injections induced a strong increase in fault
 permeability, but they also caused microseismic activity and fault slip. Fault displacements
 ranged from micrometers to millimeters, and the microseismic events had limited spatial
 extents. Although minor, these events demonstrate that faults in clay rocks can respond
 dynamically even to relatively low-pressure fluid perturbations.
 • Investigations also revealed a complex mixture of fault self-sealing mechanisms over
 extended periods. After a few hours to days of fault activation, the fault already showed
 signs of self-sealing as fluid pressures decreased (Guglielmi et al., 2025). This initial drop
 was followed by slow creep closure over months, and eventually, very slow closure due to
 swelling over years. All conceptual models employed to interpret the field data converge on
 estimating that the fault's return to its initial low transmissivity state would require a minimum
 of about 50 years. This finding holds significance in the context of radioactive waste
 repositories in clay host rock.
 The fault activation experiments and their analysis underscore the importance of investigating
 the impact of fault activation and sealing processes on repository performance, using complex
 hydromechanical models that have been tested against data from field experiments like the
 ones conducted at Mont Terri.
 In FY25, LBNL scientists also initiated planning and design of a new Mont Terri fault activation
 experiment to induce controlled thermal stimulation (Fault Heating Experiment). As described in
 Section 3.1.3, the planned setup will include a borehole placed across the fault zone about 40
 meters below the URL’s galleries. The temperature will be gradually increased to around 110°C,
 a safe limit for monitoring tools and an upper limit for the expected temperature in the host rock
 near emplacement tunnels. The primary goal of the Fault Heating Experiment is to induce
 THM-related failure in the fault over several to tens of meters, representing an intermediate
 scale between laboratory and industrial levels. The experiment will the risk of losing natural
 barrier integrity due to thermal stressing of faults, a topic becoming increasingly important with
 higher emplacement temperatures.

December 5, 2025                                                                                               136

 6.3.4 KEY ACCOMPLISHMENTS
 • In the context of the DECOVALEX-2027 HyMAR task, LBNL scientists developed a new
 constitutive mechanical model based on a smoothed Mohr-Coulomb-Rankine (MCR)
 approach that can adequately describe the hydromechanics involved in gas migration
 through clay materials that may undergo plastic deformation. The new model was
 successfully applied to mechanical laboratory experiments combined with microscopic
 analyses of damage and dilatancy. Pending further testing against gas migration
 experiments, the new MCR model offers a solid framework for simulating the anisotropic HM
 behavior of bedded argillaceous rocks.
 • DOE scientists advanced the data and modeling analysis of the Mont Terri fault reactivation
 experiments to explore the ability of reactivated faults to self-seal over time. The integration
 of in-situ experiments and modeling revealed several key mechanisms contributing to the
 observed self-sealing phenomena following the activation of a shale fault. The fault rupture
 occurred mainly without seismic activity, and several months after activation, the fault nearly
 sealed itself to almost zero permeability, but not entirely. Time estimates for the full return of
 a fault to its initial small transmissivity project a minimum of 50 years.
 • Design planning was initiated for a new thermal fault activation experiment to address the
 risk of losing natural barrier integrity due to thermal stresses, a topic becoming increasingly
 important with higher emplacement temperatures. As currently envisioned, the Fault Heating
 Experiment will be conducted in late FY26 in the same fault testbed used for isothermal
 testing, benefitting from the already installed multi-modal monitoring network.

 6.4 HYDROMECHANICS, FLUID FLOW, AND TRANSPORT IN FRACTURED
 CRYSTALLINE ROCK

 6.4.1 INTRODUCTION
 Research on crystalline rock formations as host rocks for radioactive waste disposal has been
 conducted worldwide (e.g., Sweden, Korea, Japan, Canada, China) and is available on several
 URLs, such as the Äspö HRL in Sweden, Olkiluoto in Finland, and GTS in Switzerland. The
 most advantageous features of crystalline/granite rock for radioactive waste isolation include
 mechanical stability, low permeability, and high thermal conductivity. However, because
 crystalline formations are often fractured, repository performance assessment studies need to
 understand (1) the characteristics of fracture networks and how they can be represented with
 fracture network models, (2) how stress changes and other disturbances impact these fracture
 networks through dilation, shearing, or fracturing processes, and (3) the large-scale transport
 patterns of radionuclides migrating through fracture networks from the repository to the
