 biosphere. DOE scientists conducted several activities in FY25 to address these needs, as
 briefly described below.
 Section 6.4.2 presents a HM phase-field modeling framework for analyzing fracturing processes
 in crystalline rocks. The model is validated under the umbrella of the DECOVALEX-2027
 SAFENET-2 task, by simulating two problems with known analytical solutions for static and
 propagating hydraulic fractures. Section 6.4.3 summarizes discrete fracture modeling activities
 conducted in the context of the DECOVALEX-2027 FRESCIP task which aims at predicting
 fracture geometries and groundwater inflows in crystalline rock settings at nuclear waste
 disposal sites. Section 6.4.4 describes the application of validation approaches for groundwater
 flow and transport modeling involving discrete fracture features, as outlined by Task 10 of the
 SKB Groundwater Flow and Transport Task Force. Finally, Section 6.4.5 summarizes the

December 5, 2025                                                                                               137

 development and testing of advanced characterization and monitoring techniques in cooperation
 with the Bedretto URL in Switzerland and the San Andreas Fault (SAF) testbed in California.

 6.4.2 MODELING OF HYDRAULIC FRACTURING PROCESSES IN
 CRYSTALLINE ROCK: DECOVALEX-2027 SAFENET-2 TASK
 Background and International Coordination: Comparative HM modeling of hydraulic
 fracturing benchmark problems was conducted under the umbrella of the SAFENET-2 task of
 the DECOVALEX-2027 initiative. See details on SAFENET-2 in Section 3.2.3.1 of this report.
 Objective: To validate a new phase-field model framework for hydrofracturing in crystalline
 rocks via comparison with two known analytical solutions for static and propagating hydraulic
 fractures.
 Associated Disposal R&D Campaign Milestone Report: Section 4 in Chang et al. (2025),
 Crystalline Disposal R&D at LBNL: FY25 Progress, LBNL Report, M4SF-25LB010302032.
 Summary:
 The DECOVALEX SAFENET-2 Task focuses on the development and understanding of models
 and codes for complex coupled fracture nucleation and evolution processes in crystalline rock.
 Such processes can significantly impact the properties of existing fractures and result in the
 creation of new fractures, thus affecting important repository safety functions. SAFENET-2
 brings multiple international scientists together to simulate laboratory experiments on granitic
 samples and upscale them to predict fracture propagation processes at the field scale. In FY25,
 LBNL scientists started working on the first step of the SAFENET-2 modeling challenge, to
 compare the selected modeling approach of each team with known analytical solutions.
 As further described in Chang et al. (2025), LBNL developed a HM phase-field model
 implemented in the MOOSE framework to analyze the propagation of hydraulic fractures. The
 HM phase-field method was coupled with a new algorithm for determining the crack normal
 vector. To validate the model, the team presented two benchmark examples to evaluate its
 performance when simulating both static and propagating hydraulic fractures. The good
 agreement between the model results and analytical solutions, especially for pore pressure
 evolution, demonstrates the accuracy of the phase-field model. A sensitivity analysis was then
 performed to identify key model parameters that control the accuracy of the phase-field method
 in simulating hydraulic fracturing problems.
 Due to its realignment of disposal research priorities, DOE decided in the Spring of 2025 to
 discontinue its participation in the DECOVALEX-2027 SAFENET-2 task. However, the new
 phase-field modeling capabilities developed in FY25 can be an important prediction method to
 evaluate fracturing processes in crystalline host rock in the near and far fields of nuclear
 repositories.

 6.4.3 PREDICTING FRACTURE GEOMETRIES AND GROUNDWATER INFLOWS
 IN FRACTURED CRYSTALLINE ROCK: DECOVALEX-2027 FRESCIP TASK
 Background and International Coordination: The DECOVALEX-2027 Fractured Rock
 Extrapolation, Suitability Criteria, and Inflow Prediction (FRESCIP) aims to improve modeling
 techniques, especially discrete fracture network (DFN) and channel network model (CNM)
 approaches, for predicting fracture geometries and groundwater inflows in crystalline rock
 settings at nuclear waste disposal sites. The project uses both synthetic and real fracture data
 from the Demonstration Area at Posiva’s ONKALO facility in Olkiluoto, Finland, with a focus on
 comparing conditional simulation methods against observed tunnel and borehole data. More
 details about FRESCIP task are provided in Section 3.2.3.6 of this report.

December 5, 2025                                                                                          138

 Objective: To develop and test alternative fracture network modeling concepts to match
 hydraulic measurements and fracture mapping from the Olkiluoto site. Use models to (1)
 determine suitable rock volumes for waste storage as as a function of fracture network
 characteristics and local stress/pressure/flow conditions, and (2) evaluate fluid flow conditions at
 deposition holes and tunnels to estimate potential radionuclide leakage from waste canisters
 into the geosphere.
 Associated Disposal R&D Campaign Milestone Reports: Section 2.2.3 in Hyman et al.
 (2025), Crystalline Host Rock Properties & Processes - LANL, LANL Report, M4SF-
 25LA010302012; Section 7 in Hyman (2025), dfnWorks Development in support of GDSA FY25,
       LANL Report, M4SF-25LA010304032.
 Summary:
 Organized into sequential steps, FRESCIP plans to progress from synthetic benchmarking
 through predictions of fracture characteristics in deposition tunnels, pilot holes, and deposition
 holes, culminating in assessing predictive capabilities for rock suitability and groundwater
 against actual data from ONKALO. Emphasis is placed on rigorous statistical evaluations to
 quantify model accuracy in predicting fracture orientations, spacings, intensities, and
 transmissivities, ultimately aiding decision-making processes regarding repository design,
 construction, and long-term performance assessment. LANL scientists are supporting the task
 and assisting international modeling groups that are using the discrete fracture simulator
 dfnWorks.
 To date, the FRESCIP task within DECOVALEX-2027 has completed initial benchmarking
 phases, which involved detailed comparisons between synthetic fracture network predictions
 and reference fracture datasets. Teams effectively used statistical analyses to validate
 predictions of fracture orientation, spacing, intensity, and transmissivity against known synthetic
 benchmarks. Several statistical methods have been thoroughly applied, demonstrating the
 feasibility and accuracy of conditional simulation methods under idealized conditions.
 Additionally, synthetic datasets that incorporate realistic measurement uncertainties and
 sampling errors were used to further test model robustness, enhancing the applicability of
 simulation techniques to real-world geological settings. By incorporating uncertainty and
 sampling errors into the synthetic benchmarks, FRESCIP has shown that DFN-based
 techniques are both robust and adaptable to realistic repository conditions.
 LANL has played a key leadership role within this effort by providing technical expertise, direct
 user support, and targeted development of dfnWorks for FRESCIP participants. Two
 international modeling teams from Germany, the BASE and DynaFrax teams, both relied on
 dfnWorks as their primary DFN simulator, and LANL guidance was essential in helping them
 overcome limitations such as the integration of deterministic fracture traces and the
 implementation of geometry–transmissivity relationships. These LANL-driven solutions enabled
 participants to merge deterministic and stochastic datasets effectively, producing higher-quality
 fracture hydraulics predictions. The use of dfnWorks within the FRESCIP modeling efforts also
 demonstrates the flexibility of the tool when paired with external preprocessing of deterministic
 data, allowing robust predictions and improved representation of fracture hydraulics.
 Looking forward, FRESCIP will focus on predictions of synthetic fracture intersections and
 groundwater inflows within deposition holes and their associated pilot holes. Teams are
 currently preparing for these more advanced simulations, which incorporate realistic boundary
 conditions and fracture configurations, and further expand the statistical validation framework to
 assess predictive accuracy.

December 5, 2025                                                                                              139

 6.4.4 VALIDATION APPROACHES FOR GROUNDWATER FLOW AND
 TRANSPORT MODELING INVOLVING DISCRETE FRACTURE FEATURES:
 SKB GWFTS TASK FORCE
 Background and International Coordination: Task 10 within SKB’s GWFTS Task Force (Section
 3.4.2) focuses on validation methods for groundwater flow and transport models that involve
 discrete fracture features, ultimately enhancing the reliability and credibility of predictions used
 in crystalline repository safety assessment. Bringing together international researchers from
 countries with interest in fractured crystalline host rock, the task has advanced through several
 subtasks, including detailed characterization of flow and geometry within single fractures,
 upscaling approaches (known as the “bridge task”), and practical validation methods applied to
 simple fracture networks.
 Objective: Evaluate modeling methodologies and approaches for flow and transport at the
 scale of single fractures and small fracture networks, emphasizing model validation and
 determining conceptual model uncertainty.
 Associated Disposal R&D Campaign Milestone Report: Section 2.3 in Hyman et al. (2025),
 Crystalline Host Rock Properties & Processes - LANL, LANL Report, M4SF-25LA010302012.
 Summary:
 Activities in FY25 have mainly focused on Task 10.2, which involves modeling geometry, flow,
 and transport within single fractures. Recent work included the development of innovative
 experimental methodologies, such as the use of 3D-printed fractures, enabling precise control of
 fracture geometries for laboratory tests. LANL scientists performed geostatistical analyses to
 characterize fracture surface roughness, primarily based on measurements from laser-scanned
 fracture samples. They preprocessed the fracture data onto regular grids to enable consistent
 statistical analysis and evaluated fracture roughness by calculating surface statistics (mean,
 variance, skewness, kurtosis), spatial correlation functions, and semi-variograms. Using
 different geostatistical methods, such as spherical variogram models, they assessed correlation
 lengths, the spatial structure of the roughness, and local curvature, providing insights into
 detailed roughness features. LANL’s results showed that the data resolution and quality were
 sufficient to characterize small-scale roughness; however, their study did not further explore
 uncertainties related to propagating geometric features into fracture aperture and flow modeling.
 Moving on from assessing geometric aspects of single fractures, the international teams started
 developing and applying a standardized workflow for pragmatic validation, with one report
 already published (Finsterle et al., 2024) and three additional reports nearing completion.
 Additionally, LANL made progress on Task 10.3, which focuses on the pragmatic validation of
 models at the small fracture network scale, leveraging extensive datasets from the TRUE Block
 Scale experiments at SKB’s Äspö Hard Rock Laboratory. Within this subtask, modeling groups
 have been tasked with predicting groundwater flow and transport processes based on limited
 and then progressively extended datasets, thereby testing the robustness and accuracy of their
 modeling approaches. The task also includes specific modeling efforts focusing on fracture
 intersection zones and small-scale fracture network compartmentalization.
 Looking ahead, Task 10.4 is anticipated to involve larger block-scale validation exercises,
 potentially incorporating real-world data from Posiva’s ONKALO facility or from other
 underground research facilities, thus bridging laboratory-scale insights with full-scale repository
 modeling scenarios, similar to the FRESCIP task in DECOVALEX-2027. This will however
 happen without LANL’s involvement. Due to its realignment of disposal research priorities, DOE
 decided to discontinue its participation in the SKB GWFTS Task Forces by the end of FY25

December 5, 2025                                                                                              140

 6.4.5 CHARACTERIZATION AND MONITORING OF HM FAULT BEHAVIOR IN
 CRYSTALLINE ROCK: COLLABORATION WITH NUMO AND ETH ZÜRICH
 Background and International Coordination: The long-term safety of deep geologic
 repositories in crystalline rock can be affected by hydromechanical property changes of faults
 and fractures intersecting the disposal tunnels. Such property changes may be caused by
 various dynamic stress perturbations, such as from distant earthquakes, pressure and stress
 changes, loading and unloading, or thermal fluctuations. LBNL has recently developed a new
 high-precision and high-accuracy borehole device called DOwnhole Robotic Stress/Strain
 Analyzer (DORSA) for long-term downhole hydromechanical monitoring of faults and fractures
 over an extensive range of frequencies and at micro-to-nanometer resolutions. DOE scientists
 have been evaluating how this new monitoring technology can support the long- term
 performance and safety assessment of deep geologic repositories in crystalline rock. To do so,
 DORSA sensors were installed at a depth of 70 meters in a borehole intersecting a fault zone in
 granitic rock near the SAF in California, in collaboration with the Japanese implementer NUMO
 (see Section 4.6). A similar system was set up at 1.5 km depth in a faulted granite in the URL
 (Switzerland), collaborating with Swiss researchers from ETH Zürich (see Section 4.4).
 Objectives: Demonstrate in relevant crystalline rock settings that the DORSA instrument can
 monitor fault and fracture property changes relevant to long-term repository integrity. Compare
 different workflows using DORSA to estimate fault permeability based on fluid pressure
 variations induced by pumping tests.
 Associated Disposal R&D Campaign Milestone Report: Section 3 in Chang et al. (2025),
 Crystalline Disposal R&D at LBNL: FY25 Progress, M4SF-25LB010302032.
 Summary:
 In FY25, LBNL extensively tested the application of the DORSA probe in two crystalline rock
 settings, one at a 70 m deep testbed located in gabbro rocks atop the main San Andreas Fault
 (SAF) zone, the other in 1.5 km deep fractured granite in the Bedretto URL in Switzerland.
 Results obtained to date demonstrate that the DORSA tool is effective in shallow and deep
 boreholes and can accurately monitor long-term hydromechanical property changes in faults
 and fractures. Different workflows were compared to estimate fault permeability and rock-
 specific storage variations based on fluid pressure changes induced by a series of pumping and
 water level recovery tests. Tests were also conducted to evaluate whether a combination of
 DORSA and fiber optics sensing can improve the characterization of the fault and fracture.
 It was shown that DORSA can detect visco-poroelastic signals even when housed inside a
 cased borehole, providing insights into the surrounding host rock properties. Due to its
 resolution and accuracy, it can monitor long-term changes at the infra-micrometer scale, even
 without being positioned across a well-defined fault or fracture. Fully coupled numerical
 modeling demonstrated that the DORSA response to small pumping tests can be used to
 identify fault and fracture zones, as well as the elastic properties of the bulk rock (such as
 Young's modulus, bulk modulus, and shear modulus) and hydraulic properties (like permeability
 and storage), along with their potential long-term variations.
 The properties derived from DORSA application are highly relevant for repository safety, as
 large fault displacements could potentially lead to shear stresses on waste canisters and
 fracture permeability increases could generate pathways for radionuclide transport. In addition,
 DORSA produces important data on hydromechanical properties of a fractured rock mass that
 feed predictive models of barrier integrity and performance.

December 5, 2025                                                                                          141

 6.4.6 KEY ACCOMPLISHMENTS
 • The SAFENET-2 task of the DECOVALEX-2027 initiative involves collaborative modeling of
 fracture nucleation and evolution processes from the laboratory scale to the field scale. In
 the first step of this modeling task, international scientists tested their respective modeling
 solutions for hydraulic fracturing processes against known benchmark tests. DOE scientists
 demonstrated the suitability of a new phase-field framework for hydraulic fracture generation
 and propagation.
 • Participating teams in the DECOVALEX-2027 FRESCIP task used statistical analyses to
 verify predictions of fracture orientation, spacing, intensity, and transmissivity against known
 synthetic benchmarks of complex fracture networks. Emphasis is placed on quantifying
 model accuracy and aiding decision-making processes regarding repository design,
 construction, and long-term performance assessment. LANL researchers support the
 FRESCIP task with the use of their powerful DFN software tool dfnWorks. The next step of
 FRESCIP will assess predictive capabilities for rock suitability and groundwater against
 actual data from the Demonstration Area at Posiva’s ONKALO facility in Olkiluoto, Finland.
 • Task 10 of the SKB GWFTS Task Force focuses on validation approaches for groundwater
 flow and transport modeling involving discrete fracture features. The participating groups,
 dealing with flow, geometry, and transport at the small fracture network scale, have
 developed and applying a standardized workflow for pragmatic validation, to be further
 tested against extensive datasets form the TRUE Block Scale experiments at SKB’s Äspö
 Hard Rock Laboratory.
 • The DOwnhole Robotic Stress/Strain Analyzer (DORSA) probe for long-term
 hydromechanical monitoring of faults and fractures, recently developed by DOE scientists,
 was tested in two crystalline rock testbeds made available by international collaboration with
 Japanese and Swiss programs. Enhanced with fiber optics monitoring, DORSA provided
 measurements of high relevance for repository safety assessments, as large fault
 displacements could potentially lead to shear stresses on waste canisters and fracture
 permeability increases could generate pathways for radionuclide transport.

 6.5 COUPLED PROCESSES, GEOMECHANICS, AND BRINE MIGRATION IN
 SALT ROCK

 6.5.1 INTRODUCTION
 The United States disposal research program has a long history of international collaboration on
 various R&D issues related to the disposal of radionuclide waste in rock salt formations. DOE
 scientists and their international counterparts have engaged in several cooperative initiatives
 and activities, including joint experiments, coupled-salt-mechanics modeling, and
 benchmarking. International collaboration has greatly improved our understanding of the
 complex mechanical, hydrological, thermal, and chemical processes that are expected in domal
 and bedded salt in response to repository-induced perturbations and has contributed to the
 performance and safety assessment of deep geologic repositories in salt. A high-level summary
 of the processes expected in a salt repository for disposal of heat-generating radioactive waste
 is given in Kuhlman and Mills (2025).
 At the beginning of FY25, scientists from by LANL, LBNL, and SNL continued with several
 ongoing salt-related research activities. These included international collaborations within the
 MEASURES project to further advance the understanding and modeling of crushed salt as an
 engineered barrier system and within the BenVaSim2 code comparison project for collaborative

December 5, 2025                                                                                             142

 modeling of coupled THM processes in salt repositories (Section 4.1.2). Continuing their earlier
 modeling work in DECOVALEX-2023, DOE scientists also coordinated in the BATS2 task in the
 DECOVALEX-2027 initiative and participated as modeling teams (Section 3.2.3.3). Lastly, U.S.
 salt researchers were involved in the Salt Club activities under the auspices of the NEA.
 In the Spring of 2025, DOE’s disposal research portfolio experienced a significant realignment
 of R&D priorities and scope, which resulted in the termination all salt-related disposal research
 work in May 2025, including SNL’s leadership of the BATS2 task which was transferred to
 German colleagues. Short close-out reports were written to document some of the activities
 conducted in FY25 (Kuhlman et al., 2025b; Rutqvist, 2025). Key accomplishments made in
 FY25 (until the work was terminated) are listed in Section 6.5.2 below.

 6.5.2 KEY ACCOMPLISHMENTS
 • The MEASURES project is a collaboration of German, Dutch, American, and British
 researchers seeking to improve thermo-hydro-mechanical models for crushed salt to be
 used as backfill for emplacement tunnels. DOE researchers worked on a new constitutive
 model for crushed salt featuring the following improvements: (1) a generalized grain
 geometry allowing for a consistent transition from porous to intact salt, (2) an anisotropic
 formulation capable of capturing deformation history, and (3) a new physicochemical model
 as an alternative to the phenomenological approach.
 • International collaboration within the BATS and BATS2 task of the international
 DECOVALEX project allowed DOE scientists to benefit from the engagement of
 international partners in the analysis and modeling of brine migration in heated salt. THM
 modeling conducted by the joint team of LBNL, SNL, and LANL researchers captured the
 main characteristics observed in the heater experiments at WIPP, including the dynamics of
 brine migration and damage in the rock salt during heating and cooling. This work helped
 improve understanding of the complex processes expected to occur in the EDZ of a salt
 repository for disposal of heat-generating radioactive waste and supports building the long-
 term repository safety case for radioactive waste disposal in salt.
 • Under the umbrella of the NEA Salt Club, DOE scientists and their international colleagues
 continued to develop a comprehensive FEPs database for salt host rock. They extended
 these efforts towards a generalized approach to scenario development for a high-level
 waste repository at a generic salt site.

 6.6 SEALING SYSTEMS FOR REPOSITORY TUNNELS AND SHAFTS

 6.6.1 INTRODUCTION
 In the FY23 update to its 5-year research plan (Sassani et al., 2023), the Disposal R&D
 Campaign identified the performance of sealing systems for repository tunnels and shafts as a
 priority theme for engineered barriers research. It was decided in FY24 to initially focus on shaft
 sealing systems and join the collaborative modeling of the Sandwich Experiment under the
 umbrella of the DECOVALEX-2027 initiative. Shaft sealing systems are designed to block
 potential pathways of radionuclides between a nuclear waste repository and the biosphere.
 Their role is to limit the fluid inflow from the adjacent rock or the surface in the early stage after
 closure of the repository and to delay the release of possibly contaminated fluids from the
 repository at a later stage. SNL started working on the Sandwich Experiment task of
 DECOVALEX-2027 in late FY24 and continued this work into FY25. However, the recent
 realignment of DOE’s disposal R&D priorities deemphasized research on sealing elements and
 all related activities were terminated in the Spring of 2025, including involvement in this task.

December 5, 2025                                                                                                143

 6.6.2 HM SIMULATION OF THE SANDWICH SHAFT SEALING EXPERIMENT:
 DECOVALEX-2027 SANDWICH TASK
 Background and International Coordination: The Sandwich Experiment at Mont Terri tests a
 newly developed shaft sealing concept that consists of alternating layers of bentonite and
 equipotential layers. The experiment is the key element of a modeling task within the
 DECOVALEX-2027 initiative which aims to improve the HM coupled process interactions
 between the host rock and the engineered shaft seal elements, supporting reliable performance
 and safety assessments (Section 3.2.3.4). As outlined in Friedenberg and Hinze (2024), the
 task involves four collaborative modeling steps: Before focusing on field experiment, teams will
 start with simulations of small-scale laboratory experiments followed modeling an intermediate-
 scale transition experiments referred to as MiniSandwich (see Section 3.1.5).
 Associated Disposal R&D Campaign Milestone Reports: Section 4 in Greathouse et al.
 (2025), Engineered Barrier System Research Activities at SNL: FY25 Report, SNL Report
       M3SF-25SN010308053.
 Objective: Perform HM modeling for the lab-scale, intermediate-scale and field-scale
 experiments to demonstrate the functionality of the Sandwich shaft system. Jointly simulate the
 interactions between the host rock and the engineered shaft seal elements and compare
 simulations results with the experimental data. Via model comparison and improvement,
 develop reliable hydromechanical models for shaft sealing systems that can be used to support
 robust performance and safety assessments.
 Summary:
 In contrast to conventional shaft sealing systems where one material is used as the sealing
 element, the sandwich sealing system combines components that have different hydraulic
 properties. The sealing layers are the low hydraulic conductivity layers of the shaft sealing
 system and equipotential segments are characterized by a higher hydraulic conductivity. With
 this design, fluid flow is expected to be distributed evenly within the equipotential segments over
 the cross-section of the seal, which means that more homogeneous hydration and swelling of
 the seals is obtained. This sandwich shaft sealing concept is also expected to minimize water
 fingering and unstable flows inside the system, and water bypass through pathways that
 interface between the sealing system, the host rock, and the excavation damaged zone.
 Researchers from SNL participated in the first steps of the DECOVALEX-2027 Sandwich task,
 which focused on the laboratory-scale swelling pressure test and the intermediate-scale
 MiniSandwich test. The SNL team simulated the experiment with a Richards’ equation
 describing fluid flow in the partially saturated bentonite porous medium. The extended
 Barcelona Basic Model (BBMx) was applied to represent the hydro-mechanical monotonic
 behavior of the partially water-saturated bentonite (Alonso et al., 1990; Pedroso and Farias,
 2011). The model results, which are sensitive to the selected suction parameters, correlate well
 with the experimental data, especially at higher dry densities.

 6.6.3 KEY ACCOMPLISHMENTS
 • HM simulations conducted for the DECOVALEX-2027 Sandwich task demonstrate the
 effectiveness of this innovative shaft sealing system which consists of alternating layers of
 bentonite and equipotential layers. Early modeling results agree reasonably well with data
 from laboratory-scale experiments.

December 5, 2025                                                                                             144

 6.7 PERFORMANCE ASSESSMENT AND RADIONUCLIDE TRANSPORT
 MODELING

 6.7.1 INTRODUCTION
 DOE researchers have collaborated with international colleagues on building confidence in the
 reactive transport models used for the performance and safety assessments of deep geologic
 repositories. A broad portfolio of activities has been conducted in FY25, starting with
 performance assessment model comparisons conducted under the umbrella of the
 DECOVALEX-2027 initiative (Section 3.2.3.8). International modeling teams discussed their
 modelling skills and capabilities, analyzed the influence of model choices and parameters on the
 assessment of repository performance, and compared the uncertainties introduced by model
 choices to other sources of uncertainty. Two hypothetical repository designs and geologic
 settings were considered: a repository for commercial spent nuclear fuel (SNF) in a fractured
 crystalline host rock (with ten participating modeling groups), and a repository for commercial
 SNF in a bedded salt formation (with four participating modeling groups). At the beginning of
 FY25, SNL scientists led both DECOVALEX PA tasks and participated as modeling teams.
 However, while their participation in Crystalline PA activities continues, DOE decided to
 terminate all salt-related research in the Spring of FY25 and thus SNL ended its DECOVALEX
 task activities related to Salt PA. Subsections 6.7.2 and 6.7.3 summarize FY25 activities related
 to the Crystalline and Salt PA tasks of DECOVALEX-2023.
 Developing and implementing adequate thermodynamic and thermochemical databases and
 models across broad physicochemical conditions is essential for understanding, evaluating, and
 modeling geochemical processes, such as speciation, solubility, and reaction paths. DOE
 researchers at LLNL are collaborating with international colleagues to improve the
 thermodynamic and thermochemical databases and models needed to evaluate radionuclide
 transport through engineered and natural barriers. These studies are intended to provide
 updated datasets for modeling the reactive transport of radionuclides, a central component of
 PA models, as they evaluate transport from the repository to the biosphere for dose
 calculations. Section 6.7.4 summarizes the FY25 development of these databases.
 Investigations based on natural analogs are a valuable tool for enhancing the understanding of
 the long-term performance of deep geologic repositories. Uranium ore deposits offer a unique
 opportunity to investigate near-field processes of radioactive waste disposal over geological
 time scales, like alteration of spent fuel, reduction-oxidation processes, mobilization and
 transport of radionuclides, as well as to test thermodynamic modelling concepts. An
 internationally recognized natural analog is the uranium ore deposit Cigar Lake in Canada which
 has been stable since its deposition 1.3 billion years ago. Section 6.7.5 summarizes
 collaborative studies of iodine migration at Cigar Lake under the umbrella of DECOVALEX-
 2027, modelling present-day depth concentration profiles and comparing them to measured
 data from a recent drilling campaign.
 Starting in FY25, scientists from SNL, LBNL, and LLNL have collaborated with two work
 packages in ongoing EUROPEAN Union EURAD-2 research program that focus on radionuclide
 transport modeling, namely RAMPEC (Radionuclide mobility under perturbed conditions) and
 DITSUC (Thermodynamic Database). A summary is given in Section 6.7.6.

December 5, 2025                                                                                           145

 6.7.2 COMPARATIVE PERFORMANCE ASSESSMENT MODELING OF A
 CRYSTALLINE REFERENCE CASE: DECOVALEX-2027 CRYSTALLINE PA
 TASK
 Background and International Coordination: The Crystalline Performance Assessment (PA)
 task under DECOVALEX-2027 aims to evaluate and enhance computational modeling methods
 for predicting the long-term performance of deep geological repositories in crystalline rock (see
 Section 3.2.3.8). This task extends previous work in DECOVALEX-2023 by including new
 scenarios such as receptor wells, single waste package failures, and stress-induced changes in
 fracture networks. Participating research teams simulate processes like radionuclide transport,
 fracture-matrix interactions, and groundwater flow for a set of crystalline rock reference cases
 and compare the PA modeling results to enhance confidence. A primary focus of the new
 DECOVALEX-2027 Crystalline PA task is on uncertainty quantification (UQ) and sensitivity
 analysis (SA) using nested sampling strategies, which enable a thorough assessment of
 predictive capabilities (Xu and Gertner, 2008; Osthus et al., 2008). Developing effective PA
 methods and understanding their respective uncertainties is important to DOE because PA
 calculations provide DOE decision makers with quantitative evaluations of long-term system
 behavior.
 Associated Disposal R&D Campaign Milestone Reports: Section 3.1.2 in Mariner et al.
 (2025b), GDSA Framework Development: Annual Report 2025, SNL Report, M2SF-
 25SN010304086; Section 2.2 in Hyman et al. (2025), Crystalline Host Rock Properties &
 Processes - LANL, LANL Report, M4SF-25LA010302012.
 Objective: The main goal of the PA Crystalline Task is to increase confidence in the models,
 methods, and software used for performance assessment (PA) of deep geologic repositories in
 fractured crystalline rock, and to guide further research and development needed to enhance
 PA methodologies and uncertainty assessment.
 Summary:
 The Crystalline PA Task for DECOVALEX-2027 is a continuation of the DECOVALEX-2023
 crystalline PA task. All seven teams from DECOVALEX-2023 elected to participate in the new
 task, and two additional teams joined. SNL scientists continue to lead the new task which is
 advantageous to DOE because task leads control the task planning process and focus work on
 activities beneficial to the U.S. Program. In addition, leading a task inherently increases
 interactions with international experts and exposure to new approaches and resources. In
 addition to providing task leadership, the Disposal R&D Campaign has two modeling teams
 participating in the Crystalline PA task, the SNL team with emphasis on transport processes and
 their uncertainty, and the LANL team with emphasis on stress impacts on transport.
 • SNL Crystalline PA Leadership and Modeling Studies
 Under SNL’s task leadership, the DECOVALEX-2027 task defined three new modeling
 benchmarks as follows. The first benchmark involves a four-fracture network with a single
 waste package failure scenario and a pumping well which draws water in the vicinity of the
 repository. The benchmark was simulated using a DFN model and an upscaled Equivalent
 Continuous Porous Medium (ECPM) model. The ECPM model produces much lower
 breakthrough at the extraction well compared to the DFN model, which is likely due to a high
 amount of numerical diffusion occurring up-fracture due to the cubic grid of the ECPM
 model. Further work is planned to develop a correction for this behavior.
 The second benchmark involves uncertainty quantification and sensitivity analyses (UQ/SA).
 UQ is used to propagate input parameter value uncertainties to produce model output
 probability distributions. SA in this case is used to measure how much uncertainty in the

December 5, 2025                                                                                           146

 model results is due to uncertainties in the values of input parameters. Thus, the results of
 UQ/SA can identify the sources and relative effects of uncertainties in model. This
 information can be used to prioritize additional research to reduce input parameter value
 uncertainty, and thereby model uncertainty, or possibly to justify exclusion of a process in
 the model that has negligible effects on model outputs. The UQ/SA studies focus on a
 nested sampling plan with outer loop sampling of discrete fracture networks properties and
 inner loop sampling of other epistemic transport parameters. An initial UQ/SA test case was
 conducted by SNL which varies three parameters, flow velocity, retardation coefficient, and
 tortuosity, and examines the impact on concentration as a function of time at an observation
 point as the quantity of interest.
 The third benchmark focuses on the effects of background stress on solute transport
 through fractured media. Changes in the background stress field can occur for a variety of
 reasons such as glacial loading or ground motion. The changes in normal and shear stress
 on each fracture can cause significant changes in transmissivity. This benchmark utilizes
 three distinct network scenarios from DECOVALEX-2023: a four-fracture case, a four-
 fracture case with stochastic fractures, and a scenario with only stochastic fractures. Initially,
 the study considers an unstressed condition, followed by scenarios that incorporate
 reference stresses and stress ratios.
 SNL’s UQ/SA capabilities have benefitted from participation in an international working
 group called JOSA, which stands for JOint Sensitivity Analysis group. The focus of the
 JOSA working group is on the use of sensitivity analysis in case studies involving geologic
 disposal of spent nuclear fuel with the overall aim of providing guidelines for performing
 such analyses in the context of safety assessments or safety cases for geological repository
 facilities. JOSA recently conducted a collaborative sensitivity analysis exercise to gain a
 better understanding of the strengths and weaknesses of various SA methods, identify cost
 vs. performance tradeoffs of the methods, and highlight best practices and lessons learned
 (Swiler et al., 2021). Multiple countries participated in the application of various SA methods
 on a series of case studies involving geologic disposal of radioactive waste. Findings from
 this joint SA exercise proved very useful for the DECOVALEX-2027 Crystalline PA task.
 • LANL Crystalline PA Leadership and Modeling Studies
 In support of SNL’s overall task leadership, LANL scientists are leading the subtask of the
 Crystalline PA task that focuses on understanding how changes in the geological stress
 field—such as those caused by seismic events, tectonic shifts, or glacial loading—impact
 fracture networks and their associated fluid flow and radionuclide transport characteristics.
 Recognizing the complexity of fully coupled hydromechanical modeling], the task adopts a
 simplified one-way coupling approach, where stress alterations modify fracture apertures
 using empirical relationships (e.g., Barton-Bandis model), affecting transmissivity and
 permeability. Teams explore three fracture network scenarios with varying stress
 magnitudes and anisotropies applied to deterministic and stochastic fracture networks,
 quantifying changes in effective permeability and tracer breakthrough behavior. By
 systematically comparing stressed versus unstressed network simulations, this subtask
 evaluates whether stress-induced fracture deformation represents a first-order factor in long-
 term repository performance, thus enhancing the robustness and realism of PA modeling
 frameworks for crystalline rock disposal systems.
 Initial modeling results show that stress-induced fracture deformation significantly alters
 fracture apertures, which, in turn, change the hydraulic transmissivity and effective
 permeability within the network. Under both isotropic and anisotropic stress conditions,
 fractures oriented perpendicular to the highest principal stress experienced notable closure,

December 5, 2025                                                                                               147

 leading to reduced permeability and more localized flow paths. Conversely, fractures aligned
 favorably for shear dilation experienced increases in aperture. These aperture changes
 subsequently affected fluid flow patterns, resulting in measurable differences in predicted
 breakthrough curves of conservative and reactive tracers. In scenarios involving
 deterministic large fractures, the impact of stress is less pronounced than in purely
 stochastic networks.

 6.7.3 COMPARATIVE PERFORMANCE ASSESSMENT MODELING OF A SALT
 REFERENCE CASE: DECOVALEX-2027 SALT PA TASK
 At the beginning of FY25, SNL researchers continued their task leadership of and participation
 in a DECVOVALEX-2027 task involving a simplified post-closure performance assessment of a
 repository in a domal salt formation (Section 3.2.3.8). Expanding on their previous work in
 DECOVALEX-2023, four international teams started working on a new set of modeling
 challenges as follows:
 • Benchmark cases for diffusion and salt creep
 • Heat generation and gas generation associated with steel corrosion
 • Uncertainty quantification and sensitivity analysis with a primary focus on creep closure
 models and diffusive transport.
 In the Spring of 2025, DOE’s disposal research portfolio experienced a significant realignment
 of R&D priorities and scope, with resulted in the termination all salt-related disposal research
 work in May of 2025. As a result, the SNL team ended its task participation and transferred its
 task leadership to German colleagues.

 6.7.4 THERMODYNAMIC AND THERMOCHEMICAL DATABASES
 DEVELOPMENT: COLLABORATION WITH NEA-TDB AND EURAD-2
 DITUSC
 Background and International Coordination: Thermodynamic and thermochemical data are
 essential for understanding, assessing, and modeling geochemical processes and radionuclide
 transport. Over the past several years, LLNL scientists been developing a workflow to improve
 DOE’s thermodynamic and thermochemical modeling capability (e.g., NEA’s Thermochemical
 Database, Andra’s Thermochimie database, Paul Scherrer Institute’s database). LLNL’s
 activities have been aligned with internationally accepted best practices, via collaboration with
 NEA-TBD (Section 3.5.5), EURAD-2 DITUSC (Section 3.6), and other international programs.
 Objectives: To ensure that radionuclide modeling in DOE’s safety assessments utilizes
 internationally accepted thermodynamic and thermochemical databases, thereby enhancing
 confidence in the performance and safety assessments for generic reference cases.
 Associated Disposal R&D Campaign Milestone Report: Sections 2, 3, and 4 in Zavarin et al.
 (2025), L-SCIE and SUPCRTNE Model and Database Development, LLNL Report, M3SF-
       25LL010301052.
 Summary:
 Continuing focus areas in FY25 have been the development and improvement of international
 open-source thermodynamic databases and the integration of surface complexation and ion
 exchange modeling capabilities for radionuclide sorption to clay minerals.

December 5, 2025                                                                                           148

 • Thermochemical Database Development
 In FY25, LLNL improved its SUPCRTNE thermodynamic database by combining two
 international thermodynamic databases, the Nuclear Energy Agency (NEA) database, which
 is based on the CODATA international standard, and the HP11 mineral dataset of Holland
 and Powell (2011), which is widely used in petrology and geochemistry. The NEA dataset is
 recognized for its rigorous methodology and international peer review, making it a preferred
 standard for regulatory applications such as nuclear waste disposal. However, it offers
 limited coverage of rock-forming minerals, a gap filled by the HP11 dataset, which contains
 data for 210 such minerals. A methodology was developed to adjust the HP11 data,
 maximizing consistency with the NEA standard, enabling the creation of a comprehensive
 and internally consistent thermodynamic database suitable for a wide range of geochemical
 applications.
 • Surface Complexation and Ion Exchange Database Development
 Effective parameterization of sorption processes over the wide range of conditions that may
 be present in nuclear waste repositories requires a robust and comprehensive database of
 experimentally derived sorption data. The Lawrence Livermore National Laboratory-Surface
 Complexation/Ion Exchange (L-SCIE) database is a recent effort to unify community
 sorption experimental data and metadata in a findable, accessible, interoperable, and
 reusable (FAIR) format. In FY25, the database was enhanced with additional sorption data
 from international sources and as of now contains nearly 73,000 records. Recent major
 additions to this database were the inclusion of ~5,000 clay sorption data that were provided
 through a collaboration with the ClaySor group at the Paul Scherrer Institute (Marinich et al.,
 2023), 40,000 data (out of 70,000 total) that were extracted from the online sorption
 database supported by the Japanese Atomic Energy Agency (JAEA, 2025), and small
 numbers of data (100’s out of ~4,700 total) that were provided through our collaboration with
 the SOREDA/RES3T team at the Helmholtz-Zentrum Dresden-Rossendorf (Brendler et al.,
          2023, RES³T, 2025).
 Today, the L-SCIE database provides an important and effective tool for modeling the
 sorption (retardation) of radionuclides onto subsurface minerals, supporting both traditional
 surface complexation/ion exchange modeling and hybrid machine learning (ML)
 approaches. The database has been linked to the PHREEQC and CrunchFlow geochemical
 modelling codes that can be used to identify reactions and associated reaction constants.
 High throughput automated workflows were developed in FY25 using the CrunchFlow
 geochemical modeling engine (replacing PHREEQC) to model sorption of U(VI) to
 montmorillonite. This effort expands from existing workflows that focus on single
 radionuclide-mineral pairs to more comprehensive workflows for complex radioactive
 transport modeling challenges related to disposal in clay-based media.

 6.7.5 SIMULATION OF IODINE MIGRATION AT CIGAR LAKE – NATURAL
 ANALOG: DECOVALEX-2027 ANALOG TASK
 Background and International Coordination: The 1.3-billion-year-old uranium ore deposit at
 Cigar Lake in Canada provides important data to study the long-term geochemical conditions
 and the stability of the uranium ore body. The ore body is surrounded by a clay halo, analogous
 to the clay-rich mineralogy typical in argillaceous host rocks. Therefore, the Cigar Lake site is
 used as a natural analog site for simulating long-term radionuclide migration as part of
 DECOVALEX-2027 (Section 3.2.3.7). Recent measurements of uranium and iodine
 concentrations across a 400 m depth profile through the or body and its overlying sediments

December 5, 2025                                                                                            149

 (Mozafarishamsi, 2023) have been provided to participating DECOVALEX modeling teams to
 test their models.
 Objectives: The aim of this study is to gain a quantitative understanding of radionuclide
 migration in the subsurface over very long timeframes, thereby demonstrating the long-term
 retention capability of argillaceous rock.
 Associated Disposal R&D Campaign Milestone Reports: (a) Section 10 in Rutqvist et al.
 (2025), Investigation of Coupled Processes in Argillite Rock: FY25, LBNL Report, M3SF-
 25LB010301031; (b) Part 1, Section 5 in Jové-Colón et al. (2025), Evaluation of Argillite Host
 Rock Properties & Processes (FY25), SNL Report, M2SF-26SN010203032.
 Summary:
 Modeling teams from LBNL and SNL collaborated with several international teams in the
 comparative modeling of the Cigar Lake Analog. After investigating the source term for iodine in
 the ore body (either spontaneous fission of 238U or fission from both 238U and 235U), models were
 designed to simulate the concentration profile of 129I measured across the uranium ore body and
 the surrounding clay matrix. This modeling is conducted to gain a quantitative understanding of
 temporal and spatial scales of 129I migration. Iodine, and particularly the isotope of 129I, is among
 the most important dose-relevant isotopes, and therefore of special concern in the context of
 high-level radioactive waste disposal.
 • LBNL Model for the Cigar Lake Analog
 LBNL deployed a diffusion-sorption geochemical model to simulate the 129I concentration
 profile from the center of the ore body into the surrounding clay halo. The model domain is
 50 m covering 25 m above and below the ore body, which has a thickness of 10 m.
 Simulations are realized with an in-house python-based implementation of Fick’s second law
 including the source term variations. The main assumption underlying the simulations is that
 129
 I transport through the model domain is controlled by diffusion and sorption due to the
 high clay mineral content and associated low permeability. Since no site-specific
 measurements for diffusion and sorption are available, values are selected based on a
 literature review for sorption and diffusion experiments using illite or illite-rich formations
 considered for nuclear waste disposal. Two source term scenarios were investigated for that
 the production of 129I in the ore body: one considering only the spontaneous fission of 238U,
 and the other including fission from both 238U and 235U. Additionally, the sensitivity of the
 model results was examined in regard to diffusion coefficients and sorption capacities
 represented by distribution coefficients with the assumption that illite is the sole adsorbent.
 LBNL’s preliminary modeling exercises showed that the present-day 129I concentration
 profile cannot be explained solely by the spontaneous fission of 238U. Concentrations of 129I
 in the ore body are underestimated by the model in comparison to the data. Instead,
 neutron-induced fission of 235U must also be incorporated into the source term calculation,
 as this process is particularly significant at high concentrations of U in the central part of the
 ore body. However, the incorporation of neutron-induced fission of 235U in the source term
 led to the overestimation of 129I concentration in ore body. To better match the measured
 data, a higher-than-normal adsorption capacity needed to be used for the ore body. This
 modeling exercise suggests that minerals other than illite, such as Cu(I)-sulfides, might be
 important additional adsorbents governing the radioactive transport.
 • SNL Model for the Cigar Lake Analog
 SNL used the reactive transport simulator PFLOTRAN to simulate the 129I concentration
 profiles in a model domain of 100 m vertical extent, comprising from top to bottom 40 m of

December 5, 2025                                                                                               150

 sandstone overburden, 10 m of clay, 10 m or iron ore, and 40 m of crystalline basement.
 The observed bulk uranium concentrations specified in the task were used to adjust the
 volume fractions of uraninite (UO2) of the ore and other rock regions in the PFLOTRAN
 model to closely match the observed total uranium concentrations in the layered domain.
 This fitting approach assumes that total uranium is controlled by uraninite solubility. 129I
 generation as a fission product is represented by a “fictive” 238U à 129I decay to produce
 sufficient iodine to evaluate diffusive transport in the near-field domain. The use of a linear
 distribution coefficient, Kd, allows for representation of iodine partitioning in the ore and
 adjacent regions. The simulated 129I concentrations could match the measured data well, but
 only when higher than typically expected sorption coefficients were used for the iodine. This
 finding is similar to LBNL’s results. SNL’s model also suggests that unsaturated conditions
 in the ore region result in better agreement with the field data, particularly in domains above
 and below the ore region.
 Although the mechanisms of 129I migration at the Cigar Lake uranium deposit remain
 somewhat unclear due to data uncertainties, the preliminary modeling conducted to date by
 LBNL and SNL illustrates the complex interactions between rate-controlling reactions and
 transport processes.

 6.7.6 EURAD-2 INTERACTIONS WITH RAMPEC AND DITUSC
 Background and International Coordination: The ongoing EUROPEAN Union EURAD-2
 research program has two work packages with focus on radionuclide transport modeling,
 namely RAMPEC (Radionuclide mobility under perturbed conditions) and DITSUC
 (Thermodynamic Database). To explore synergistic benefits, DOE scientists from SNL, LBNL,
 and LLNL are participating “in kind” as Associate Partners with some of the EURAD-2 activities
 in these work packages.
 Objectives: To ensure knowledge exchange with EURAD-2 activities and utilize synergistic
 collaboration opportunities.
 Associated Disposal R&D Campaign Milestone Report: Part 1, Section 4.5 in Jové-Colón et
 al. (2025), Evaluation of Argillite Host Rock Properties & Processes (FY25), SNL Report, M2SF-
       26SN010203032.
 Summary:
 Modeling teams from SNL, LBNL and LLNL have participated “in kind” with select activities in
 RAMPEC and DITUSC, as follows.
 • RAMPEC Collaboration
 The EURAD-2 RAMPEC work packages aims for improved understanding and prediction of
 disposal system chemistry and radionuclide mobility under perturbed conditions. Three main
 types of perturbations are addressed through research activities: temperature changes,
 saturation changes, and chemical (pH, salinity, organics, etc.) perturbations. This involves
 research investigations assessing knowledge gaps related to the effects of these
 perturbations by developing new experimental data and supporting development of
 mechanistic models on radionuclide migration in deep geological disposal environments.
 RAMPEC will consider collaboration on (i) techniques for pre-conditioning clay samples
 (representing perturbed conditions) for transport experiments, (ii) approaches for conducting
 sorption studies at elevated temperatures, (iii) utilization of similar clay materials for
 transport experiments under partially saturated conditions, (iv) and identification of
 prioritized radionuclides. SNL has been participating in a task on clay systems. Early in
 FY25, SNL proposed the use of molecular modeling to assess the effect of perturbations on

December 5, 2025                                                                                            151

 radionuclide transport in illitic clay systems. However, due to funding limitations, the role of
 SNL will be mainly virtual participation in online meetings/workshops and discussions.
 • DITUSC Collaboration
 DITUSC’s goal is the development quality assured thermodynamic understanding and data
 in support of nuclear waste disposal safety cases. The work package is currently a strategic
 study with the goal of producing a “white paper” on the importance of thermodynamic and
 associated data. The “white paper” is expected to make a case for a future EURAD-2
 research project on thermodynamic database improvements. The participation of DOE
 scientists in DITUSC activities has been mainly via attending project meetings/workshops,
 giving a presentation on thermodynamic database development in the Disposal R&D
 Campaign (Dr. Mavrik Zavarin – LLNL), and performing document reviews.

 6.7.7 KEY ACCOMPLISHMENTS
 • In the Crystalline PA Task of the DECOVALEX-2027 initiative, confidence in performance
 assessment models and methods was enhanced by comparing PA modeling results from
 ten international disposal programs applied to the same reference case involving a
 repository in fractured crystalline host rock. To identify the sources and relative effects of
 uncertainties in models, participating teams developed and tested a workflow to conduct
 uncertainty quantification and sensitivity analyses (UQ/SA) on radionuclide transport in
 complex fracture networks.
 • Initial modeling results from the DECOVALEX-2027 Crystalline PA task for deterministic and
 stochastic fracture network scenarios reveal that stress-driven aperture adjustments can
 result in up to an order of magnitude variation in transport times, particularly in networks with
 dominant fractures oriented perpendicular to principal stress directions. The influence of
 stress is less prominent in modeling scenarios with deterministic large fractures than in
 purely stochastic networks. Validating reliable models for performance and safety
 assessments is a primary goal of DECOVALEX-2027.
 • Continued international collaboration of LLNL researchers with the international
 geochemical modeling community led to significant improvement in thermodynamic and
 thermochemical databases and models for radionuclide transport simulations. Interoperable
 open-source databases have been built that harness modern data science workflows and
 algorithms. This collaborative achievement provides a strong foundation for future work in
 geochemical modeling, with the potential to support complex scenarios in nuclear waste
 isolation, rock-water interactions, and other related applications.
 • Natural analogs are a very useful tool to bridge the scales (spatial and temporal) between
 laboratory/field tests and a deep geologic repository. In the context of DECOVALEX-2027,
 modeling teams from LBNL and SNL have been collaborating with several international
 teams in the comparative modeling of the Cigar Lake Analog. Although the mechanisms of
 129
 I migration at the Cigar Lake uranium deposit remain somewhat unclear due to data
 uncertainties, the preliminary models developed to date illustrate the complex interactions
 between rate-controlling reactions and transport processes.

 6.8 NEXT GENERATION WORKFORCE DEVELOPMENT
 6.8.1 INTRODUCTION
 Investing in the next generation of diverse, talented nuclear waste scientists and leaders is vital
 to fulfilling DOE NE’s mission of developing solutions for the long-term geologic disposal of

December 5, 2025                                                                                              152

 radioactive waste. In FY22, the Disposal R&D Campaign established a Next Generation
 Workforce Development Pilot Program to attract, train and advance a talented and diverse
 future workforce in disposal research. LBNL and SNL initiated the program, and some other
 national laboratories were brought into the program in later years. International collaboration
 has been important in this context: To help recruit and train a broad talent pool, the workforce
 development program provided interesting learning/research challenges and attractive work-
 abroad opportunities in conjunction with international collaboration partners. The program had
 been a great success and the Disposal R&D Campaign initially envisioned continuation into
 FY25 and beyond. However, the funding for disposal workforce development ended in early
 2025 when DOE’s disposal R&D priorities were going through a significant realignment. This
 section summarizes the program activities from FY22 until its termination in FY25, based on two
 close-out reports provided by LBNL and SNL.

 6.8.2 OVERVIEW OF WORKFORCE DEVELOPMENT ACTIVITIES
 Background and International Coordination: International training programs and international
 research activities provide valuable opportunities for attracting and training talented young
 scientists. To tap into these opportunities, the Disposal R&D Campaign in FY22 established a
 multi-year Next Generation Workforce Development Pilot Program to attract, train and advance
 a talented and diverse future workforce in disposal research.
 Objectives: From FY22 until its termination in the Spring of FY25, the pilot program activities at
 focused on the following objectives:
 • Stimulating Student Interest in Nuclear Waste Disposal: This involved coordinating seminars
 and summer classes, working with minority serving universities, establishing a virtual
 seminar series for interested undergraduate/graduate students and young nuclear waste
 disposal researchers, and organizing student participation in existing international training
 programs.
 • Creating the Pipeline: Through undergraduate and graduate internship programs,
 participating national laboratories provided internship opportunities with undergraduate and
 graduate students placed into selected disposal research activities. Intern projects were
 often related to international research activities conducted at national labs and included
 opportunities to spend some research time at an international partner institution.
 • Advancing the Pipeline: LBNL and SNL each provided one dedicated postdoc position in a
 disposal research project with international collaboration. The projects include opportunities
 to spend some research time at international partner institutions.
 Associated Disposal R&D Campaign Milestone Reports: (a) Zheng et al. (2025b),
 Developing a Diverse Next Generation Workforce for Radioactive Waste Disposal: FY25
 Accomplishments Memo (Close-Out Report), LBNL Report, SF-25LB01010802, (b) Mendez
 (2025), Developing the Next Generation Workforce for Radioactive Waste Disposal: SNL
 Activities in FY25, Close-Out Report, SNL Report, M4SF-24SN010307052.
 Summary:
 • Stimulating Student Interest in Nuclear Waste Disposal
 The workforce development program supported five individual and recurring engagements
 during the pilot phase:
 (1) A webinar series “Next Generation Workforce Development for Spent Fuel and Waste
 Science and Technology” with 7 talks was co-organized in 2024 by MIT, Texas A&M,
 SNL, and LBNL. The seminar reached a broad audience with an average attendance of

December 5, 2025                                                                                            153

 around 50-60. Another series for the spring semester starting from January 2025 were
 planned by the same organizations but was canceled as the program’s direction
 changed early 2025.
 (2) Since FY22, SNL collaborated with Dr. Neven Ali, a lecturer at the University of New
 Mexico Department of Nuclear Engineering. SNL scientists developed and delivered
 lectures in support of UNM’s Radioactive Waste Management course, centered around
 current topics and challenges in the field. The most recent lecture series was held in the
 Fall semester of 2024, with ten lectures and a tour of SNL facilities.
 (3) SNL collaborated with the University of Puerto Rico, Mayaguez, to support a Mechanical
 Engineering Graduate Seminar (2022-2024). SNL researchers in Radioactive Waste
 Management developed and delivered a series of virtual talks to introduce students to
 the field of Radioactive Waste Management and the work performed at the National
 Labs.
 (4) SNL partnered with Texas A&M on a two-week course titled “Backend of the Nuclear
 Fuel Cycle and Radioactive Waste Management, Storage and Disposal” (2022-2024)
 (5) SNL contributed to the Massachusetts Institute of Technology Nuclear Waste Educator’s
              Workshop (2022).
 • Creating the Pipeline
 LBNL launched the “INGENUITY” summer internship program in 2022 and repeated the
 program successfully in 2023 and 2024 (https://sites.google.com/lbl.gov/eesa-student-
 opportunities/nextgen-nuclear-waste-disposal-internship). In the last application phase in
 2024, LBNL offered seven internship projects and received 38 student applications from 14
 countries. The program was to be offered again in FY25. It was promoted on a variety of
 occasions and platforms and received about 40 applications by February 2025. LBNL then
 notified the applicants that this program was unfortunately canceled. In summary, a total of
 19 summer interns participated in 2022, 2023, and 2024 in research projects across a
 variety of disciplines (geochemistry, geomechanics, and geophysics). Several summer
 interns continued their research work with their mentor after the official internship ended and
 three interns were later hired at LBNL as postdoctoral researchers. Research conducted by
 interns contributed to 13 scientific publications.
 SNL launched a similar internship program over the past 3 year. In total, five undergraduate
 interns and four graduate interns participated in the Workforce Development program. A
 total of six interns were ultimately converted to year-round internships working on disposal
 research work packages. While on the program, two undergraduate interns completed their
 degree in 2023 and are continuing to participate as graduate students. In addition, one
 graduate intern transitioned to “all but dissertation” status and is making progress to finish
 the PhD degree this year. All interns remain in the workforce development pipeline and
 have now transitioned from Workforce Development funds to directly funded projects. No
 new interns were identified in FY25 since the program was closed out before the summer
 session.
 • Advancing the Pipeline
 A postdoctoral researcher, Dr. Radhavi Abeysiridara Samarakoon, was hired at LBNL at the
 end of FY22 and dedicated all her effort to the HotBENT project, an international
 collaborative project that studies the evolution of bentonite buffer under high-temperature
 heating. She successfully developed coupled a thermal, hydrological, and mechanical model
 for the HotBENT field test and contributed to a milestone reports and conferences

December 5, 2025                                                                                            154

 publications. She also attended two courses at the Grimsel Training Centre in Switzerland:
 “Data Management - Making Geoscientific Data FAIR” and “In-situ Testing and Hydraulic
 Characterization in URL” (https://www.grimsel.com/grimsel-training-centre-gtc). In FY25,
 LBNL planned to convert her to a scientist position, but these plans stalled due to funding
 restrictions in the Disposal R&D Campaign.
 After an extensive search in FY22, SNL was able to identify and hire a postdoctoral
 appointee to support the disposal research program during FY23. The successful candidate,
 Dr. Lisa Bigler, holds a PhD in Mathematics from Oregon State University and came to
 Sandia with previous internship experiences at both LBNL and ORNL. Dr. Bigler was
 converted to staff in early FY24 to work with the team in GDSA Repository Safety
 Assessment (RSA), incorporating geology into uncertainty quantification and sensitivity
 analysis (UQ/SA). SNL then conducted a second search for a new postdoctoral appointee
 and successfully hired Dr. Byung Tark Lee to support additional disposal research work
 under workforce development. Dr. Lee has been making contributions to the PFLOTRAN
 development team by improving the geomechanical aspects of the PFLOTRAN simulator.
 Unfortunately, these talented staff members are separating from SNL due to funding
 reductions in the Disposal R&D Campaign.
 In summary, the pilot program has provided a valuable blueprint for next-generation workforce
 development. Early engagement with students, especially before they have chosen a career
 path, is critical to increase their awareness of the challenges of nuclear waste management and
 the variety of fields of expertise needed to address these challenges. This was done
 successfully via lab-university organized lectures and seminars as well as through internship
 programs at national labs. Potential partnerships with professors should be explored in this
 context, both through their university curricula and through part-time or temporary R&D
 opportunities for students. Recruiting and retaining qualified candidates for post-doctoral
 appointments proofed to be challenging. A long-term path from internships to post-doctoral and
 full-time employment opportunities is critical to retain talent.

 6.8.3 KEY ACCOMPLISHMENTS
 • Stimulating Student Interest in Nuclear Waste Research: Between FY22 and FY24, the Next
 Generation Workforce Development Pilot Program inspired hundreds of students via lab-
 university organized lectures and seminars to become interested in nuclear waste disposal
 science.
 • Creating the Pipeline: The summer internship program trained a large diverse student group
 of about 30 interns at LBNL and SNL. This clearly created a pipeline: Many of the summer
 interns returned to their hosting national lab (or other opportunities) after their initial summer
 program ended, either for another summer program or in some cases as year-round interns.
 At LBNL, three summer interns were later hired as postdoctoral researchers.
 • Advancing the Pipeline: LBNL and SNL each hired a dedicated “next generation” postdoc for
 a three-year research project with international collaboration, which allowed them to make
 remarkable research contributions. For example, LBNL’s dedicated post-doc works on THM
 modeling for the international HotBENT Project and, to better connect her with the
 international community, participated in a 2023 summer training program at the Grimsel Test
 Site. SNL’s dedicated postdoc worked in GDSA RSA, incorporating geology into UQ/SA.
 Unfortunately, the postdocs could not be transferred into career scientist positions at the two
 national laboratories, due to recent funding reductions in the Disposal R&D Campaign.

December 5, 2025                                                                                               155

 7 SUMMARY AND CONCLUDING REMARKS
 This report describes the FY25 status of international collaboration focused on investigations of
 nuclear waste disposal in geological formations as part of DOE’s Disposal R&D Campaign.
 Since 2012, the campaign has actively collaborated with several international geologic disposal
 programs in Europe, North America, and Asia. The joint research activities with international
 programs, initiatives, and projects are extremely beneficial to DOE’s disposal research program,
 because they: (1) provide first-hand access to the decades of experience that our international
 partners have gained in various disposal options and geologic environments, (2) give DOE
 scientists an extensive library of experimental data from many past and ongoing in-situ tests
 conducted in several URLs in different host rocks, (3) provide a framework for active peer-to-
 peer research participation in international groups that conduct, analyze, and model
 performance-relevant processes, and (4) open the door for DOE to conducting in-situ
 experiments in international URLs not available in the U.S. Last not least, international
 collaboration allows the Disposal R&D Campaign to benefit from substantial international
 investments in research facilities (such as underground research laboratory testing and
 modeling) and achieve cost savings via joint funding of expensive field experiments.
 This report provides an in-depth overview of the various opportunities for international
 collaboration available to DOE researchers, with a primary focus on those opportunities that
 involve field experiments in international URLs. Section 3 of this report contains a summary of
 current and previously active international opportunities resulting from DOE’s formal partnership
 in collaborative initiatives: the Mont Terri Project (since 2012), the DECOVALEX Initiative (since
 2012), the SKB Task Forces (2014 – 2025), and the HotBENT Project (since 2018), as well as
 in past years the Colloid Formation and Migration Project (2012 – 2015) and the FEBEX
 Dismantling Project (2015 – 2018). All of these are multinational initiatives with emphasis on
 field experiments in international URLs. Other multinational initiatives, such as several NEA-
 coordinated activities, provide valuable opportunities for information exchange and data
 assessment. Additional opportunities for multinational collaboration exist: For example, DOE
 just instituted a technical partnership with the EURAD-2 European Commission collaborative
 research program which initiated a new round of repository R&D work for a 5-year period from
 2024 through 2029. The Disposal R&D Campaign has also explored direct bilateral
 opportunities for active research collaboration, with institutions from Germany, Republic of
 Korea, Sweden, Israel, France, Japan, Belgium, Finland, Czech Republic, and China. Some of
 these opportunities have resulted in close bilateral research activities between DOE scientists
 and their international counterparts; the others provide opportunities for active research
 collaboration in the future.
 Over the years, as research priorities have changed and new opportunities for collaboration
 developed, DOE’s international disposal research portfolio has evolved and will continue to
 evolve. The Disposal R&D Campaign has made a targeted effort to reassess its international
 collaboration activities on a regular (annual) basis. Section 5 describes the continued planning
 process which comprises (1) re-evaluating ongoing international research activities, (2)
 assessing the technical merit of new collaboration opportunities, and (3) revising the overall
 portfolio of international R&D activities as appropriate. Given its importance within the overall
 portfolio of disposal research activities, any such planning and prioritization has been done in
 tight integration with other disposal research areas, e.g., the host-rock-specific, EBS, and
 performance assessment work packages. Section 5 also gives a brief high-level overview of the
 major R&D themes and international experiments that DOE researchers have participated in
 since 2012, either as active experimental team members or as researchers involved in the
 interpretative evaluation and modeling of the experimental data.

December 5, 2025                                                                                            156

 A major realignment of DOE’s disposal R&D priorities and scope took place in the Spring of
 2025. As a result, the international disposal R&D portfolio changed substantially during the fiscal
 year, placing an increased emphasis on argillite and crystalline as potential host rocks, and
 highlighting R&D collaborations on demonstrating the feasibility of high-temperature disposal
 and testing the validity of performance and safety assessment predictions. This report covers
 the portfolio transition in FY25 as follows: all collaborations and activities undertaken during the
 fiscal year are documented, even if some concluded before or at the end of the year, with clear
 references to any portfolio change that may have occurred.
 Section 6 provides summaries of all FY25 research activities in the Disposal R&D Campaign
 which have been conducted under the umbrella of the international collaboration. A
 comprehensive list of research activities attests to the fact that DOE researchers have
 effectively used these opportunities and international collaboration activities formed a
 considerable portion of the campaign’s disposal research program. International collaboration
 has been a prominent element of key work packages, such as the EBS, Crystalline, Argillite,
 and Salt Work Packages, and more recently also in the Geologic Disposal Safety Assessment
 (GDSA) Work Packages. In a balanced portfolio of relevant R&D challenges, international
 collaboration addresses essential research areas such as engineered barrier integrity, near-field
 perturbations, radionuclide transport, integrated system behavior, performance and safety
 assessment, site characterization, and monitoring approaches. Note that this report handles the
 portfolio transition in FY25 as follows: All collaborations and activities conducted during the
 fiscal year are reported on, even if some ended throughout or at the end of the year, while
 making sure to point to any portfolio change that took place.
 International collaboration has led to substantial scientific and technical advances over the past
 years. DOE researchers have significantly improved their understanding of the current technical
 basis through the joint R&D with international researchers, the worldwide sharing of knowledge
 and experience, and the access to field and laboratory experiments from a variety of URLs and
 host rocks. They have utilized data and results from laboratory and field studies that have been
 and are being conducted with millions of R&D investments provided by international partners.
 Advanced simulation models have been verified and validated against these experimental data,
 providing a robust modeling and experimental basis for the prediction of the complex processes
 defining the performance of a multi-barrier waste repository system. In addition, international
 scientists have contributed to the analysis and interpretative modeling of laboratory or field
 studies performed by the Disposal R&D Campaign, such as the HotBENT lab experiments and
 the BATS/BATS2 field experiments. Comparison of model results with other international
 modeling groups, using their simulation tools and conceptual understanding, has enhanced
 confidence in the robustness of predictive models used for performance assessment. In
 addition, the possibility of linking model differences to conceptual model setup has provided
 valuable guidance into “best” modeling choices and understanding the effect of conceptual
 model selection on prediction uncertainty. Such advances in the ability to make long-term
 predictions of complex coupled THMC and flow and transport processes contribute directly to
 improved post-closure PA models. Access to international field testing has also provided an
 opportunity to develop, test, and demonstrate new methods and workflows for site
 characterization and monitoring of deep geological disposal.
 In the Disposal R&D Campaign, the work packages for international collaboration, EBS, host-
 rock-specific topics, and performance assessment are well integrated to make optimal use of
 improved process models leading to better safety assessment models. For example, the joint
 modeling and comparative analysis of complex in-situ experiments can ultimately lead to better
 predictive models and thus directly contribute to the confidence building of PA predictions.
 International collaboration has allowed for the engagement of DOE researchers with the

December 5, 2025                                                                                              157

 international waste management R&D community, which helped implement best practices, new
 science advances, state-of-the-art simulation tools, and lessons learned, and has provided
 ample opportunity for training/educating junior staff to move a disposal program forward into the
 next decades.
 Below are brief highlights of selected research areas where international collaboration has
 resulted in significant scientific advances that are relevant not only to the Disposal R&D
 Campaign but also to the broader radioactive waste management and disposal community.
 Progress Related to Temperature-Induced Impacts on Engineered and Natural Barriers
       (Section 6.1)
 Collaborations with the international HotBENT project include experimental and modeling
 studies to understand how temperature-dependent disturbances and modifications in bentonite
 impact its use as an engineered barrier material at temperatures up to 200oC. These activities
 have provided valuable insights into how repository performance might be affected by these
 disturbances and changes.
 • 3D THM modeling of the HotBENT field experiment shows that the simulation results match
 the observed evolution of temperature increase and relative humidity. Overall, simulations
 confirm the combined effects of heating and hydration on the spatial variability of porosity,
 which influences the density distribution within the buffer. The 3D THM model, successfully
 tested against HotBENT field test data, can now be used to predict the long-term and large-
