 scale temperature behavior of a high-temperature repository.
 • A series of benchtop-scale high-temperature column experiments was conducted to support
 the HotBENT field test. Building on lessons learned from previous HotBENT Lab Tests #1
 and #2, a new column experiment, HotBENT Lab #3, began in FY25, with the goal of
 helping to calibrate time-domain reflectometry (TDR) data obtained in the field test to
 evaluate hydration status.
 • The full-scale FE heater test at Mont Terri, now over ten years into its heating phase with
 temperatures up to about 130°C near the heater, continues to provide valuable THM data at
 relevant spatial and temporal scales. Under the umbrella of Task C of the DECOVALEX-
 2023 project, DOE researchers and several international partners finalized their
 interpretative and comparative THM simulations for model validation. Based on a
 comprehensive sensitivity study, the teams achieved an excellent overall match with the
 measured pore pressure changes in the host rock, demonstrating that reliable models exist
 for the long-term and large-scale temperature behavior of a moderate-temperature
 repository in argillite host rock.
 Progress Related to Experimental and Modeling Studies of Engineered Barrier and Host
 Rock Interactions (Section 6.2)
 Several international collaboration studies made important progress in evaluating interfacial
 reactions between EBS components and host rock materials. Materials alterations in the vicinity
 of these interfaces can affect repository performance in multiple ways, for example by changing
 the flow and transport properties for radionuclides to migrate from the engineered into the
 natural barrier systems. Interfacial reactions are most prominent early in the lifetime of a
 repository when EBS components such as bentonite backfill, metal containers, and cementitious
 materials have just been emplaced and when TH perturbations remain strong.
 • Reactive transport simulations were conducted to interpret interfacial reactions and their
 impact across aged bentonite, concrete/cement, and clay rock interfaces. These activities
 were aligned with (1) the international CI-D experiment, which probes diffusive transport

December 5, 2025                                                                                           158

 across materials that were emplaced more than 15 years ago in a borehole at the Mont Terri
 URL, and (2) the reactive-transport modeling of long-term cement-bentonite interaction as
 part of the SKB EBS Task Force Task 12 benchmarking activities. These experimental and
 modeling studies provide valuable insights interfacial processes and alterations and how
 they might impact repository performance in the long term.
 • High-resolution 3-D simulations of the CI-D diffusion test at Mont Terri considered both the
 complex 3D geometry and the anisotropy of the geological formation. The high resolution of
 the model enabled the inclusion of “skin effects” developed at the interface between two
 highly reactive materials, the high-pH cement and the circumneutral but electrostatic
 Opalinus Clay. With these model improvements made, the simulation results were in good
 agreement with distinct geochemical and physical properties of the cement and the Opalinus
 Clay in the field experiment. Such analyses are crucial for establishing for long-term
 predictions of barrier performance.
 • The reactive-transport benchmarking exercise for interfacial reactions, conducted under the
 umbrella of SKB’s EBS Task Force, demonstrated good qualitative consistency in mineral
 dissolution and precipitation behavior among four international models, despite differences
 in some aspects of the model setup. Of particular importance are results for a modeling
 benchmark where advective and diffusive processes control interfacial processes between
 cement and bentonite. Modeling results suggest that a strong reduction in cement porosity
 near the interface effectively prevents any further mass exchange between the two
 engineered barrier materials. If confirmed, this “precipitation” barrier in the bentonite would
 improve the long-term safety of a repository as radionuclides cannot easily escape from the
 bentonite buffer.
 Progress Related to Repository-Induced Perturbations in Argillite Rock (Section 6.3)
 Through analysis of in-situ experiments at the Mont Terri URL, DOE scientists made significant
 scientific advances in understanding how repository-induced perturbations may affect the long-
 term performance of low-permeability argillite host rocks. One potential concern addressed is
 that gas generation in a repository could lead to pressure increases in the low-permeability
 barriers that may induce rock deformation and possibly damage. The second issue addressed is
 the potential for thermally induced fault reactivation within the host rock, which may in turn
 cause the formation of permeable transport pathways through the otherwise low-permeability
 argillite.
 • In the context of the DECOVALEX-2027 HyMAR task, LBNL scientists developed a new
 constitutive mechanical model based on a smoothed Mohr-Coulomb-Rankine (MCR)
 approach that can effectively describe the hydromechanics involved in gas migration
 through clay materials that may undergo plastic deformation. The new model was
 successfully applied to mechanical laboratory experiments combined with microscopic
 analyses of damage and dilatancy. Pending further testing against gas migration
 experiments, the new MCR model provides a solid framework for simulating the anisotropic
 HM behavior of bedded argillaceous rocks.
 • DOE scientists advanced the data and modeling analysis of the Mont Terri fault reactivation
 experiments to explore the ability of reactivated faults to self-seal over time. The integration
 of in-situ experiments and modeling revealed several key mechanisms contributing to the
 observed self-sealing phenomena following the activation of a shale fault. The fault rupture
 occurred mainly without seismic activity, and several months after activation, the fault nearly
 sealed itself to almost zero permeability, but not entirely. Time estimates for the full return of
 a fault to its initial small transmissivity project a minimum of 50 years.

December 5, 2025                                                                                               159

 • Design planning was initiated for a new thermal fault activation experiment to address the
 risk of losing natural barrier integrity due to thermal stresses, a topic becoming increasingly
 important with higher emplacement temperatures. As currently envisioned, the Fault Heating
 Experiment will be conducted in late FY26 in the same fault testbed used for isothermal
 testing, benefiting from the already installed multi-modal monitoring network.
 Progress Related to Hydromechanics, Fluid Flow, and Transport in Fractured Crystalline
 Rock (Section 6.4)
 Because crystalline formations are often fractured, repository performance assessment studies
 require an understanding of (1) the characteristics of fracture networks and how they can be
 represented with fracture network models, (2) the impact of stress changes and other
 perturbations on fracture flow and transport properties and (32) the ability to simulate large-
 scale transport patterns of radionuclides migrating in fracture networks from the repository to the
 biosphere. Four international collaboration activities have made significant progress in
 addressing these challenges.
 • The SAFENET-2 task of the DECOVALEX-2027 initiative involves collaborative modeling of
 fracture nucleation and evolution processes from the laboratory scale to the field scale. In
 the first step of this modeling task, international scientists tested their respective modeling
 solutions for hydraulic fracturing processes against known benchmark tests. DOE scientists
 demonstrated the suitability of a new phase-field framework for hydraulic fracture generation
 and propagation.
 • Participating teams in the DECOVALEX-2027 FRESCIP task used statistical analyses to
 verify predictions of fracture orientation, spacing, intensity, and transmissivity against known
 synthetic benchmarks of complex fracture networks. Emphasis is placed on quantifying
 model accuracy and supporting decision-making processes related to repository design,
 construction, and long-term performance assessment. LANL researchers support the
 FRESCIP task with the use of their powerful DFN software tool dfnWorks. The next step of
 FRESCIP will assess predictive capabilities for rock suitability and groundwater against
 actual data from the Demonstration Area at Posiva's ONKALO facility in Olkiluoto, Finland.
 • Task 10 of the SKB GWFTS Task Force focuses on validation approaches for groundwater
 flow and transport modeling involving discrete fracture features. The participating groups,
 dealing with flow, geometry, and transport at the small fracture network scale, have
 developed and applying a standardized workflow for pragmatic validation, to be further
 tested against extensive datasets form the TRUE Block Scale experiments at SKB's Äspö
 Hard Rock Laboratory.
 • The DOwnhole Robotic Stress/Strain Analyzer (DORSA) probe for long-term
 hydromechanical monitoring of faults and fractures, recently developed by DOE scientists,
 was tested in two crystalline rock testbeds made available by international collaboration with
 Japanese and Swiss programs. Enhanced with fiber optics monitoring, DORSA provided
 measurements of high relevance for repository safety assessments, as large fault
 displacements could potentially lead to shear stresses on waste canisters and fracture
 permeability increases could generate pathways for radionuclide transport.

December 5, 2025                                                                                             160

 Progress Related to Coupled Processes, Geomechanics, and Brine Migration in Salt
 Rock (Section 6.5)
 A broad portfolio of international collaboration resulted in significant advances in understanding
 complex mechanical, hydrological, thermal, and chemical processes in salt host rock. In the
 Spring of 2025, DOE’s disposal research portfolio experienced a significant realignment of R&D
 priorities and scope, with resulted in the termination all salt-related disposal research work in
 May 2025, Key accomplishments made in FY25 until the work was terminated are listed in
 below.
 • The MEASURES project is a collaboration of German, Dutch, American, and British
 researchers seeking to improve thermo-hydro-mechanical models for crushed salt to be
 used as backfill for emplacement tunnels. DOE researchers worked on a new constitutive
 model for crushed salt featuring the following improvements: (1) a generalized grain
 geometry allowing for a consistent transition from porous to intact salt, (2) an anisotropic
 formulation capable of capturing deformation history, and (3) a new physicochemical model
 as an alternative to the phenomenological approach.
 • International collaboration within the BATS and BATS2 tasks of the international
 DECOVALEX Initiative allowed DOE scientists to benefit from the engagement of
 international partners from Germany, the Netherlands, and the United Kingdom in the
 analysis and modeling of brine migration in heated salt. THM modeling conducted by the
 joint team of LBNL, SNL, and LANL researchers captured the main characteristics observed
 in the heater experiments at WIPP, including the dynamics of brine migration and damage in
 the rock salt during heating and cooling. This work helped improve the understanding of the
 complex processes expected to occur in the EDZ of a salt repository for the disposal of
 heat-generating radioactive waste and supports building the long-term repository safety
 case for radioactive waste disposal in salt.
 • Under the umbrella of the NEA Salt Club, DOE scientists and their international colleagues
 continued to develop a comprehensive FEPs database for salt host rock. They extended
 these efforts towards a generalized approach to scenario development for a high-level
 waste repository at a generic salt site.
 Progress Related to Sealing Systems for Repository Tunnels and Shafts (Section 6.6)
 Effective sealing of tunnels and shafts is important to block potential pathways of radionuclides
 between a nuclear waste repository and the biosphere. Under the umbrella of DECOVALEX-
 2027, DOE scientists joined the collaborative modeling of the Sandwich Experiment at Mont
 Terri, which tests a newly developed shaft sealing concept comprising alternating layers of
 bentonite and equipotential layers.
 • Hydro-mechanical simulations conducted for the DECOVALEX-2027 Sandwich task
 demonstrate the effectiveness of this innovative shaft sealing system, which consists of
 alternating layers of bentonite and equipotential layers. Early modeling results agree
 reasonably well with data from laboratory-scale experiments.
 Progress Related to Performance Assessment and Radionuclide Transport Modeling
       (Section 6.7)
 Recognizing the importance of building confidence in the models, methods, and software used
 for post-closure performance/safety assessments and radionuclide transport predictions, DOE
 researchers have increased their international collaboration in this area. Important activities
 were the (1) collaborative modeling of crystalline and salt reference cases under the umbrella of
 the DECOVALEX-2027 initiative, (2) the development of thermodynamic and thermochemical

December 5, 2025                                                                                            161

 databases in collaboration with international partners, (3) the joint modeling of iodine transport
 at the Cigar Lake natural analog.
 • In the Crystalline PA Task of the DECOVALEX-2027 initiative, confidence in performance
 assessment models and methods was enhanced by comparing PA modeling results from
 ten international disposal programs applied to the same reference case involving a
 repository in fractured crystalline host rock. To identify the sources and relative effects of
 uncertainties in models, participating teams developed and tested a workflow to conduct
 uncertainty quantification and sensitivity analyses (UQ/SA) on radionuclide transport in
 complex fracture networks.
 • Initial modeling results from the DECOVALEX-2027 Crystalline PA task for deterministic and
 stochastic fracture network scenarios indicate that stress-driven aperture adjustments can
 cause up to an order of magnitude variation in transport times, especially in networks with
 dominant fractures oriented perpendicular to principal stress directions. The impact of stress
 is less prominent in modeling scenarios with deterministic large fractures compared to purely
 stochastic networks. Developing reliable models for performance and safety assessments is
 a primary goal of DECOVALEX-2027.
 • Continued international collaboration of LLNL researchers with the international
 geochemical modeling community led to significant improvement in thermodynamic and
 thermochemical databases and models for radionuclide transport simulations. Interoperable
 open-source databases have been developed that leverage modern data science workflows
 and algorithms. This collaborative achievement provides a strong foundation for future work
 in geochemical modeling, with the potential to support complex scenarios in nuclear waste
 isolation, rock-water interactions, and other related applications.
 • Natural analogs are a valuable tool for bridging the scales (spatial and temporal) between
 laboratory/field tests and a deep geologic repository. In the context of DECOVALEX-2027,
 modeling teams from LBNL and SNL have been collaborating with several international
 teams in the comparative modeling of the Cigar Lake Analog. Although the mechanisms of
 129
 I migration at the Cigar Lake uranium deposit remain somewhat unclear due to data
 uncertainties, the preliminary models developed to date illustrate the complex interactions
 between rate-controlling reactions and transport processes.
 Progress Related to Next Generation Workforce Development (Section 6.8)
 Investing in the next generation of nuclear waste scientists and leaders is vital to fulfilling DOE
 NE’s mission of developing solutions for the long-term geologic disposal of radioactive waste. A
 few years ago, the Disposal R&D Campaign established a Next Generation Workforce
 Development Pilot Program to attract, train and advance a talented and diverse future workforce
 in disposal research. LBNL and SNL initiated the program and additional national laboratories
 were incorporated in later years. International collaboration has been important in this context.
 To help recruit and train a broad talent pool, the workforce development program provided
 interesting research challenges and attractive work-abroad opportunities in conjunction with
 international collaboration partners. Unfortunately, the funding for the program ended in early
 2025 when DOE’s disposal research priorities were going through a significant realignment.
 • Stimulating Student Interest in Nuclear Waste Research: Between FY22 and FY24, the Next
 Generation Workforce Development Pilot Program inspired hundreds of students via lab-
 university organized lectures and seminars to become interested in nuclear waste disposal
 science.
 • Creating the Pipeline: The summer internship program trained a large diverse student group
 of about 30 interns at LBNL and SNL. This clearly created a pipeline: Many of the summer

December 5, 2025                                                                                             162

 interns returned to their hosting national lab (or other opportunities) after their initial summer
 program ended, either for another summer program or in some cases as year-round interns.
 At LBNL, three summer interns were later hired as postdoctoral researchers.
 • Advancing the Pipeline: LBNL and SNL each hired a dedicated “next generation” postdoc for
 a three-year research project with international collaboration, which enabled them to make
 significant research contributions. For example, LBNL’s dedicated post-doc worked on THM
 modeling for the international HotBENT Project and, to better connect with the international
 community, participated in a 2023 summer training program at the Grimsel Test Site. SNL’s
 dedicated postdoc worked in GDSA Repository Safety Assessment (RSA), incorporating
 geology into UQ/SA. Unfortunately, the postdocs could not transition into career scientist
 positions at the two national laboratories, due to recent funding reductions in the Disposal
 R&D Campaign.
 To disseminate research findings and lessons learned from international collaboration, DOE
 scientists often present at international conferences and symposia. In doing so, they engage
 with the broader radioactive waste management and disposal sciences community, as well as
 with the other geoscience fields. They also play a leading role in organizing international
 conferences or conference sessions dedicated to geologic disposal research: For example, the
 First International DECOVALEX Coupled Processes Symposium, co-organized by DOE
 researchers, was held in Brugg, Switzerland, in 2019 (http://decovalex-coupled-processes-
 symposium.org/), followed by the Second International DECOVALEX Coupled Processes
 Symposium held in Troyes, France, in 2023 (https://2023-decovalex-coupled-processes-
 symposium.lbl.gov/home). A session on international collaboration in disposal research was
 organized during the “International Conference on Coupled Processes in Fractured Geological
 Media – CouFRAC” (held in Berkeley, November 14-16, 2022) and focused on recent advances
 from the nuclear waste disposal field and many other applications; a session with a similar focus
 was recently held at the TOUGH Symposium 2023, also in Berkeley from September 18-20,
 2023. Recently, from November 3-7, 2025, the 4th DECOVALEX-2027 Workshop was held in
 LBNL in Berkeley.
 DOE’s international research activities are also a frequent topic at public meetings organized by
 the U.S. Nuclear Waste Technical Review Board (NWTRB). Most prominently, the April 2019
 meeting in San Francisco was dedicated to “Recent Advances in Repository Science and
 Operations from International Underground Research Laboratory Collaborations”. The workshop
 report developed by the NWTRB provides an excellent summary of international activities and
 the critical role of underground research laboratories (NWTRB, 2020). Other recent NTWRB
 public meetings with international activities in disposal research on the agenda are the
 December 2020 meeting with a focus on generic disposal research and development program
 and priorities; the November 2021 meeting with a focus on research and development activities
 related to the geologic disposal safety assessment framework; the September 2022 meeting
 with a focus on geologic disposal in clay-bearing host rocks and R&D on clay-based engineered
 barriers; and the May 2024 meeting with a focus on geologic disposal in crystalline rocks.
 We note that the U.S. Nuclear Waste Technical Review Board (NWTRB) in FY21 released a
 report to the U.S. Congress and the Department of Energy entitled “Six Overarching
 Recommendations for How to Move the Nation’s Nuclear Waste Management Program
 Forward” (NWTRB, 2021). One of the six recommendations is to continue and expand
 engagement with the international community, and to sustain active engagement in international
 programs given the tangible benefits derived from close involvement. The Board also
 recommended continuing and expand participation in collaborative international URL activities,
 which is a key goal of DOE’s international collaboration activities in disposal research.

December 5, 2025                                                                                               163

 DOE’s disposal experts have been highly active internationally, taking on leadership roles in
 global initiatives and shaping the future of international disposal science. For instance, with Jens
 Birkholzer of LBNL serving as Chair of the DECOVALEX Initiative, DOE scientists have played
 a significant role in project task selection and have led related modeling efforts in recent years.
 Additionally, DOE researchers co-developed the planning and design of the HotBENT Project
 with international partners. Moreover, DOE scientists have held leadership positions in NEA
 initiatives such as the Salt Club, the Crystalline Club, and the Thermodynamic Database
 Project. In recent years, DOE has started moving from a mostly participatory role in ongoing in-
 situ experiments conducted by other nations to a more active role in conducting its experimental
 program in international URLs. The advantage of active planning is that the experimental focus
 and design can be better tailored to the campaign's needs. Some collaborative initiatives like
 the Mont Terri Project provide their partner organizations with the opportunity to conduct their
 experimental work and invite other partners to join. This option would allow the U.S. disposal
 program to lead targeted in-situ field activities in representative host rocks, even though there
 are currently no operating underground research laboratories in clay or crystalline host rock
 environments in the U.S.
 As mentioned earlier, DOE’s disposal research portfolio in FY25 underwent a realignment of
 R&D priorities and scope, placing a stronger focus on argillite and crystalline host rocks and
 emphasizing R&D collaborations and activities that support two main campaign priorities: (1) To
 demonstrate technical readiness and provide options for decision makers with robust safety
 assessments for disposal reference cases, and (2) To reduce program liabilities by
 demonstrating the feasibility of more cost-effective high-temperature disposal. The following
 international activities will be conducted in FY26, aligned with the major campaign priorities as
 follows:
 • Demonstrate Technical Readiness and Provide Options for Decision Makers:
 International activities in this area support the validity of and confidence in performance and
 safety assessment predictions for the evaluation and comparison of disposal solutions. The
 Disposal R&D Campaign has a strong interest in reducing unwarranted conservatism in
 performance and safety assessment modeling for future licensing purposes. The FY26
 portfolio includes three model comparison tasks in the DECOVALEX-2027 initiative
 (Crystalline PA, ANALOG, and FRESCIP), interpretative analysis and modeling of the CI-D
 cement-clay interaction test conducted at the Mont Terri URL, and cooperation with three
 relevant EURAD-2 work packages referred to as RAMPEC, ANCHORS, and DITUSC.
 • Reduce Liabilities:
 International activities in this area are focused on understanding the potential performance
 and safety implications of high-temperature disposal. DOE is considering the direct
 geological disposal of DPCs currently in dry storage (Hardin et al., 2014), which would
 benefit from much higher emplacement temperatures of up to 200°C at the canister surface.
 The FY26 portfolio includes participation in three thermal experiments at the Mont Terri URL
 (FE Heater Test, HE-E 300 Heater Test, and Thermal Fault Activation experiment) and
 continued work on the HotBENT experiment at the Grimsel Test Site.
 With these changes, the FY26 international disposal portfolio has become smaller and more
 focused than it has been in previous years. Yet, international collaboration will continue to be a
 key element within the Disposal R&D Campaign.
